Microscopic Derivation of Ginzburg–Landau Theory and the BCS Critical Temperature Shift in a Weak Homogeneous Magnetic Field
Andreas Deuchert, Christian Hainzl, Marcel Maier (born Schaub)

Abstract
Starting from the Bardeen–Cooper–Schrieffer (BCS) free energy functional, we derive the Ginzburg–Landau functional in the presence of a weak homogeneous magnetic field. We also provide an asymptotic formula for the BCS critical temperature as a function of the magnetic field. This extends the previous works [ 19 , 20 ] of Frank, Hainzl, Seiringer and Solovej to the case of external magnetic fields with non-vanishing magnetic flux through the unit cell.

1
Introduction and Main Results

1.1
Introduction

In 1950 Ginzburg and Landau (GL) introduced a phenomenological theory of superconductivity that is based on a system of nonlinear partial differential equations for a complex-valued wave function (the order parameter) and an effective magnetic field
[
27
]
. Their theory is
macroscopic
in nature and contains no reference to a
microscopic
mechanism behind the phenomenon of superconductivity. The GL equations show a rich mathematical structure, which has been investigated in great detail, see, e.g.,
[
7
,
8
,
48
,
47
,
10
,
11
,
13
,
12
]
and references therein. They also inspired interesting new concepts beyond the realm of their original application.

The first generally accepted
microscopic
theory of superconductivity was discovered seven years later by Bardeen, Cooper and Schrieffer (BCS) in
[
2
]
. In a major breakthrough they realized that a pairing mechanism between the conduction electrons (formation of Cooper pairs) causes the resistance in certain materials to drop down to absolute zero if their temperature is sufficiently low. This pairing phenomenon at low temperatures is induced by an effective attraction between the electrons mediated by phonons, that is, by the quantized lattice vibrations of the crystal formed by the ion cores. In recognition of this contribution BCS were awarded the Nobel prize in physics in 1972.

In the physics literature BCS theory is often formulated in terms of the gap equation, which, in the absence of external fields, is a nonlinear integral equation for a complex-valued function called the gap function (the order parameter of BCS theory). The name of the equation is related to the fact that its solution allows to determine the spectral gap of an effective
quadratic
Hamiltonian that is open only in the superconducting phase. BCS theory also has a variational interpretation, where the gap equation arises as the Euler–Lagrange equation of the BCS free energy functional. This free energy functional can be obtained from a full quantum mechanical description of the system by restricting attention to quasi-free states, a point of view that was emphasized by Leggett in
[
39
]
, see also
[
26
]
. In this formulation, the system is described in terms of a one-particle density matrix and a Cooper pair wave function.

Although it was originally introduced to describe the phase transition from the normal to the superconducting state in metals and alloys, BCS theory can also be applied to describe the phase transition to the superfluid state in cold fermionic gases. In this case, the usual non-local phonon-induced interaction in the gap equation needs to be replaced by a local pair potential. From a mathematical point of view, the gap equation has been studied for interaction kernels suitable to describe the physics of conduction electrons in solids in
[
45
,
3
,
51
,
53
,
43
,
54
]
. We refer to
[
30
,
18
,
33
,
34
,
24
,
4
,
21
,
15
]
for works that investigate the translation-invariant BCS functional with a local pair interaction. BCS theory in the presence of external fields has been studied in
[
35
,
5
,
22
,
14
,
6
]
.

A relation between the
macroscopic
GL theory and the
microscopic
BCS theory was established by Gor’kov in 1959
[
28
]
. He showed that, close to the critical temperature, where the order parameters of both models are expected to be small, GL theory arises from BCS theory when the free energy is expanded in powers of the gap function. The first mathematically rigorous proof of this relation was given by Frank, Hainzl, Seiringer and Solovej in 2012
[
19
]
. They showed that in the presence of weak and
macroscopic
external fields, the
macroscopic
variations of the Cooper pair wave function of the system are correctly described by GL theory if the temperature is close to the critical temperature of the sample in an appropriate sense. The precise parameter regime is as follows: The external electric field
W
and the vector potential
A
of the external magnetic field are given by
h^{2}W(hx)
and
hA(hx)
, respectively. Here
0<h\ll 1
denotes the ratio between the microscopic and the macroscopic length scale of the system. Such external fields change the energy by an amount of the order
h^{2}
and it is therefore natural to consider temperatures
T=T_{\mathrm{c}}(1-Dh^{2})
with
D>0
, where
T_{\mathrm{c}}
denotes the critical temperature of the sample in the absence of external fields. Within this setup it has been shown in
[
19
]
that the correction to the BCS free energy on the order
h^{4}
is correctly described by GL theory. Moreover, the Cooper pair wave function of the system is, to leading order in
h
, given by

\alpha(x,y)=h\,\alpha_{*}(x-y)\,\psi\left(\frac{h(x+y)}{2}\right).

(1.1)

Here,
\psi
denotes the order parameter of GL theory and
\alpha_{*}(x-y)
is related to the Cooper pair wave function in the absence of external fields.

External electric and magnetic fields may change the critical temperature of a superconductor and this shift is expected to be described by GL theory. A justification of this claim has been provided in
[
20
]
. More precisely, it has been shown that, within the setup of
[
19
]
described above, the critical temperature of the sample obeys the asymptotic expansion

T_{\mathrm{c}}(h)=T_{\mathrm{c}}(1-D_{\mathrm{c}}h^{2})+o(h^{2}),

(1.2)

where the constant
D_{\mathrm{c}}
can be computed using linearized GL theory.

One crucial assumption in
[
19
]
and
[
20
]
is that the vector potential related to the external magnetic field is periodic. In this case the magnetic flux through the unit cell equals zero. An important step towards an extension of the results in
[
20
]
to the case of a magnetic field with non-vanishing magnetic flux through the unit cell has been provided by Frank, Hainzl and Langmann in
[
17
]
. In this article the authors consider the problem of computing the BCS critical temperature shift in the presence of a weak homogeneous magnetic field within linearized BCS theory. Heuristically, this approximation is justified by the fact that linearized GL theory is sufficient to predict the critical temperature shift, see the discussion in the previous paragraph. In the physics literature this approximation appears in
[
36
,
52
,
38
,
37
]
, for instance.

The aim of the present article is to extend the results in
[
19
]
and
[
20
]
to a setting with an external magnetic field having non-zero flux through the unit cell. More precisely, we consider a large periodic sample of fermionic particles subject to a weak homogeneous magnetic field
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The temperatures
T
is chosen such that
(T_{\mathrm{c}}-T)/T_{\mathrm{c}}=D|\mathbf{B}|
with
D\in\mathbb{R}
. We show that the correction of the BCS free energy of the sample at the order
|\mathbf{B}|^{2}
is given by GL theory. Moreover, to leading order in
|\mathbf{B}|
the Cooper pair wave function of the system is given by (
1.1
) with
h
replaced by
|\mathbf{B}|^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
. We also show that the BCS critical temperature shift caused by the external magnetic field is given by (
1.2
) with
D_{\mathrm{c}}
determined by linearized GL theory. Our analysis yields the same formula that was computed within the framework of linearized BCS theory in
[
17
]
. This can be interpreted as a justification of the approximation to use linearized BCS theory to compute the BCS critical temperature shift. The main new ingredient of our proof are a priori bounds for certain low-energy states of the BCS functional that include the magnetic field.

1.2
Gauge-periodic samples

We consider a
3
-dimensional sample of fermionic particles described by BCS theory that is subject to an external magnetic field
\mathbf{B}:=Be_{3}
with strength
B>0
, pointing in the
e_{3}
-direction. We choose the magnetic vector potential
\mathbf{A}(x):=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{B}\wedge x
so that
\curl\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{B}
, where
\mathbf{B}\wedge x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
denotes the cross product of two vectors. The corresponding magnetic momentum operator
\pi:=-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}
commutes with the magnetic translations
T(v)
, defined by

\displaystyle T(v)f(x)

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot(v\wedge x)}f(x+v),

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

(1.3)

The family
\{T(v)\}_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}
obeys the relation
T(v+w)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot(v\wedge w)}\;T(v)T(w)
, that is, it is a unitary representation of the Heisenberg group. We assume that our system is periodic with respect to the Bravais lattice
\Lambda_{B}=\sqrt{2\pi B^{-1}}\,\mathbb{Z}^{3}
with fundamental cell

\displaystyle Q_{B}

\displaystyle:=\bigl[0,\sqrt{2\pi B^{-1}}\bigr]^{3}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}.

(1.4)

The magnetic flux through the unit cell
Q_{B}
equals
\mathbf{B}\cdot(b_{1}\wedge b_{2})=2\pi
, where
b_{i}=\sqrt{2\pi B^{-1}}\,e_{i}
are the basis vectors spanning
\Lambda_{B}
. This assures that the abelian subgroup
\{T(\lambda)\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{B}}
is a unitary representation of the lattice group.

1.3
The BCS functional

In BCS theory a state is described by a generalized fermionic one-particle density matrix, that is, by a self-adjoint operator
\Gamma
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
which satisfies
0\leqslant\Gamma\leqslant 1
and is of the form

\displaystyle\Gamma=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma&\alpha\\
\overline{\alpha}&1-\overline{\gamma}\end{pmatrix}.

(1.5)

Here,
\overline{\alpha}=J\alpha J
with the Riesz identification operator
J\colon L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
f\mapsto\overline{f}
, realized by complex conjugation. The condition
\Gamma=\Gamma^{*}
implies that the one-particle density matrix
\gamma
is a self-adjoint operator. It also implies that the Cooper pair wave function
\alpha(x,y)
, the kernel of
\alpha
, is symmetric under the exchange of its coordinates. The symmetry of
\alpha
is due to the fact that we exclude spin variables from our description and assume that Cooper pairs are in a spin singlet state. The condition
0\leqslant\Gamma\leqslant 1
implies
0\leqslant\gamma\leqslant 1
as well as that
\gamma
and
\alpha
are related through the operator inequality

\displaystyle\alpha\alpha^{*}\leqslant\gamma(1-\gamma).

(1.6)

A BCS state
\Gamma
is called
gauge-periodic
if
\mathbf{T}(\lambda)\,\Gamma\,\mathbf{T}(\lambda)^{*}=\Gamma
holds for every
\lambda\in\Lambda_{B}
, with the magnetic translations
\mathbf{T}(\lambda)
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
defined by

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}(v)

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}T(v)&0\\
0&\overline{T(v)}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

For
\gamma
and
\alpha
, this implies
T(\lambda)\gamma T(\lambda)^{*}=\gamma
and
T(\lambda)\,\alpha\,\overline{T(\lambda)}^{*}=\alpha
or, in terms of their kernels,

\displaystyle\gamma(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot(\lambda\wedge(x-y))}\;\gamma(x+\lambda,y+\lambda),

\displaystyle\alpha(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot(\lambda\wedge(x+y))}\;\alpha(x+\lambda,y+\lambda),

\displaystyle\lambda\in\Lambda_{B}.

(1.7)

Remark 1.1
.

Since we are interested in the situation of a constant magnetic field it seems natural to consider magnetically translation-invariant BCS states, that is, states obeying
\mathbf{T}(v)\,\Gamma\,\mathbf{T}(v)^{*}=\Gamma
for every
v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. However, in this case one obtains a trivial model because the Cooper pair wave function
\alpha
of a magnetically translation-invariant state necessarily vanishes. To see this, we note that
\alpha
satisfies
T(v)\,\alpha\,\overline{T(v)}^{*}=\alpha
for all
v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Using this and the relation
T(v+w)\,\alpha\,\overline{T(v+w)}^{*}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mathbf{B}\cdot(v\wedge w)}\,T(v)T(w)\,\alpha\,\overline{T(w)}^{*}\,\overline{T(v)}^{*}
, we conclude that
\alpha=0
.

A gauge-periodic BCS state
\Gamma
is said to be
admissible
if

\displaystyle\Tr\bigl[\gamma+(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A})^{2}\gamma\bigr]<\infty

(1.8)

holds. Here
\Tr[A]
denotes the trace per unit volume of
A
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\Tr[A]

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{|Q_{B}|}\Tr_{L^{2}(Q_{B})}[\chi A\chi],

(1.9)

with the characteristic function
\chi
of the cube
Q_{B}
in (
1.4
). By
\Tr_{L^{2}(Q_{B})}[A]
we denote the usual trace of an operator
A
on
L^{2}(Q_{B})
. The condition in (
1.8
) is meant to say that
\gamma
and
(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A})^{2}\gamma
are locally trace class, that is, they are trace class with respect to the trace in (
1.9
). Eq. (
1.8
), the same inequality with
\gamma
replaced by
\overline{\gamma}
, and the inequality in (
1.6
) imply that
\alpha
,
(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A})\alpha
, and
(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A})\overline{\alpha}
are locally Hilbert–Schmidt. In Section
2
below we will express this property in terms of
H^{1}
-regularity of the kernel of
\alpha
.

For any admissible BCS state
\Gamma
, we define the Bardeen–Cooper–Schrieffer free energy functional (in the following: BCS functional) at temperature
T\geqslant 0
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle:=\Tr\bigl[\bigl((-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A})^{2}-\mu\bigr)\gamma\bigr]-T\,S(\Gamma)-\frac{1}{|Q_{B}|}\int_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;V(r)\,|\alpha(X,r)|^{2},

(1.10)

with the von Neumann entropy per unit volume
S(\Gamma)=-\Tr[\Gamma\ln(\Gamma)]
and the chemical potential
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
. The particles interact via a two-body potential
V\in L^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})+L_{\varepsilon}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. The condition on
V
guarantees that it is relatively form bounded with respect to the Laplacian and implies that
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}
is bounded from below. Furthermore, we introduced center-of-mass and relative coordinates
X=\frac{x+y}{2}
and
r=x-y
. Here and in the following, we abuse notation slightly by writing
\alpha(X,r)\equiv\alpha(x,y)
.

Remark 1.2
.

We opt for the above set-up because the solution of the problem for the constant magnetic field already contains the main difficulties of the case of a general magnetic field. This is related to the fact that the vector potential of any magnetic field with non-zero flux through the unit cell can be written as a sum of a vector potential of a homogeneous magnetic field and a periodic vector potential, see e.g.
[
50
, Proposition 4.1]
. The latter can be treated in some sense as a perturbation, see
[
19
,
20
]
. However, this is not true for the constant magnetic field, see Remark
1.6
(a) below. To solve the general case it is therefore crucial to understand the case of a homogeneous magnetic field. To keep the presentation to a reasonable length and to be able to convey the main ideas more clearly, we therefore decided to present this case first. We plan to extend our treatment to the case of a general magnetic field in a second paper. One motivation to treat general periodic magnetic fields with non-zero flux through the unit cell stems from the fact that it is an interesting and highly relevant problem to consider magnetic fields that are chosen self-consistently.

The BCS functional is bounded from below and coercive on the set of admissible states. More precisely, it can be shown that the kinetic energy dominates the entropy and the interaction energy, i.e., there is a constant
C>0
such that for all admissible
\Gamma
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\Tr\bigl[\gamma+(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A})^{2}\gamma\bigr]-C.

(1.11)

The unique minimizer of the BCS functional among admissible states with
\alpha=0
is given by

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0}

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{0}&0\\
0&1-\overline{\gamma}_{0}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{1+\mathrm{e}^{((-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A})^{2}-\mu)/T}}.

(1.12)

Since
\Gamma_{0}
is also the unique minimizer of the BCS functional for sufficiently large temperatures
T
, it is called the normal state.
We define the BCS free energy by

\displaystyle F^{\mathrm{BCS}}(B,T):=\inf\bigl\{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{0}):\Gamma\text{ admissible}\bigr\}

(1.13)

and say that our system is superconducting if
F^{\mathrm{BCS}}(B,T)<0
, that is, if the minimal energy is strictly smaller than that of the normal state. In this work we are interested in the regime of weak magnetic fields
0<B\ll 1
. Our goal is to obtain an asymptotic expansion of
F^{\mathrm{BCS}}(B,T)
in powers of
B
that allows us to derive Ginzburg–Landau theory, and to show how the BCS critical temperature depends on the magnetic field
B
. For our main results to hold, we need the following assumptions concerning the regularity of the interaction potential
V
.

Assumption 1.3
.

We assume that the interaction potential
V
is a nonnegative, radial function such that
(1+|\cdot|^{2})V\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

Remark 1.4
.

Our main results Theorem
1
and Theorem
2
still hold if the assumption
V\geqslant 0
is dropped. We only use it in Appendix
A
when we investigate the spectral properties of a certain linear operator involving
V
. These statements still hold in the case of potentials without a definite sign but their proof is longer. A proof of these statements in the general setting can be found in
[
42
, Chapter 6]
. The property
V\geqslant 0
does not simplify the remaining part of our analysis. We expect our results to be true also if
V
has moderate local singularities. Furthermore, it may be possible to slightly weaken the decay assumptions of
V
. We choose to work with the assumptions above to keep the presentation at a reasonable length.

1.4
The translation-invariant BCS functional

If no external fields are present, i.e. if
\mathbf{B}=0
, we describe the system by translation-invariant states, that is, we assume that the kernels of
\gamma
and
\alpha
are of the form
\gamma(x-y)
and
\alpha(x-y)
. To define the trace per unit volume we choose a cube of side length
1
. The resulting translation-invariant BCS functional and its infimum minus the free energy of the normal state are denoted by
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathrm{ti},T}
and
F^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathrm{ti}}(T)
, respectively. This functional has been studied in detail in
[
30
]
, see also
[
29
]
and the references therein, where it has been shown that there is a unique critical temperature
T_{\mathrm{c}}\geqslant 0
such that
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathrm{ti},T}
has a minimizer with
\alpha\neq 0
if
T<T_{\mathrm{c}}
. For
T\geqslant T_{\mathrm{c}}
the normal state in (
1.12
) with
B=0
is the unique minimizer. In terms of the energy, we have
F^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathrm{ti}}(T)<0
for
T<T_{\mathrm{c}}
, while
F^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathrm{ti}}(T)=0
if
T\geqslant T_{\mathrm{c}}
.

It has also been shown in
[
30
]
that the critical temperature
T_{\mathrm{c}}
can be characterized via a linear criterion. More precisely, the critical temperature is determined by the unique value of
T
such that the operator

K_{T}-V

acting on
L^{2}_{\mathrm{sym}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, the space of reflection-symmetric square-integrable functions, has zero as its lowest eigenvalue. Here,
K_{T}=K_{T}(-\mathrm{i}\nabla)
with the symbol

\displaystyle K_{T}(p):=\frac{p^{2}-\mu}{\tanh\frac{p^{2}-\mu}{2T}}.

(1.14)

It should be noted that the function
T\mapsto K_{T}(p)
is strictly monotone increasing for fixed
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and that
K_{T}(p)\geqslant 2T
if
\mu\geqslant 0
and
K_{T}(p)\geqslant|\mu|/\tanh(|\mu|/(2T))
if
\mu<0
. Our assumptions on
V
guarantee that the essential spectrum of the operator
K_{T}-V
equals
[2T,\infty)
if
\mu\geqslant 0
and
[|\mu|/\tanh(|\mu|/(2T)),\infty)
if
\mu<0
. Accordingly, an eigenvalue at zero is necessarily isolated and of finite multiplicity.

The results in
[
30
]
have been obtained in the case where the Cooper pair wave function
\alpha(x)
is not necessarily an even function (as opposed to our setup), which means that
K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(-\mathrm{i}\nabla)-V
has to be understood to act on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. The results in
[
30
]
, however, equally hold if the symmetry of
\alpha
is enforced. That is, they hold in the same way if
V
is reflection symmetric and if the translation-invariant BCS functional is minimized over functions
\gamma(x)
and
\alpha(x)
that are both assumed to be reflection symmetric.

We are interested in the situation where (a)
T_{\mathrm{c}}>0
and (b) the translation-invariant BCS functional has a unique minimizer with a radial Cooper pair wave function (
s
-wave Cooper pairs) for
T
close to
T_{\mathrm{c}}
. This is implied by the following assumption. Part (b) should be compared to
[
15
, Theorem 2.8]
.

Assumption 1.5
.

(a)

We assume that
T_{\mathrm{c}}>0
. If
V\geqslant 0
and it does not vanish identically this is automatically implied, see
[
30
, Theorem 3]
. In the case of an interaction potential without a definite sign it is a separate assumption.

(b)

We assume that the lowest eigenvalue of
K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V
is simple.

In the following we denote by
\alpha_{*}
the unique ground state of the operator
K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V
, i.e.,

\displaystyle K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}\alpha_{*}=V\alpha_{*}.

(1.15)

We choose the normalization of
\alpha_{*}
such that it is real-valued and
\|\alpha_{*}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}=1
. Since
V
is a radial function and
\alpha_{*}
is the unique solution of (
1.15
) it follows that
\alpha_{*}
is radial, too.

1.5
The Ginzburg–Landau functional

We call a function
\Psi
on
Q_{B}

gauge-periodic

if it is left invariant by the magnetic translations of the form

\displaystyle T_{B}(\lambda)\Psi(X)

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mathbf{B}\cdot(\lambda\wedge X)}\;\Psi(X+\lambda),

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle\in\Lambda_{B}.

(1.16)

The operator
T(\lambda)
in (
1.3
) coincides with
T_{B}(\lambda)
when
\mathbf{B}
is replaced by
2\mathbf{B}
.

Let
\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{2},\Lambda_{3}>0
and
D\in\mathbb{R}
be given. For
B>0
and a gauge-periodic function
\Psi
, the Ginzburg–Landau functional is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D,B}(\Psi)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{B^{2}}\frac{1}{|Q_{B}|}\int_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\;\bigl\{\Lambda_{0}\;|(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+2\mathbf{A})\Psi(X)|^{2}-DB\,\Lambda_{2}\,|\Psi(X)|^{2}+\Lambda_{3}\,|\Psi(X)|^{4}\bigr\}.

(1.17)

We highlight the factor of
2
in front of the magnetic potential in (
1.17
) and that the definition of the magnetic translation in (
1.16
) differs from that in (
1.3
) by a factor
2
. These two factors reflect the fact that
\Psi
describes Cooper pairs, which carry twice the charge of a single particle.
The Ginzburg–Landau energy is defined by

\displaystyle E^{\mathrm{GL}}(D):=\inf\bigl\{\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D,B}(\Psi):\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})\bigr\},

where
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})
denotes the set of gauge-periodic functions
\Psi\in L^{2}(Q_{B})
that satisfy
(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+2\mathbf{A})\Psi\in L^{2}(Q_{B})
. It is independent of
B
by scaling. More precisely, for given
\psi
the function

\displaystyle\Psi(X)

\displaystyle:=\sqrt{B}\;\psi\bigl(\sqrt{B}\,X\bigr),

\displaystyle X\in\mathbb{R}^{3},

(1.18)

satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D,B}(\Psi)=\mathcal{E}_{D,1}^{\mathrm{GL}}(\psi).

(1.19)

We also define the critical parameter

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{c}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\Lambda_{2}}\inf\spec_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{1})}\bigl((-\mathrm{i}\nabla+e_{3}\wedge X)^{2}\bigr),

(1.20)

where
L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{1})
denotes the set of gauge-periodic square-integrable functions on
Q_{1}
. Its definition is motivated by the fact that
E^{\mathrm{GL}}(D)<0
if
D>D_{\mathrm{c}}
and
E^{\mathrm{GL}}(D)=0
if
D\leqslant D_{\mathrm{c}}
. The proof of this statement goes along the same lines as that of
[
20
, Lemma 2.5]
. In our situation with a constant magnetic field the lowest eigenvalue of the Hamiltonian in (
1.20
) equals
2
, see
[
50
, Eq. (6.2)]
, and
D_{\mathrm{c}}
is explicit. In the situation of
[
20
]
, where general external fields excluding the constant magnetic field are present, the parameter
D_{\mathrm{c}}
is not explicit.

1.6
Main results

Our first main result concerns the asymptotics of the BCS free energy in (
1.13
) in the regime
B\ll 1
. It also contains a statement about the asymptotics of the Cooper pair wave function of states
\Gamma
, whose energy
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma)
has the same asymptotic behavior as the BCS free energy (approximate minimizers). The precise statement is captured in the following theorem.

Theorem 1
.

Let Assumptions
1.3
and
1.5
hold, let
D\in\mathbb{R}
, and let the coefficients
\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{2},\Lambda_{3}>0
be given by (
3.22
)-(
3.24
) below. Then there are constants
C>0
and
B_{0}>0
such that for all
0<B\leqslant B_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle F^{\mathrm{BCS}}(B,\,T_{\mathrm{c}}(1-DB))=B^{2}\;\bigl(E^{\mathrm{GL}}(D)+R\bigr),

(1.21)

with
R
satisfying the estimate

\displaystyle CB\geqslant R\geqslant-\mathcal{R}:=-CB^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{12}}}.

(1.22)

Moreover, for any approximate minimizer
\Gamma
of
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}
at
T=T_{\mathrm{c}}(1-DB)
in the sense that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{0})\leqslant B^{2}\bigl(E^{\mathrm{GL}}(D)+\rho\bigr)

(1.23)

holds for some
\rho\geqslant 0
, we have the decomposition

\displaystyle\alpha(X,r)=\Psi(X)\,\alpha_{*}(r)+\sigma(X,r)

(1.24)

for the Cooper pair wave function
\alpha=\Gamma_{12}
. Here,
\sigma
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|Q_{B}|}\int_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;|\sigma(X,r)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant CB^{\nicefrac{{11}}{{6}}},

(1.25)

\alpha_{*}
is the normalized zero energy eigenstate of
K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V
, and the function
\Psi
obeys

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D,B}(\Psi)\leqslant E^{\mathrm{GL}}(D)+\rho+\mathcal{R}.

(1.26)

Our second main result concerns the shift of the BCS critical temperature that is caused by the external magnetic field.

Theorem 2
.

Let Assumptions
1.3
and
1.5
hold. Then there are constants
C>0
and
B_{0}>0
such that for all
0<B\leqslant B_{0}
the following holds:

(a)

Let
0<T_{0}<T_{\mathrm{c}}
. If the temperature
T
satisfies

T_{0}\leqslant T\leqslant T_{\mathrm{c}}\,(1-B\,(D_{\mathrm{c}}+C\,B^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}))

(1.27)

with
D_{\mathrm{c}}
in (
1.20
), then we have

F^{\mathrm{BCS}}(B,T)<0.

(b)

If the temperature
T
satisfies

T\geqslant T_{\mathrm{c}}\,(1-B\,(D_{\mathrm{c}}-\mathcal{R}))

(1.28)

with
D_{\mathrm{c}}
in (
1.20
) and
\mathcal{R}
in (
1.22
), then we have

\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{0})>0

unless
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0}
.

Remarks 1.6
.

(a)

Theorem
1
and Theorem
2
extend similar results in
[
19
, Theorem 1]
and
[
20
, Theorem 2.4]
to the case of a homogeneous magnetic field. Such a magnetic field has a non-periodic vector potential and a non-zero magnetic flux through the unit cell
Q_{B}
. The main reason why the problem with a homogeneous magnetic field is more complicated is that it cannot be treated as a perturbation of the Laplacian. More precisely, it was possible in
[
19
,
20
]
to work with a priori bounds for low-energy states that only involve the Laplacian and not the external fields. As noticed in
[
17
]
, see the discussion below Remark 6, this is not possible in the case of a homogeneous magnetic field. In the proof of comparable a priori estimates involving the homogeneous magnetic field, see Theorem
5.1
below, we have to deal with the fact that the components of the magnetic momentum operator do not commute, which leads to significant technical difficulties.

(b)

If we compare Theorem
1
to
[
19
, Theorem 1]
or Theorem
2
to
[
20
, Theorem 2.4]
we note the following technical differences: (1) The parameter
h
in
[
19
,
20
]
equals
B^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
in our work. (2) We use microscopic coordinates while macroscopic coordinates are used in
[
19
,
20
]
, see the discussion above Eq. 1.4 in
[
19
]
. (3) Our free energy is normalized by a large volume factor, see (
1.9
) and (
1.10
). This is not the case in
[
19
,
20
]
. Accordingly, the GL energy appears on the order
B^{2}
in our setting and on the order
h
in the setting in
[
19
]
. See also point (1) for the relation between
B
and
h
. (4) The leading order of the Cooper pair wave function in
[
19
, Theorem 1]
is of the form

\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{*}(x-y)(\Psi(x)+\Psi(y)).

(1.29)

This should be compared to (
1.24
), where relative and center-of-mass coordinates are used. Using the a priori bound for the
L^{2}
-norm of
\nabla\Psi
below (5.61) in
[
19
]
, one can see that (
1.29
) equals the first term in (
1.24
) to leading order in
h
. The analogue in our setup does not seem to be correct.

(c)

The Ginzburg–Landau energy appears at the order
B^{2}
. This should be compared to the free energy
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{0})
of the normal state, which is of order
1
.

(d)

To appreciate the bound in (
1.25
), we note that the first term in the decomposition of
\alpha
in (
1.24
) obeys

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|Q_{B}|}\int_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;|\Psi(X)\alpha_{*}(r)|^{2}

\displaystyle\sim B

if
D>0
.

(e)

We stated Theorem
1
with fixed
D\in\mathbb{R}
. Our explicit error bounds show that
D
is allowed to vary with
B
as long as there is a
B
-independent constant
D_{0}>0
such that
|D|\leqslant D_{0}
holds.

(f)

Theorem
2
gives bounds on the range of temperatures where superconductivity is present, see (
1.27
), or absent, see (
1.28
). The interpretation of this theorem is that for small magnetic fields
B
the critical temperature obeys the asymptotic expansion

T_{\mathrm{c}}(B)=T_{\mathrm{c}}(1-D_{\mathrm{c}}B)+o(B).

(1.30)

We highlight that
T_{\mathrm{c}}
is determined by the translation-invariant problem, and that
D_{\mathrm{c}}
is given by the macroscopic (linearized) GL theory. The same result has been obtained in
[
17
, Theorem 4]
in the case of linearized BCS theory. Theorem
2
can therefore be interpreted as a justification of this approximation. Eq. (
1.30
) allows us to compute the upper critical field
B_{c2}
. That is, the magnetic field, above which, for a given temperature
T
, superconductivity is absent. In particular, it allows us to compute the derivative of
B_{c2}
with respect to
T
at the critical temperature from the BCS functional. For more details we refer to
[
17
, Appendix A]
.

(g)

We expect that the assumption
0<T_{0}\leqslant T
for some arbitrary but
B
-independent constant
T_{0}
in Theorem
2
(a) is of technical nature. We need this assumption, which similarly appears in
[
17
, Theorem 4]
, because our trial state analysis in Section
3
breaks down when the temperature
T
approaches zero. This is related to the fact that the Fermi distribution function
f_{T}(x)=(\mathrm{e}^{x/T}+1)^{-1}
cannot be represented by a Cauchy-integral uniformly in the temperature. We note that there is no such restriction in Theorem
2
(b). It is also not needed in
[
20
, Theorem 2.4]
.

1.7
Organization of the paper and strategy of proof

In Section
1.8
we provide a brief non-rigorous computation that shows from which terms in the BCS functional the different terms in the Ginzburg–Landau functional arise.

In Section
2
we complete the introduction of our mathematical setup. We recall several properties of the trace per unit volume and introduce the relevant spaces of gauge-periodic functions.

Section
3
is dedicated to a trial state analysis. We start by introducing a class of Gibbs states, whose Cooper pair wave function is given by a product of the form
\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)
to leading order in
B
with
\alpha_{*}
in (
1.15
) and with a gauge-periodic function
\Psi
on
Q_{B}
. We state and motivate several results concerning these Gibbs states and their BCS free energy, whose proofs are deferred to Section
4
. Afterwards, these statements are used to prove the upper bound on (
1.21
) as well as Theorem
2
(a). As will be explained below, they are also relevant for the proofs of the lower bound in (
1.21
) and of Theorem
2
(b) in Section
6
.

Section
4
contains the proof of the results concerning the Gibbs states and their BCS free energy that have been stated without proof in Section
3
. Our analysis is based on an extension of the phase approximation method, which has been pioneered in the framework of linearized BCS theory in
[
17
]
, to our nonlinear setting. The phase approximation is a well-known tool in the physics literature, see, e.g.,
[
36
]
, and has also been used in the mathematical literature to study spectral properties of Schrödinger operators involving a magnetic field, for instance in
[
9
,
44
]
. Our approach should be compared to the trial state analysis in
[
19
,
20
]
, where a semi-classical expansion is used. The main novelty of our trial state analysis is Lemma
4.2
, where we provide an alternative way to compute a certain trace function involving the trial state. It should be compared to the related part in the proof of
[
19
, Theorem 2]
. While the analysis in
[
19
]
uses a Cauchy integral representation of the function
z\mapsto\ln(1+\mathrm{e}^{-z})
, our approach is based on a product expansion of the hyperbolic cosine in terms of Matsubara frequencies. In this way we obtain better decay properties in the subsequent resolvent expansion, which, in our opinion, simplifies the analysis considerably.

Section
5
contains the proof of a priori estimates for BCS states, whose BCS free energy is smaller than or equal to that of the normal state
\Gamma_{0}
in (
1.12
) plus a correction of the order
B^{2}
(low-energy states). The result is captured in Theorem
5.1
, which is the main novelty of the present article. It states that the Cooper pair wave function of any low-energy state in the above sense has a Cooper pair wave function, which is, to leading order in
B
, given by a product of the form
\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)
with
\alpha_{*}(r)
in (
1.15
) and with a gauge-periodic function
\Psi(X)
on
Q_{B}
. Furthermore, the function
\Psi(X)
obeys certain bounds, which show that it is slowly varying and small in an appropriate sense. As explained in Remark
1.6
(a), the main difficulty to overcome is that our a priori bounds involve the magnetic field. Therefore, we have to deal with the non-commutativity of the components of the magnetic momentum operator. The step where this problem appears most prominently is in the proof of Proposition
5.7
.

The proof of the lower bound on (
1.21
) and of Theorem
2
(b) is provided in Section
6
, which mostly follows the strategy in
[
19
, Section 6]
and
[
20
, Section 4.2]
. Two main ingredients for the analysis in this section are the trial state analysis in Section
3
and Section
4
, and the a priori bounds for low-energy states in Section
5
. From Theorem
5.1
we know that the Cooper pair wave function of any low-energy state has a product structure to leading order in
B
. The main idea of the proof of the lower bound in (
1.21
) is to construct a Gibbs state, whose Cooper pair wave function has the same asymptotics to leading order in
B
. The precise characterization of the Cooper pair wave function of the Gibbs state in Section
3
and the a priori bounds in Theorem
5.1
then allow us to bound the BCS free energy of the original state from below in terms of that of the Gibbs state. The latter has been computed with sufficient precision in Section
3
and Section
4
.

Throughout the paper,
c
and
C
denote generic positive constants that change from line to line. We allow them to depend on the various fixed quantities like
B_{0}
,
\mu
,
T_{\mathrm{c}}
,
V
,
\alpha_{*}
, etc. Further dependencies are indexed.

1.8
Heuristic computation of the terms in the Ginzburg–Landau functional

In this section we present a brief non-rigorous computation with the trial state that we use in the proof of the upper bound for the BCS free energy in Section
3
, to show from which terms in the BCS functional the different terms in the Ginzburg–Landau (GL) functional arise. The trial state (a Gibbs state) is defined by

\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{\Delta}&\alpha_{\Delta}\\
\overline{\alpha_{\Delta}}&1-\overline{\gamma_{\Delta}}\end{pmatrix}=\Gamma_{\Delta}=\frac{1}{1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta H_{\Delta}}}

(1.31)

with the Hamiltonian

H_{\Delta}=\begin{pmatrix}(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A})^{2}-\mu&\Delta\\
\overline{\Delta}&-\overline{(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A})^{2}}+\mu\end{pmatrix},

where the operator
\Delta
is defined via its integral kernel

\Delta(x,y)=-2\,V\alpha_{*}(x-y)\,\Psi_{B}\left(\frac{x+y}{2}\right)

and
\beta^{-1}=T=T_{\mathrm{c}}(1-DB)
. We choose
\Psi_{B}(X)
to be a minimizer of the Ginzburg–Landau functional. From (
1.18
) it follows that
|Q_{B}|^{-1}\int_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\ |\Psi(X)|^{2}\sim B
as well as that

\Tr[\Delta^{*}\Delta]=\frac{4}{|Q_{B}|}\int_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\Psi(X)|^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;|V(r)\alpha_{*}(r)|^{2}\sim B.

(1.32)

Here
r=x-y
and
X=(x+y)/2
denote relative and center-of-mass coordinates. The operator
\Delta
in (
1.31
) is therefore a small perturbation of the state
\Gamma_{\Delta}
in (
1.31
) if
0<B\ll 1
.

The BCS free energy of
\Gamma_{\Delta}
reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Tr\left[H_{0}(\Gamma_{\Delta}-\Gamma_{0})\right]-TS(\Gamma)+TS(\Gamma_{0})

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt-\frac{1}{|Q_{B}|}\int_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;V(r)|\alpha_{\Delta}(X,r)|^{2}.

Using the identity

\Tr\left[H_{0}(\Gamma_{\Delta}-\Gamma_{0})\right]=\Tr\left[H_{\Delta}\Gamma_{\Delta}-H_{0}\Gamma_{0}\right]-\Tr[(H_{\Delta}-H_{0})\Gamma_{\Delta}],

and
[
19
, Eqs. (4.3–4.5)]
, this formula can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{0})=-\frac{1}{2\beta}\Tr_{0}\bigl[\ln\bigl(1+\exp\bigr(-\beta H_{\Delta}\bigr)\bigr)-\ln\bigl(1+\exp\bigl(-\beta H_{0}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle+\frac{\langle\alpha_{*},V\alpha_{*}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}{|Q_{B}|}\int_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\ |\Psi(X)|^{2}\;-\frac{1}{|Q_{B}|}\int_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;V(r)\,\bigl|\alpha(X,r)-\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)\bigr|^{2}.

(1.33)

Here
\Tr_{0}[A]=\Tr[PAP]+\Tr[QAQ]
with

P=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}

and
Q=1-P
.

To obtain the terms in the GL functional, we need to expand the terms in (
1.33
) in powers of
B
. To that end, we first expand them up to fourth order in powers of
\Delta
(The Ginzburg–Landau functional is a fourth order polynomial in
\Psi_{B}
.) and afterwards expand the resulting terms in powers of
B
, keeping in mind that
T=T_{\mathrm{c}}(1-DB)
. It turns out that the last term on the right side of (
1.33
) is of the order
o(B^{2})
, and therefore does not contribute to the GL energy. In our trial state analysis in Sections
3
and
4
we show that there exists a linear operator
L_{T,B}
and a cubic map
N_{T,B}(\Delta)
such that

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\beta}\Tr_{0}\bigl[\ln\bigl(1+\exp\bigr(-\beta H_{\Delta}\bigr)\bigr)-\ln\bigl(1+\exp\bigl(-\beta H_{0}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle\hskip-120.0pt=-\frac{1}{4}\langle\Delta,L_{T,B}\Delta\rangle+\frac{1}{8}\langle\Delta,N_{T,B}(\Delta)\rangle+o(B^{2}).

(1.34)

In combination with the first term in the second line of (
1.33
), the quadratic terms in (
1.34
) give the quadratic terms in the Ginzburg–Landau functional:

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\langle\Delta,L_{T,B}\Delta\rangle+\frac{\langle\alpha_{*},V\alpha_{*}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}{|Q_{B}|}\int_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\Psi(X)|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt=\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{|Q_{B}|}\int_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\Pi\Psi(X)|^{2}-\frac{DB\Lambda_{2}}{|Q_{B}|}\int_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\Pi\Psi(X)|^{2}+o(B^{2}).

From the quartic term in (
1.34
) we will extract the quartic term in the GL functional, that is,

\frac{1}{8}\langle\Delta,N_{T,B}(\Delta)\rangle=\frac{\Lambda_{3}}{|Q_{B}|}\int_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\Psi(X)|^{4}+o(B^{2}),

and hence

\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{0})=B^{2}\left(E^{\mathrm{GL}}(D)+o(1)\right).

In the last step we used
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D,B}(\Psi_{B})=B^{2}E^{\mathrm{GL}}(D)
.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Schatten classes

In our proofs we frequently use Schatten norms of periodic operators, which are defined with respect to the trace per unit volume in (
1.9
). In this section we recall some basic facts about these norms.

A gauge-periodic operator
A
belongs to the
p
{}^{\textrm{th}}
local von-Neumann–Schatten class
\mathcal{S}^{p}
with
1\leqslant p<\infty
if it has finite
p
-norm, that is, if
\|A\|_{p}^{p}:=\Tr(|A|^{p})<\infty
. By
\mathcal{S}^{\infty}
we denote the set of bounded gauge-periodic operators and
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
is the usual operator norm. For the above norms the triangle inequality

\displaystyle\|A+B\|_{p}\leqslant\|A\|_{p}+\|B\|_{p}

holds for
1\leqslant p\leqslant\infty
. Moreover, for
1\leqslant p,q,r\leqslant\infty
with
\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}
we have the general Hölder inequality

\displaystyle\|AB\|_{r}\leqslant\|A\|_{p}\|B\|_{q}.

(2.1)

It is important to note that the above norms are not monotone decreasing in the index
p
. This should be compared to the usual Schatten norms, where such a property holds. The familiar inequality

\displaystyle|\Tr A|\leqslant\|A\|_{1}

is true also in the case of local Schatten norms.

The above inequalities can be reduced to the case of the usual Schatten norms, see, e.g.,
[
49
]
, using the magnetic Bloch–Floquet decomposition. We refer to
[
46
, Section XIII.16]
for an introduction to the Bloch–Floquet transformation and to
[
25
]
for a particular treatment of the magnetic case. More specifically, for a gauge-periodic operator
A
we use the unitary equivalence

\displaystyle A\cong\int^{\oplus}_{[0,\sqrt{2\pi B}]^{3}}\mathrm{d}k\;A_{k}

to write the trace per unit volume as

\Tr A=\int_{[0,\sqrt{2\pi B}]^{3}}\mathrm{d}k\;\Tr_{L^{2}(Q_{B})}A_{k},

(2.2)

where
\Tr_{L^{2}(Q_{B})}
denotes the usual trace over
L^{2}(Q_{B})
. The inequalities for the trace per unit volume from above follow from the usual ones when we use that
(AB)_{k}=A_{k}B_{k}
holds for two gauge-periodic operators
A
and
B
.

2.2
Gauge-periodic Sobolev spaces

In this section we introduce several spaces of gauge-periodic functions, which will be used to describe the center-of-mass part of Cooper pair wave functions.

For
1\leqslant p<\infty
, the space
L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{p}(Q_{B})
consists of all
L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-functions
\Psi
, which satisfy
T_{B}(\lambda)\Psi=\Psi
for all
\lambda\in\Lambda_{B}
with
T_{B}(\lambda)
in (
1.16
). The space is equipped with the usual
p
-norm per unit volume

\displaystyle\|\Psi\|_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{p}(Q_{B})}^{p}

\displaystyle:=\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\Psi(X)|^{p}:=\frac{1}{|Q_{B}|}\int_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\Psi(X)|^{p},

(2.3)

and we use the conventional abbreviation
\|\Psi\|_{p}
when this does not lead to confusion.

For
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the corresponding gauge-periodic Sobolev space is defined by

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{m}(Q_{B})

\displaystyle:=\bigl\{\Psi\in L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B}):\Pi^{\nu}\Psi\in L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})\quad\forall\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3},|\nu|_{1}\leqslant m\bigr\},

(2.4)

where

\displaystyle\Pi:=-\mathrm{i}\nabla+2\mathbf{A}

denotes the magnetic momentum operator and
|\nu|_{1}:=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\nu_{i}
for
\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}
.
Equipped with the scalar product

\displaystyle\langle\Phi,\Psi\rangle_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{m}(Q_{B})}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{|\nu|_{1}\leqslant m}B^{-1-|\nu|_{1}}\;\langle\Pi^{\nu}\Phi,\Pi^{\nu}\Psi\rangle_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})},

(2.5)

it is a Hilbert space. We note that
\Pi^{\nu}\Psi
is a gauge-periodic function if
\Psi
is gauge-periodic because
\Pi
commutes with the magnetic translations
T_{B}(\lambda)
in (
1.16
). We also note that
\Pi
is a self-adjoint operator on
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})
.

At this point, we shall briefly explain the scaling behavior in
B
of the norms introduced in (
2.3
) and (
2.5
) in terms of the Ginzburg–Landau scaling in (
1.18
). First, we note that if
\psi\in L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{p}(Q_{1})
and
\Psi
is as in (
1.18
), then

\displaystyle\|\Psi\|_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{p}(Q_{B})}=B^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,\|\psi\|_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{p}(Q_{1})}

(2.6)

for every
1\leqslant p\leqslant\infty
. In contrast, the scaling of the norm in (
2.5
) is chosen such that

\displaystyle\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{m}(Q_{B})}=\|\psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{m}(Q_{1})}.

This follows from (
2.6
) and the fact that

\|\Pi^{\nu}\Psi\|_{2}^{2}
scales as
B^{1+|\nu|_{1}}
for
\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}
.

We also mention the following magnetic Sobolev inequality because it will be used frequently in the course of the paper. For any
B>0
and any
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})
, we have

\displaystyle\|\Psi\|_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{6}(Q_{B})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,B^{-1}\,\|\Pi\Psi\|_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})}^{2}.

(2.7)

Proof of (
2.7
).

Since
Q_{1}
satisfies the cone property,
[
40
, Theorem 8.8]
implies

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{6}(Q_{1})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,\bigl(\|\psi\|_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{1})}^{2}+\|\nabla|\psi|\,\|_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{1})}^{2}\bigr).

From
[
50
, Eq. (6.2)]
we know that the bottom of the spectrum of
(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+e_{3}\wedge X)^{2}
equals
2
. For the first term on the right side, this implies
2\|\psi\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\|(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+e_{3}\wedge X)\psi\|_{2}^{2}
. To bound the second term, we apply the diamagnetic inequality
|\nabla|\psi(X)||\leqslant|(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+e_{3}\wedge X)\psi(X)|
, see
[
40
, Theorem 7.21]
. This proves (
2.7
) for
B=1
and the scaling in (
1.18
) yields (
2.7
) for
B>0
.
∎

As indicated below (
1.9
), the Cooper pair wave function
\alpha
related to an admissible state
\Gamma
belongs to
\mathcal{S}^{2}
, the Hilbert–Schmidt class introduced in Section
2.1
. In terms of the center-of-mass and relative coordinates, the gauge-periodicity and the symmetry of the kernel of
\alpha
in (
1.7
) read

\displaystyle\alpha(X,r)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mathbf{B}\cdot(\lambda\wedge X)}\;\alpha(X+\lambda,r),\quad\lambda\in\Lambda_{B};

\displaystyle\alpha(X,r)

\displaystyle=\alpha(X,-r).

(2.8)

That is,
\alpha(X,r)
is a gauge-periodic function of the center-of-mass coordinate
X
and a reflection-symmetric function of the relative coordinate
r\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We make use of the isometric identification of
\mathcal{S}^{2}
with the space

\displaystyle L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3}):=L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})\otimes L_{\mathrm{sym}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),

the square-integrable functions obeying (
2.8
), for which the norm

\displaystyle\|\alpha\|_{{L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}}^{2}:=\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;|\alpha(X,r)|^{2}=\frac{1}{|Q_{B}|}\int_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;|\alpha(X,r)|^{2}

is finite. By (
2.8
), the identity
\|\alpha\|_{2}=\|\alpha\|_{{L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}}
holds. Therefore, we do not distinguish between the scalar products
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
on
{L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
and
\mathcal{S}^{2}
and identify operators in
\mathcal{S}^{2}
with their kernels whenever this appears convenient.

Finally, the Sobolev space
H^{1}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})
consists of all functions
\alpha\in L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})
with finite
H^{1}
-norm given by

\displaystyle\|\alpha\|_{H^{1}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}^{2}

\displaystyle:=\|\alpha\|_{2}^{2}+\|\Pi_{X}\alpha\|_{2}^{2}+\|\widetilde{\pi}_{r}\alpha\|_{2}^{2}.

(2.9)

Here, we used the magnetic momentum operators

\displaystyle\Pi_{X}

\displaystyle:=-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X}+2\mathbf{A}(X),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}_{r}

\displaystyle:=-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{r}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{A}(r),

(2.10)

where
\mathbf{A}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{B}\wedge x
. We note that the norm in (
2.9
) is equivalent to the norm given by
\Tr[\alpha\alpha^{*}]+\Tr[(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A})\alpha\alpha^{*}(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A})]+\Tr[(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A})\alpha^{*}\alpha(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A})]
, which, in turn, is given by

\|\alpha\|_{2}^{2}+\|(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A})\alpha\|_{2}^{2}+\|\alpha(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A})\|_{2}^{2}
. See also the discussion below (
1.9
).

3
Trial States and their BCS Energy

The goal of this section is to provide the upper bound on (
1.21
) and the proof of Theorem
2
(a). Both bounds are proved with a trial state argument using Gibbs states
\Gamma_{\Delta}
that are defined via a gap function
\Delta
in the effective Hamiltonian. In Proposition
3.2
we show that the Cooper pair wave function
\alpha_{\Delta}
of
\Gamma_{\Delta}
is a product function with respect to relative and center-of-mass coordinates to leading order provided
\Delta
is a product function that is small in a suitable sense. A representation formula for the BCS energy in terms of the energy of these states is provided in Proposition
3.4
. Finally, in Theorem
3.5
, we show that certain parts of the BCS energy of the trial states
\Gamma_{\Delta}
equal the terms in the Ginzburg–Landau functional in (
1.17
) with sufficient precision provided
T=T_{\mathrm{c}}(1-DB)
for some fixed
D\in\mathbb{R}
. These results, whose proofs are deferred to Section
4
, are combined in Section
3.3
to give the proof of the results mentioned in the beginning of this paragraph.

3.1
The Gibbs states
\Gamma_{\Delta}

For any
\Psi\in L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
, let us introduce the gap function
\Delta\in L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})
, given by

\displaystyle\Delta(X,r):=\Delta_{\Psi}(X,r)

\displaystyle:=-2\;V\alpha_{*}(r)\;\Psi(X).

(3.1)

In our trial state analysis,
\Psi
is going to be a minimizer of the Ginzburg–Landau functional in (
1.17
). It therefore obeys the scaling in (
1.18
), which implies that the local Hilbert-Schmidt norm
\|\Delta\|_{2}^{2}
is of the order
B
. We highlight that the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-norm of
V\alpha_{*}
is of the order
1
, that is, the size of
\|\Delta\|_{2}^{2}
is determined by
\Psi
. In the proof of the lower bound we have less information on
\Psi
. The related difficulties are discussed in Remark
3.3
below. With

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}_{B}

\displaystyle:=(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A})^{2}-\mu,

(3.2)

we define the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H_{\Delta}

\displaystyle:=H_{0}+\delta:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{h}_{B}&0\\
0&-\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{B}}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&\Delta\\
\overline{\Delta}&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{h}_{B}&\Delta\\
\overline{\Delta}&-\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{B}}\end{pmatrix}

(3.3)

and the corresponding Gibbs state at inverse temperature
\beta=T^{-1}>0
as

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{\Delta}&\alpha_{\Delta}\\
\overline{\alpha_{\Delta}}&1-\overline{\gamma_{\Delta}}\end{pmatrix}=\Gamma_{\Delta}:=\frac{1}{1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta H_{\Delta}}}.

(3.4)

We note that the normal state
\Gamma_{0}
in (
1.12
) corresponds to setting
\Delta=0
in (
3.4
).

Lemma 3.1

(Admissibility of
\Gamma_{\Delta}
)
.

Let Assumptions
1.3
and
1.5
hold. Then, for any
B>0
, any
T>0
, and any
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})
, the state
\Gamma_{\Delta}
in (
3.4
) is admissible, where
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
as in (
3.1
).

The states
\Gamma_{\Delta}
are inspired by the following observation. Via variational arguments it is straightforward to see that any minimizer of
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}
in (
1.10
) solves the nonlinear Bogolubov–de Gennes equation

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta\,\mathbb{H}_{V\alpha}}},

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{V\alpha}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{h}_{B}&-2\,V\alpha\\
-2\,\overline{V\alpha}&-\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{B}}\end{pmatrix}.

(3.5)

Here,
V\alpha
is the operator given by the kernel
V(r)\alpha(X,r)
. As we look for approximate minimizers of
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}
, we choose
\Gamma_{\Delta}
in order to approximately solve (
3.5
). As far as the leading term of
\alpha_{\Delta}
is concerned this is indeed the case, as the following result shows. It should be compared to (
1.24
).

Proposition 3.2

(Structure of
\alpha_{\Delta}
)
.

Let Assumption
1.3
and
1.5
(a) be satisfied and let
T_{0}>0
be given. Then, there is a constant
B_{0}>0
such that for any
0<B\leqslant B_{0}
, any
T\geqslant T_{0}
, and any
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
the function
\alpha_{\Delta}
in (
3.4
) with
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
as in (
3.1
) has the decomposition

\displaystyle\alpha_{\Delta}(X,r)

\displaystyle=\Psi(X)\alpha_{*}(r)-\eta_{0}(\Delta)(X,r)-\eta_{\perp}(\Delta)(X,r).

(3.6)

The remainder functions
\eta_{0}(\Delta)
and
\eta_{\perp}(\Delta)
have the following properties:

(a)

The function
\eta_{0}
satisfies the bound

\displaystyle\|\eta_{0}\|_{H^{1}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;\bigl(B^{3}+B\,|T-T_{\mathrm{c}}|^{2}\bigr)\;\bigl(\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{6}+\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}\bigr).

(3.7)

(b)

The function
\eta_{\perp}
satisfies the bound

\displaystyle\|\eta_{\perp}\|_{{H^{1}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}}^{2}+\||r|\eta_{\perp}\|_{{L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;B^{3}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})}^{2}.

(3.8)

(c)

The function
\eta_{\perp}
has the explicit form

\displaystyle\eta_{\perp}(X,r)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}s\;k_{T}(Z,r-s)\,V\alpha_{*}(s)\,\bigl[\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{X})-1\bigr]\Psi(X)

with
k_{T}(Z,r)
defined in Section
4
below (
4.71
). For any radial
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
the operator

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s\;f(r)\,k_{T}(Z,r-s)\,g(s)\,\bigl[\cos(Z\cdot\Pi)-1\bigr]

commutes with
\Pi^{2}
, and, in particular,
if
P
and
Q
are two spectral projections of
\Pi^{2}
with
PQ=0
, then
\eta_{\perp}
satisfies the orthogonality property

\displaystyle\bigl\langle f(r)\,(P\Psi)(X),\,\eta_{\perp}(\Delta_{Q\Psi})\bigr\rangle=0.

(3.9)

Remark 3.3
.

The statement of Proposition
3.2
should be read in two different ways, depending on whether we are interested in proving the upper or the lower bound for the BCS free energy. When we prove the upper bound using trial states
\Gamma_{\Delta}
, the bound on
\||r|\eta_{\perp}\|_{{L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}}
in part (b) and part (c) are irrelevant. In this case the gap function
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
is defined with a minimizer
\Psi
of the GL functional, whose
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
-norm is uniformly bounded, and all remainder terms can be estimated using (
3.7
) and (
3.8
).

In the proof of the lower bound for the BCS free energy in Section
6
we are forced to work with a trial state
\Gamma_{\Delta}
, whose gap function is defined via a function
\Psi
that is related to a low-energy state of the BCS functional, see Theorem
5.1
below. For such functions we only have a bound on the
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})
-norm at our disposal. To obtain a function in
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
, we introduce a regularized version of
\Psi
as in
[
19
, Section 6]
,
[
20
, Section 6]
, and
[
17
, Section 7]
by
\Psi_{\leqslant}:=\mathbbs 1_{[0,\varepsilon]}(\Pi^{2})\Psi
for some
B\ll\varepsilon\ll 1
, see Corollary
5.2
. The
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
-norm of
\Psi_{\leqslant}
is not uniformly bounded in
B
, see (
5.5
) below. This causes a certain error term, namely the left side of (
6.12
) below, to be large, a priori.

To overcome this problem we use the bound on
\||r|\eta_{\perp}\|_{{L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}}
in part (b) of Proposition
5.1
and part (c). Part (c) exploits the fact that the first term on the right side of (
6.12
) has an explicit form that satisfies the orthogonality property in (
3.9
), which implies that the left side of (
6.12
) is indeed small. This is the reason why we need to distinguish between
\eta_{0}
and
\eta_{\perp}
.

3.2
The BCS energy of the states
\Gamma_{\Delta}

This section pertains to the BCS energy of the states
\Gamma_{\Delta}
, which is given by the Ginzburg–Landau functional to leading order. We will see in Section
4.3
that the BCS energy of
\Gamma_{\Delta}
can be calculated in terms of

\displaystyle\Tr_{0}\Bigl[\ln\bigl(\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}\bigr)\bigr)-\ln\bigl(\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{0}\bigr)\bigr)\Bigr].

(3.10)

Here,
\Tr_{0}
is a weaker form of trace which will be introduced later in (
4.8
). The operator inside the trace is closely related to the relative entropy of
H_{\Delta}
and
H_{0}
but also incorporates the interaction energy of
\alpha_{\Delta}
. We refer to (
4.9
) for more details. In the following, we explain how the terms of the Ginzburg–Landau functional, which appear in the energy expansion in (
1.21
), are obtained from the operator in (
3.10
).

As pointed out in Remark
1.6
, we should think of
\Delta
as being small. In order to expand the term in (
3.10
) in powers of
\Delta
, we use the fundamental theorem to formally write (
3.10
) as

\displaystyle\frac{\beta}{2}\Tr_{0}\Bigl[\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\tanh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{t\Delta}\bigr)\Bigl(\begin{matrix}0&\Delta\\
\overline{\Delta}&0\end{matrix}\Bigr)\Bigr].

(3.11)

This identity is not rigorous because it ignores the subtlety that
H_{t\Delta}
,
t\in[0,1]
, are unbounded operators which do not commute for distinct values of
t
.
We present a rigorous version of (
3.11
) in Lemma
4.2
below. For the sake of the following discussion it is legitimate to assume that equality between (
3.10
) and (
3.11
) holds.

We use the Mittag-Leffler series expansion, see e.g.
[
17
, Eq. (7)]
, to write the hyperbolic tangent in (
3.11
) as

\displaystyle\tanh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}z\bigr)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-z}

(3.12)

with the Matsubara frequencies

\displaystyle\omega_{n}

\displaystyle:=\pi(2n+1)T,\qquad n\in\mathbb{Z}.

(3.13)

The convergence of (
3.12
) becomes manifest by combining the
+n
and
-n
terms. Thus,

\displaystyle\tanh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}\bigr)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{\Delta}}.

(3.14)

We use this representation to expand the operator in (
3.11
) in powers of
\Delta
using the resolvent equation. The first term obtained in this way is
\langle\Delta,L_{T,B}\Delta\rangle
with the linear operator
L_{T,B}\colon{L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}\rightarrow{L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
, given by

\displaystyle L_{T,B}\Delta

\displaystyle:=-\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{B})^{-1}\,\Delta\,(\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{B}})^{-1}.

(3.15)

In the temperature regime we are interested in, we will obtain the quadratic terms in the Ginzburg–Landau functional from
\langle\Delta,L_{T,B}\Delta\rangle
.

The next term in the expansion of (
3.14
) is the quartic term
\langle\Delta,N_{T,B}(\Delta)\rangle
with the nonlinear map
N_{T,B}\colon{H^{1}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}\rightarrow{L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
defined as

\displaystyle N_{T,B}(\Delta)

\displaystyle:=\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{B})^{-1}\,\Delta\,(\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{B}})^{-1}\,\overline{\Delta}\,(\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{B})^{-1}\,\Delta\,(\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{B}})^{-1}.

(3.16)

The expression
\langle\Delta,N_{T,B}(\Delta)\rangle
will determine the quartic term in the Ginzburg–Landau functional. All higher order terms in the expansion of (
3.11
) in
\Delta
will be summarized in a trace-class operator called
\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)
, whose local trace norm is small.

With the operators
L_{T,B}
and
N_{T,B}
at hand, we are in position to state a representation formula for the BCS functional. It serves as the fundamental equation, on which the proofs of Theorems
1
and
2
are based. In particular, it will be applied in the proofs of upper and lower bounds, and we therefore formulate the statement for a general state
\Gamma
and not only for Gibbs states.

Proposition 3.4

(Representation formula for the BCS functional)
.

Let
\Gamma
be an admissible state. For any
B>0
, let
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})
and let
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
be as in (
3.1
). For
T>0
and if
V\alpha_{*}\in L^{\nicefrac{{6}}{{5}}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, there is an operator
\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)\in\mathcal{S}^{1}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{0})

\displaystyle\hskip-70.0pt=-\frac{1}{4}\langle\Delta,L_{T,B}\Delta\rangle+\frac{1}{8}\langle\Delta,N_{T,B}(\Delta)\rangle+\|\Psi\|_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})}^{2}\;\langle\alpha_{*},V\alpha_{*}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\hskip-40.0pt+\Tr\bigl[\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)\bigr]

\displaystyle\hskip-40.0pt+\frac{T}{2}\mathcal{H}_{0}(\Gamma,\Gamma_{\Delta})-\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;V(r)\,\bigl|\alpha(X,r)-\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)\bigr|^{2},

(3.17)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{0}(\Gamma,\Gamma_{\Delta}):=\Tr_{0}\bigl[\Gamma(\ln\Gamma-\ln\Gamma_{\Delta})+(1-\Gamma)(\ln(1-\Gamma)-\ln(1-\Gamma_{\Delta}))\bigr]

(3.18)

denotes the relative entropy of
\Gamma
with respect to
\Gamma_{\Delta}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)
obeys the estimate

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)\|_{1}\leqslant C\;T^{-5}\;B^{3}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{6}.

The relative entropy defined in (
3.18
) is based on the weaker form of trace
\Tr_{0}
, whose introduction we postpone until (
4.8
).

The right side of (
3.17
) should be read as follows. The first line yields the Ginzburg–Landau functional, see Theorem
3.5
below.
The second and third line consist of remainder terms. The second line is small in absolute value whereas the techniques used to bound the third line differ for upper and lower bounds. This is responsible for the different qualities of the upper and lower bounds in Theorems
1
and
2
, see (
1.22
). For an upper bound, when choosing
\Gamma:=\Gamma_{\Delta}
as a trial state, the relative entropy term
\mathcal{H}_{0}(\Gamma_{\Delta},\Gamma_{\Delta})=0
drops out. The last term in (
3.17
) is nonpositive by our assumptions on
V
and can be dropped for an upper bound. In case of an interaction potential without a sign it can easily be estimated using Proposition
3.2
. In case of the proof of the lower bound for the BCS free energy, we need to estimate the third line in (
3.17
) from below using a relative entropy estimate that we provide in Section
6
.

It remains to show that the first line of the right side of (
3.17
) is indeed given by the Ginzburg–Landau functional.
In order to state the result, we need the function

\displaystyle\widehat{V\alpha_{*}}(p):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}x\;\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}\,V(x)\alpha_{*}(x),

(3.19)

which fixes our convention on the Fourier transform in this paper.

Theorem 3.5

(Calculation of the GL energy)
.

Let Assumptions
1.3
and
1.5
(a) hold and let
D\in\mathbb{R}
be given. Then, there is a constant
B_{0}>0
such that for any
0<B\leqslant B_{0}
, any
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
,
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
as in (
3.1
), and
T=T_{\mathrm{c}}(1-DB)
, we have

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\langle\Delta,L_{T,B}\Delta\rangle+\frac{1}{8}\langle\Delta,N_{T,B}(\Delta)\rangle+\|\Psi\|_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})}^{2}\;\langle\alpha_{*},V\alpha_{*}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\hskip-40.0pt=B^{2}\;\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D,B}(\Psi)+R(B).

(3.20)

Here,

\displaystyle|R(B)|\leqslant C\,B^{3}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})}^{2}\;\bigl[1+\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}\bigr]

and with the functions

\displaystyle g_{1}(x)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\tanh(x/2)}{x^{2}}-\frac{1}{2x}\frac{1}{\cosh^{2}(x/2)},

\displaystyle g_{2}(x)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2x}\frac{\tanh(x/2)}{\cosh^{2}(x/2)},

(3.21)

the coefficients
\Lambda_{0}
,
\Lambda_{2}
, and
\Lambda_{3}
in
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D,B}
are given by

\displaystyle\Lambda_{0}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\beta_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{16}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;|(-2)\widehat{V\alpha_{*}}(p)|^{2}\;\bigl(g_{1}(\beta_{\mathrm{c}}(p^{2}-\mu))+\frac{2}{3}\beta_{\mathrm{c}}\,p^{2}\,g_{2}(\beta_{\mathrm{c}}(p^{2}-\mu))\bigr),

(3.22)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{2}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\beta_{\mathrm{c}}}{8}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;\frac{|(-2)\widehat{V\alpha_{*}}(p)|^{2}}{\cosh^{2}(\frac{\beta_{\mathrm{c}}}{2}(p^{2}-\mu))},

(3.23)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{3}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\beta_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{16}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;|(-2)\widehat{V\alpha_{*}}(p)|^{4}\;\frac{g_{1}(\beta_{\mathrm{c}}(p^{2}-\mu))}{p^{2}-\mu}.

(3.24)

Let us comment on the positivity of the coefficients (
3.22
)-(
3.24
). First,
\Lambda_{2}
is trivially positive.
Since
g_{1}(x)/x>0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, the coefficient
\Lambda_{3}
is positive as well. It cannot be immediately seen that
\Lambda_{0}
is positive, however. In order to prove this, we introduce the positive function

\displaystyle g_{3}(x)

\displaystyle:=\frac{2}{x^{2}}\frac{1}{\cosh^{2}(x/2)}-\frac{1}{x}\frac{1}{\tanh(x/2)}\frac{1}{\cosh^{2}(x/2)}

and compute

\displaystyle 2\Re\langle\alpha_{*},x_{i}(K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V)x_{i}\alpha_{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-3}\langle\widehat{V\alpha_{*}},K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(p)^{-1}[-\mathrm{i}\partial_{p_{i}},[K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(p),-\mathrm{i}\partial_{p_{i}}]]K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(p)^{-1}\widehat{V\alpha_{*}}\rangle

\displaystyle=8\,\Lambda_{0}-2\beta_{\mathrm{c}}^{3}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;|\widehat{V\alpha_{*}}(p)|^{2}\;p_{i}^{2}\,g_{3}(\beta_{\mathrm{c}}(p^{2}-\mu)).

(3.25)

Since the left side is nonnegative, this proves that
\Lambda_{0}>0
. The idea for this proof is borrowed from
[
19
, Eq. (1.22)]
.

Let us comment on the connection between (
3.20
) and
[
17
]
. The two-particle Birman–Schwinger operator
1-V^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}L_{T,B}V^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
has been intensively studied in
[
17
]

to identify temperature regimes, where the bottom of its spectrum is positive or negative. This operator also appears in (
3.20
) because

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\langle\Delta,L_{T,B}\Delta\rangle+\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\,\langle\alpha_{*},V\alpha_{*}\rangle=\bigl\langle V^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\alpha_{*}\Psi,\bigl(1-V^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}L_{T,B}V^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\bigr)V^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\alpha_{*}\Psi\bigr\rangle.

(3.26)

That is, the question whether the bottom of the spectrum of
1-V^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}L_{T,B}V^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
is positive or negative is intimately related to the sign of (
3.20
), and thus of (
3.17
) and (
1.13
). Accordingly, it is related to the question whether the systems displays superconductivity or not. We highlight that the operator on the right side of (
3.26
) acts on functions in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{6})
in
[
17
]
, while it acts on
{L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
in our case. Since the lowest eigenvalue of the operator
(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+2\mathbf{A})^{2}
equals
2B
when understood to act on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
or on
L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
, we obtain the same asymptotic behavior of
T_{\mathrm{c}}(B)
as in
[
17
, Theorem 4]
.

Theorem
3.5
is valid for the precise temperature scaling
T=T_{\mathrm{c}}(1-DB)
. In order to prove Theorem
2
(a), we also need to show that the system is superconducting for temperatures that are small compared to
T_{\mathrm{c}}(1-DB)
. This is guaranteed by the following proposition.

Proposition 3.6

(A priori bound on Theorem
2
(a))
.

Let Assumptions
1.3
and
1.5
(a) hold. Then, for every
T_{0}>0
there are constants
B_{0}>0
and
D_{0}>0
such that for all
0<B\leqslant B_{0}
and all temperatures
T
obeying

\displaystyle T_{0}\leqslant T<T_{\mathrm{c}}(1-D_{0}B),

there is an admissible BCS state
\Gamma
with

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{0})<0.

(3.27)

3.3
The upper bound on (
1.21
) and proof of Theorem
2
(a)

Using the results in the previous section, we provide the proofs of the upper bound on (
1.21
) and of Theorem
2
(a). The statements in the previous section, that is, Propositions
3.2
and
3.4
, as well as Theorem
3.5
are proven in Section
4
.

Proof of the upper bound on (
1.21
).

Let
D\in\mathbb{R}
be given, let
D_{0}:=1+|D|
, and let
\Psi
be a minimizer of the Ginzburg–Landau functional, i.e.,
\mathcal{E}_{D,B}^{\mathrm{GL}}(\Psi)=E^{\mathrm{GL}}(D)
. We note that
\Psi
belongs to
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
and has uniformly bounded
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
-norm. Let
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
be as in (
3.1
) and let
T=T_{\mathrm{c}}(1-DB)
. We apply Proposition
3.4
with the choice
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\Delta}
and find

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{\Delta})-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{0})

\displaystyle\leqslant-\frac{1}{4}\langle\Delta,L_{T,B}\Delta\rangle+\frac{1}{8}\langle\Delta,N_{T,B}(\Delta)\rangle+\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\,\langle\alpha_{*},V\alpha_{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip-20.0pt-\int_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;V(r)\,\bigl|\alpha_{\Delta}(X,r)-\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)\bigr|^{2}+CB^{3}.

(3.28)

The first term in the last line is bounded by
\|V\|_{\infty}\|\eta\|_{2}^{2}
and a bound for the
L^{2}
-norm of
\eta:=\eta_{0}+\eta_{\perp}
is provided by (
3.7
) and (
3.8
). In fact, by Assumption
1.3
, this term is nonpositive but we do not need to use this here. By Theorem
3.5
, this implies

\displaystyle F^{\mathrm{BCS}}(T_{\mathrm{c}}(1-DB),B)

\displaystyle\leqslant B^{2}\,E^{\mathrm{GL}}(D)+CB^{3},

which concludes the proof of the upper bound on (
1.21
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
2
(a).

Let
D_{0}>0
be given and let us recall the definition of
D_{\mathrm{c}}
in (
1.20
). We show that there is a constant
D_{1}>0
and appropriate trial states such that (
3.27
) holds for all temperatures
T
obeying

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{c}}(1-D_{0}B)\leqslant T<T_{\mathrm{c}}(1-D_{\mathrm{c}}\,B-D_{1}\,B^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}),

(3.29)

provided
B>0
is small enough. Since Proposition
3.6
covers the remaining range of
T
, this proves Theorem
2
(a).

We define
D:=\frac{T_{\mathrm{c}}-T}{BT_{\mathrm{c}}}
and note that (
3.29
) yields
D-D_{\mathrm{c}}>D_{1}B^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
. Let
\psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{1})
be a ground state of the linear operator in (
1.20
) and let
\Psi
be as in (
1.18
). Accordingly, we have
(\Lambda_{0}/\Lambda_{2})\Pi^{2}\Psi=BD_{\mathrm{c}}\Psi
and

\displaystyle\inf_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D,B}(\theta\Psi)=-\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{2}(D-D_{\mathrm{c}})^{2}\|\psi\|_{2}^{4}}{4\Lambda_{3}\|\psi\|_{4}^{4}},

where the optimal
\mathrm{\theta_{c}}
satisfies
\Lambda_{2}(D-D_{\mathrm{c}})\|\psi\|_{2}^{2}=2\Lambda_{3}\|\psi\|_{4}^{4}\,\mathrm{\theta_{c}}^{2}
. We combine Proposition
3.4
and Theorem
3.5
applied to
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\Delta}
with
\Delta=\Delta_{\mathrm{\theta_{c}}\Psi}
, to see that (
3.28
) holds in this case as well. Let us note that (
3.29
) implies
|T-T_{\mathrm{c}}|\leqslant CB
. Proposition
3.2
and (
3.28
) therefore allow us to conclude that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{\Delta})-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{0})

\displaystyle\leqslant-\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{2}\|\psi\|_{2}^{4}}{4\Lambda_{3}\|\psi\|_{4}^{4}}\;(D-D_{\mathrm{c}})^{2}\;B^{2}+CB^{3}.

(3.30)

The right side is negative provided
D_{1}>0
is chosen large enough since
D-D_{\mathrm{c}}>D_{1}B^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
. This shows (
3.27
) for temperatures
T
satisfying (
3.29
) and completes the proof of Theorem
2
(a).
∎

4
Proofs of the Results in Section
3

4.1
Schatten norm estimates for operators given by product kernels

In this subsection we provide estimates for several norms of gauge-periodic operators with integral kernels given by product functions of the form
\tau(x-y)\Psi((x+y)/2)
, which will be used frequently in our proofs.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
B>0
, let
\Psi
be a gauge-periodic function on
Q_{B}
and let
\tau
be an even and real-valued function on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Moreover, let the operator
\alpha
be defined via its integral kernel
\alpha(X,r):=\tau(r)\Psi(X)
, i.e.,
\alpha
acts as

\displaystyle\alpha f(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}y\;\tau(x-y)\Psi\bigl(\frac{x+y}{2}\bigr)f(y),

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}).

(a)

Let
p\in\{2,4,6\}
. If
\Psi\in L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{p}(Q_{B})
and
\tau\in L^{\frac{p}{p-1}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, then
\alpha\in\mathcal{S}^{p}
and

\displaystyle\|\alpha\|_{p}\leqslant C\;\|\tau\|_{\frac{p}{p-1}}\;\|\Psi\|_{p}.

(b)

For any
\nu>3
, there is a
C_{\nu}>0
, independent of
B
, such that if
(1+|\cdot|)^{\nu}\tau\in L^{\nicefrac{{6}}{{5}}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
\Psi\in L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{6}(Q_{B})
, then
\alpha\in\mathcal{S}^{\infty}
and

\displaystyle\|\alpha\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{\nu}\,B^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}\;\max\{1,B^{\nicefrac{{\nu}}{{2}}}\}\;\|(1+|\cdot|)^{\nu}\tau\|_{\nicefrac{{6}}{{5}}}\;\|\Psi\|_{6}.

Proof.

The case
p=2
of part (a) holds trivially with equality and
C=1
. Since
\tau
is even and real-valued, the kernel of
\alpha^{*}\alpha
is given by

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}\alpha(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}z\;\tau(x-z)\overline{\Psi\bigl(\frac{x+z}{2}\bigr)}\tau(z-y)\Psi\bigl(\frac{z+y}{2}\bigr).

Using
\|\alpha\|_{4}^{4}=\|\alpha^{*}\alpha\|_{2}^{2}
and the change of variables
z\mapsto x-z
and
y\mapsto x-y
, we see that

\displaystyle\|\alpha\|_{4}^{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|Q_{B}|}\iint_{Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\;\Bigl|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}z\;\tau(z)\,\tau(y-z)\,\overline{\Psi\bigl(x-\frac{z}{2}\bigr)}\,\Psi\bigl(x-\frac{y+z}{2}\bigr)\Bigr|^{2}.

By Hölder’s inequality, Young’s inequality and
the fact that
|\Psi|
is periodic, we see that

\displaystyle\|\alpha\|_{4}^{4}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\Psi\|_{4}^{4}\;\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}y\,\Bigl|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}z\;|\tau(z)\tau(y-z)|\Bigr|^{2}\leqslant C\;\|\tau\|_{\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}}^{4}\;\|\Psi\|_{4}^{4}

holds. This proves part (a) for
p=4
. If
p=6
we use
\|\alpha\|_{6}^{6}=\|\alpha\,\alpha^{*}\alpha\|_{2}^{2}
and a similar change of variables to write

\displaystyle\|\alpha\alpha^{*}\alpha\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|Q_{B}|}\iint_{Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\;\Bigl|\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}z_{1}\mathrm{d}z_{2}\;\tau(z_{1})\,\tau(z_{2}-z_{1})\,\tau(y-z_{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 120.0pt\times\Psi\bigl(x-\frac{z_{1}}{2}\bigr)\overline{\Psi\bigl(x-\frac{x_{1}+z_{2}}{2}\bigr)}\Psi\bigl(x-\frac{z_{2}+y}{2}\bigr)\Bigr|^{2}.

We thus obtain
\|\alpha\|_{6}^{6}\leqslant\|\Psi\|_{6}^{6}\;\|\tau*\tau*\tau\|_{2}^{2}
, which, in combination with Young’s inequality, proves the claimed bound.

In case of part (b), we follow closely the strategy of the proof of
[
19
, Eq. (5.51)]
. Let
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and let
\chi_{j}
denote the characteristic function of the cube with side length
\sqrt{2\pi B^{-1}}
centered at
j\in\Lambda_{B}
. We estimate

\displaystyle|\langle f,\alpha g\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{j,k\in\Lambda_{B}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\;\bigl|\chi_{j}(x)f(x)\Psi\bigl(\frac{x+y}{2}\bigr)\tau(x-y)\chi_{k}(y)g(y)\bigr|.

(4.1)

Let
|\cdot|_{\infty}
and
|\cdot|
denote the maximum norm and the euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, respectively. We observe that the estimates
|x-j|_{\infty}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{2\pi B^{-1}}
and
|y-k|_{\infty}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{2\pi B^{-1}}
imply
|\frac{x+y}{2}-\frac{j+k}{2}|_{\infty}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{2\pi B^{-1}}
. Accordingly, if
\chi_{j}(x)\chi_{k}(y)
equals
1
, so does
\chi_{\frac{j+k}{2}}(\frac{x+y}{2})
and we may replace
\Psi
on the right side of (
4.1
) by
\chi_{\frac{j+k}{2}}\Psi
without changing the term. The above bounds for
|x-j|_{\infty}
and
|y-k|_{\infty}
also imply
|j-k|\leqslant|x-y|+\sqrt{6\pi B^{-1}}
, which yields the lower bound

\displaystyle|x-y|\geqslant\bigl[|j-k|-\sqrt{6\pi B^{-1}}\bigr]_{+}.

(4.2)

We choose
\nu>3
, insert the factor
(\sqrt{2\pi B^{-1}}+|x-y|)^{\nu}
and its inverse in (
4.1
), use (
4.2
) to estimate the inverse, apply Cauchy–Schwarz in the
x
-coordinate, and obtain

\displaystyle|\langle f,\alpha g\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{j,k\in\Lambda_{B}}\bigl(\sqrt{2\pi B^{-1}}+\bigl[|j-k|-\sqrt{6\pi B^{-1}}\bigr]_{+}\bigr)^{-\nu}\;\|\chi_{j}f\|_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-20.0pt\times\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}x\,\Bigl|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}y\;\bigl|(\chi_{\frac{j+k}{2}}\Psi)\bigl(\frac{x+y}{2}\bigr)\bigl(\sqrt{2\pi B^{-1}}+|x-y|\bigr)^{\nu}\tau(x-y)\chi_{k}(y)g(y)\bigr|\;\Bigr|^{2}\Bigr)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}.

An application of Hölder’s inequality in the
y
-coordinate shows that the second line is bounded by

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\bigl|(\sqrt{2\pi B^{-1}}+|\cdot|)^{\nu}\tau\bigr|^{\nicefrac{{6}}{{5}}}*|\chi_{k}g|^{\nicefrac{{6}}{{5}}}\Bigr\|_{\nicefrac{{5}}{{3}}}^{\nicefrac{{5}}{{6}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\bigl\|\bigl(\sqrt{2\pi B^{-1}}+|\cdot|\bigr)^{\nu}\tau\bigr\|_{\nicefrac{{6}}{{5}}}\,\|\chi_{k}g\|_{2}

times
|Q_{B}|^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{6}}}\|\Psi\|_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{6}(Q_{B})}
. We highlight that the
L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{6}(Q_{B})
-norm is defined via a normalized integral, whence we needed to insert the factor of
|Q_{B}|^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{6}}}
.
Hence,

\displaystyle|\langle f,\alpha g\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant CB^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}\,\|\Psi\|_{6}\,\bigl\|\bigl(\sqrt{2\pi B^{-1}}+|\cdot|\bigr)^{\nu}\tau\bigr\|_{\nicefrac{{6}}{{5}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt\times\sum_{j,k\in\Lambda_{B}}\bigl(\sqrt{2\pi B^{-1}}+\bigl[|j-k|-\sqrt{6\pi B^{-1}}\bigr]_{+}\bigr)^{-\nu}\;\|\chi_{j}f\|_{2}\;\|\chi_{k}g\|_{2}.

For
\lambda>0
we estimate
\|\chi_{j}f\|_{2}\|\chi_{k}g\|_{2}\leqslant\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\chi_{j}f\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2\lambda}\|\chi_{k}g\|_{2}^{2}
. In each term, we carry out one of the sums and optimize the resulting expression over
\lambda
. We find
\lambda=\|g\|_{2}\;\|f\|_{2}^{-1}
as well as

\displaystyle|\langle f,\alpha g\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant CB^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}\|f\|_{2}\;\|g\|_{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{6}\;\frac{\|(\sqrt{2\pi B^{-1}}+|\cdot|)^{\nu}\tau\|_{\nicefrac{{6}}{{5}}}}{(2\pi B^{-1})^{\nicefrac{{\nu}}{{2}}}}\;\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}}\bigl(1+[|j|-\sqrt{3}]_{+}\bigr)^{-\nu}.

The fraction involving
\tau
is bounded by
