C_{\nu}\max\{1,B^{\nicefrac{{\nu}}{{2}}}\}\|(1+|\cdot|)^{\nu}\tau\|_{\nicefrac{{6}}{{5}}}
. This proves the claim.
∎

4.2
Proof of Lemma
3.1

We recall the definition of
\Gamma_{\Delta}
in (
3.4
) and that of the normal state
\Gamma_{0}
in (
1.12
). By definition,
\Gamma_{\Delta}
is a gauge-periodic generalized fermionic one-particle density matrix. Therefore, we only have to check the trace class condition (
1.8
).

To this end, we use the expansion (
3.14
) of the hyperbolic tangent in terms of the Matsubara frequencies, the identity
(\exp(x)+1)^{-1}=(1-\tanh(x/2))/2
, and the resolvent equation

\displaystyle(z-H_{\Delta})^{-1}=(z-H_{0})^{-1}+(z-H_{0})^{-1}\;(H_{\Delta}-H_{0})\;(z-H_{\Delta})^{-1}

(4.3)

to write

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\Delta}=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\tanh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}\bigr)=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{\Delta}}=\Gamma_{0}+\mathcal{O}+\mathcal{Q}_{T,B}(\Delta),

(4.4)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{0}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{T,B}(\Delta)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{\Delta}}

(4.5)

with
\delta
in (
3.3
). Since
\mathcal{O}
is offdiagonal, we have
[\mathcal{O}]_{11}=0
and the operator
(1+\pi^{2})[\mathcal{O}]_{11}
is locally trace class trivially. Using (
4.25
), we see that

\displaystyle\bigl[\mathcal{Q}_{T,B}(\Delta)\bigr]_{11}=\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{B}}\Delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{B}}}\overline{\Delta}\Bigl[\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{\Delta}}\Bigr]_{11}.

An application of Hölder’s inequality shows that
(1+\pi^{2})[\mathcal{Q}_{T,B}(\Delta)]_{11}
is locally trace class. It remains to show that
(1+\pi^{2})\gamma_{0}
is locally trace class. But this follows from the bound
(1+x)(\exp(\beta(x-\mu))+1)^{-1}\leqslant C_{\beta,a}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\beta}{2}(x-\mu)}
for
x\geqslant a
, the diamagnetic inequality for the magnetic heat kernel, see e.g.
[
41
, Theorem 4.4]
, and the explicit formula for the heat kernel of the Laplacian. This concludes the proof of Lemma
3.1
.

4.3
Proof of Proposition
3.4

We recall the definitions of
\Delta(X,r)=-2\,V\alpha_{*}(r)\,\Psi(X)
in (
3.1
), the Hamiltonian
H_{\Delta}
in (
3.3
) and
\Gamma_{\Delta}=(1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta H_{\Delta}})^{-1}
in (
3.4
). Throughout this section we assume that the function
\Psi
in the definition of
\Delta
is in
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})
. From Lemma
3.1
, which is proved in Section
4.2
below, we know that
\Gamma_{\Delta}
is an admissible BCS state in this case. We define the anti-unitary operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}0&J\\
-J&0\end{pmatrix}

with
J
defined below (
1.5
). The operator
H_{\Delta}
obeys the relation
\mathcal{J}H_{\Delta}\mathcal{J}^{*}=-H_{\Delta}
, which implies
\mathcal{J}\Gamma_{\Delta}\mathcal{J}^{*}=1-\Gamma_{\Delta}
. Using this and the cyclicity of the trace, we write the entropy of
\Gamma_{\Delta}
as

\displaystyle S(\Gamma_{\Delta})=\frac{1}{2}\Tr[\varphi(\Gamma_{\Delta})],

(4.6)

where
\varphi(x):=-[x\ln(x)+(1-x)\ln(1-x)]
for
0\leqslant x\leqslant 1
.

In order to rewrite the BCS functional, it is useful to introduce a weaker notion of trace per unit volume. More precisely, we call a gauge-periodic operator
A
acting on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
weakly locally trace class if
P_{0}AP_{0}
and
Q_{0}AQ_{0}
are locally trace class, where

\displaystyle P_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}

(4.7)

and
Q_{0}=1-P_{0}
, and we define its weak trace per unit volume by

\displaystyle\Tr_{0}(A):=\Tr\bigl(P_{0}AP_{0}+Q_{0}AQ_{0}\bigr).

(4.8)

If an operator is locally trace class then it is also weakly locally trace class but the converse need not be true. It is true, however, in case of nonnegative operators. If an operator is locally trace class then its weak trace per unit volume and its usual trace per unit volume coincide.

Before their appearance in the context of BCS theory in
[
19
,
20
]
, weak traces of the above kind appeared in
[
32
,
23
]
. In
[
32
, Lemma 1]
it has been shown that if two weak traces
\Tr_{P}
and
\Tr_{P^{\prime}}
are defined via projections
P
and
P^{\prime}
then
\Tr_{P}(A)=\Tr_{P^{\prime}}(A)
holds for appropriate
A
if
P-P^{\prime}
is a Hilbert–Schmidt operator.

Let
\Gamma
be an admissible BCS state and recall the normal state
\Gamma_{0}
in (
1.12
).
In terms of the weak trace per unit volume, the BCS functional can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{0})\hskip-90.0pt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Tr\bigl[(H_{0}\Gamma-H_{0}\Gamma_{0})-T\varphi(\Gamma)+T\varphi(\Gamma_{0})\bigr]-\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;V(r)\,|\alpha(X,r)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Tr_{0}\bigl[(H_{\Delta}\Gamma_{\Delta}-H_{0}\Gamma_{0})-T\varphi(\Gamma_{\Delta})+T\varphi(\Gamma_{0})\bigr]

(4.9)

\displaystyle\hskip 55.0pt+\frac{1}{2}\Tr_{0}\bigl[(H_{\Delta}\Gamma-H_{\Delta}\Gamma_{\Delta})-T\varphi(\Gamma)+T\varphi(\Gamma_{\Delta})\bigr]

(4.10)

\displaystyle\hskip 55.0pt-\frac{1}{2}\Tr_{0}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Delta\\
\overline{\Delta}&0\end{pmatrix}\Gamma-\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;V(r)\,|\alpha(X,r)|^{2}.

(4.11)

Note that we added and subtracted the first term in (
4.9
) and that we added and subtracted the first term in (
4.11
) to replace the Hamiltonian
H_{0}
in (
4.10
) by
H_{\Delta}
. The operators inside the traces in (
4.9
) and (
4.10
) are not necessarily locally trace class, which is the reason we introduce the weak local trace. We also note that (
4.10
) equals
\frac{T}{2}
times the relative entropy
\mathcal{H}_{0}(\Gamma,\Gamma_{\Delta})
of
\Gamma
with respect to
\Gamma_{\Delta}
, defined in (
3.18
).

The first term in (
4.11
) can be written as

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\Tr_{0}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Delta\\
\overline{\Delta}&0\end{pmatrix}\Gamma

\displaystyle=2\Re\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;(V\alpha_{*})(r)\Psi(X)\;\overline{\alpha}(X,r).

(4.12)

The factors in (
4.11
) and (
4.12
) multiplying
V
are equal to

-|\alpha(X,r)|^{2}+2\Re\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)\;\overline{\alpha}(X,r)=-\bigl|\alpha(X,r)-\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)\bigr|^{2}+\bigl|\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)\bigr|^{2}.

To rewrite (
4.9
) we need the following identities, whose proofs are straightforward computations:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\Delta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\tanh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}\bigr),

\displaystyle\ln(\Gamma_{\Delta})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}-\ln\bigl(2\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}\bigr)\bigr),

\displaystyle 1-\Gamma_{\Delta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\tanh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}\bigr),

\displaystyle\ln(1-\Gamma_{\Delta})

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}-\ln\bigl(2\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}\bigr)\bigr).

(4.13)

Eq. (
4.13
) implies

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\Delta}\ln(\Gamma_{\Delta})+(1-\Gamma_{\Delta})\ln(1-\Gamma_{\Delta})=-\ln\bigl(2\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}\bigr)\bigr)+\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}\tanh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}\bigr)\bigr),

(4.14)

as well as

\displaystyle\beta H_{\Delta}\Gamma_{\Delta}-\varphi(\Gamma_{\Delta})

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}-\ln\bigl(2\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}\bigr)\bigr).

This allows us to rewrite (
4.9
) as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\beta}\,\Tr_{0}\bigl[(\beta H_{\Delta}\Gamma_{\Delta}-\beta H_{0}\Gamma_{0})-\varphi(\Gamma_{\Delta})+\varphi(\Gamma_{0})\bigr]

\displaystyle\hskip-180.0pt=\frac{1}{4}\Tr_{0}\bigl[H_{\Delta}-H_{0}\bigr]-\frac{1}{2\beta}\Tr_{0}\bigl[\ln\bigl(\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}\bigr)\bigr)-\ln\bigl(\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{0}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr].

(4.15)

We note that
H_{\Delta}-H_{0}
is weakly locally trace class and that its weak trace equals
0
. This, in particular, implies that the second term on the right side of (
4.15
) is weakly locally trace class. To summarize, our intermediate result reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{0})\hskip-60.0pt

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\beta}\Tr_{0}\bigl[\ln\bigl(\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}\bigr)\bigr)-\ln\bigl(\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{0}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt+\|\Psi\|_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})}^{2}\;\langle\alpha_{*},V\alpha_{*}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt+\frac{T}{2}\mathcal{H}_{0}(\Gamma,\Gamma_{\Delta})-\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;V(r)\,\bigl|\alpha(X,r)-\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)\bigr|^{2}.

(4.16)

In order to compute the first term on the right side of (
4.16
), we need Lemma
4.2
below. It is the main technical novelty of our trial state analysis and should be compared to the related part in the proof of
[
19
, Theorem 2]
. The main difference between our proof of Lemma
4.2
and the relevant parts of the proof of
[
19
, Theorem 2]
is that we use the product representation of the hyperbolic cosine in (
4.18
) below instead of a Cauchy integral representation of the function
z\mapsto\ln(1+e^{-z})
. In this way we obtain better decay properties in the subsequent resolvent expansion, which simplifies the analysis considerably.

As already noted above, the admissibility of
\Gamma_{\Delta}
implies that the difference between the two operators in the first term on the right side of (
4.16
) is weakly locally trace class. We highlight that this is a nontrivial statement because each of the two operators separately does not share this property. We also highlight that our proof of Lemma
4.2
does not require this as an assumption, it implies the statement independently.

In combination with (
4.16
), Lemma
4.2
below proves Proposition
3.4
. Before we state the lemma, we recall the definitions of the operators
L_{T,B}
and
N_{T,B}
in (
3.15
) and (
3.16
), respectively.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
V\alpha_{*}\in L^{\nicefrac{{6}}{{5}}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. For any
B>0
, any
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})
, and any
T>0
, the operator

\displaystyle\ln\bigl(\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}\bigr)\bigr)-\ln\bigl(\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{0}\bigr)\bigr)

is weakly locally trace class and its weak local trace equals

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\beta}\Tr_{0}\Bigl[\ln\bigl(\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}\bigr)\bigr)-\ln\bigl(\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{0}\bigr)\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle\hskip-100.0pt=-\frac{1}{4}\langle\Delta,L_{T,B}\Delta\rangle+\frac{1}{8}\langle\Delta,N_{T,B}(\Delta)\rangle+\Tr\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta).

(4.17)

The operator
\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)
is locally trace class and its trace norm satisfies the bound

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;T^{-5}\;B^{3}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{6}.

Proof of Lemma
4.2
.

We recall the Matsubara frequencies in (
3.13
) and write the hyperbolic cosine in terms of the following product expansion, see
[
1
, Eq. (4.5.69)]
,

\displaystyle\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}x\bigr)

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=0}^{\infty}\Bigl(1+\frac{x^{2}}{\omega_{k}^{2}}\Bigr).

(4.18)

We have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\ln\bigl(1+\frac{x^{2}}{\omega_{k}^{2}}\bigr)=\ln\bigl(\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}x\bigr)\bigr)\leqslant\frac{\beta}{2}\;|x|,

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{R},

and accordingly

\displaystyle\ln\bigl(\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}\bigr)\bigr)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\ln\bigl(1+\frac{H_{\Delta}^{2}}{\omega_{k}^{2}}\bigr)

holds in a strong sense on the domain of
|H_{\Delta}|
. Since
\Delta
is a bounded operator by Lemma
4.1
, the domains of
|H_{\Delta}|
and
|H_{0}|
coincide. The identity

\displaystyle\ln\bigl(\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}\bigr)\bigr)-\ln\bigl(\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{0}\bigr)\bigr)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\bigl[\ln\bigl(\omega_{k}^{2}+H_{\Delta}^{2}\bigr)-\ln\bigl(\omega_{k}^{2}+H_{0}^{2}\bigr)\bigr]

(4.19)

therefore holds in a strong sense on the domain of
|H_{0}|
. Elementary arguments show that

\displaystyle\ln\bigl(\omega^{2}+H_{\Delta}^{2}\bigr)-\ln\bigl(\omega^{2}+H_{0}^{2}\bigr)=-\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{\omega}^{R}\mathrm{d}u\;\Bigl[\frac{2u}{u^{2}+H_{\Delta}^{2}}-\frac{2u}{u^{2}+H_{0}^{2}}\Bigr]

(4.20)

holds for
\omega>0
in a strong sense on the domain of
\ln(1+|H_{0}|)
. Therefore, by (
4.19
) and (
4.20
), we have

\displaystyle\ln\bigl(\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}\bigr)\bigr)-\ln\bigl(\cosh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{0}\bigr)\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip-100.0pt=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\int_{\omega_{k}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}u\;\Bigl[\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{\Delta}}-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}+\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{\Delta}}-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\Bigr]

(4.21)

in a strong sense on the domain of
|H_{0}|
. By a slight abuse of notation, we have incorporated the limit in (
4.20
) into the integral.

In the next step we use the resolvent expansion in (
4.3
) to see that the right side of (
4.21
) equals

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{1}+\mathcal{D}_{2}+\mathcal{O}_{3}+\mathcal{D}_{4}+\mathcal{O}_{5}-2\beta\,\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta),

with two diagonal operators
\mathcal{D}_{2}
and
\mathcal{D}_{4}
, three offdiagonal operators
\mathcal{O}_{1}
,
\mathcal{O}_{3}
and
\mathcal{O}_{5}
and a remainder term
\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)
. The index of the operators reflects the number of
\delta
matrices appearing in their definition. The diagonal operators
\mathcal{D}_{2}
and
\mathcal{D}_{4}
are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{2}

\displaystyle:=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\int_{\omega_{k}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}u\;\Bigl[\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}+\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\Bigr],

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{4}

\displaystyle:=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\int_{\omega_{k}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}u\;\Bigl[\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}

\displaystyle\hskip 120.0pt+\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\Bigr]

and the offdiagonal operators read

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{1}

\displaystyle:=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\int_{\omega_{k}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}u\;\Bigl[\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}+\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\Bigr],

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{3}

\displaystyle:=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\int_{\omega_{k}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}u\;\Bigl[\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.0pt+\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\Bigr],

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{5}

\displaystyle:=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\int_{\omega_{k}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}u\;\Bigl[\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.0pt+\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\Bigr].

Since the operators
\mathcal{O}_{1}
,
\mathcal{O}_{3}
, and
\mathcal{O}_{5}
are offdiagonal, they are weakly locally trace class and their weak local trace equals
0
. We also note that the operator
\mathcal{O}_{1}
is not necessarily locally trace class, which is why we need to work with the weak local trace. The operator
\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)

\displaystyle\hskip-40.0pt:=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\beta}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\int_{\omega_{k}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}u\;\Bigl[\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{\Delta}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt+\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{\Delta}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\Bigr].

As we show in the next paragraph, the operators
\mathcal{D}_{2}
,
\mathcal{D}_{4}
and
\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)
are locally trace class. This, in particular, implies that (
4.21
) also holds when the expectation with respect to vectors
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
satisfying either
P_{0}\psi=\psi
or
Q_{0}\psi=\psi
is taken on both sides of the equation.

It remains to compute the traces of
\mathcal{D}_{2}
and
\mathcal{D}_{4}
, and to estimate the trace norm of
\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)
. We first consider
\mathcal{D}_{2}
and use Hölder’s inequality in (
2.1
) to estimate

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u\pm H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u\pm H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u\pm H_{0}}\Bigr\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\Bigl\|\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u\pm H_{0}}\Bigr\|_{\infty}^{3}\;\|\delta\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{2}{u^{3}}\;\|\Delta\|_{2}^{2}.

(4.22)

Therefore, Lemma
4.1
shows that the combination of the series and the integral defining
\mathcal{D}_{2}
converges absolutely in local trace norm.
In particular,
\mathcal{D}_{2}
is locally trace class and we may arbitrarily interchange the trace, the sum, and the integral to compute its trace. We do this, use the cyclicity of the trace, and obtain

\Tr\mathcal{D}_{2}=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\int_{\omega_{k}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}u\;\Tr\Bigl[\Bigl(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\Bigr)^{2}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u-H_{0}}\delta+\Bigl(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\Bigr)^{2}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u+H_{0}}\delta\Bigr].

(4.23)

Integration by parts shows

\displaystyle\int_{\omega_{k}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}u\;\Bigl(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u\pm H_{0}}\Bigr)^{2}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u\pm H_{0}}\delta

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\;\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{k}\pm H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{k}\pm H_{0}}\delta

\displaystyle\hskip 80.0pt-\int_{\omega_{k}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}u\;\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u\pm H_{0}}\delta\Bigl(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u\pm H_{0}}\Bigr)^{2}\delta,

and another application of the cyclicity of the trace yields

\displaystyle\Tr\int_{\omega_{k}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}u\;\Bigl(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u\pm H_{0}}\Bigr)^{2}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}u\pm H_{0}}\delta

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\;\Tr\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{k}\pm H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{k}\pm H_{0}}\delta.

(4.24)

Note that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{k}\pm H_{0}}\,\delta\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{k}\pm H_{0}}\,\delta

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{k}\pm\mathfrak{h}_{B}}\,\Delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{k}\mp\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{B}}}\,\overline{\Delta}\\
&\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{k}\mp\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{B}}}\,\overline{\Delta}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{k}\pm\mathfrak{h}_{B}}\,\Delta\end{pmatrix}.

(4.25)

We combine this with (
4.23
) and (
4.24
) and summarize the cases
\pm
into a single sum over
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. This yields

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\beta}\Tr\mathcal{D}_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Bigl\langle\Delta,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{B}}\Delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{B}}}\Bigr\rangle=-\frac{1}{4}\langle\Delta,L_{T,B}\Delta\rangle,

where
L_{T,B}
is the operator defined in (
3.15
).

We argue as above to see that the integrand in the definition of
\mathcal{D}_{4}
is bounded by
C\|\Delta\|_{4}^{4}\,u^{-5}
. Moreover, we have
\|\Delta\|_{4}^{4}\leqslant CB^{2}\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}}^{4}\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{4}
by (
2.7
) and Lemma
4.1
.
Therefore, the integral and the sum in
\mathcal{D}_{4}
are absolutely convergent with respect to the local trace norm.
The trace of
\mathcal{D}_{4}
is computed similar to that of
\mathcal{D}_{2}
. With
N_{T,B}
defined in (
3.16
), the result reads

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\beta}\Tr\mathcal{D}_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\langle\Delta,N_{T,B}(\Delta)\rangle.

(4.26)

In the case of
\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)
, we bound the trace norm of the operator inside the integral by
u^{-7}\|\Delta\|_{6}^{6}
. Using (
2.7
) and Lemma
4.1
, we estimate the second factor by a constant times
\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{\nicefrac{{6}}{{5}}}^{6}B^{-3}\|\Pi\Psi\|_{2}^{6}\leqslant CB^{3}\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{6}
. Finally, integration over
u
yields the term
6\pi^{-6}T^{-6}(2k+1)^{-6}
, which is summable in
k
. This proves the claimed bound for the trace norm of
\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)
.
∎

4.4
Proof of Theorem
3.5

4.4.1
Magnetic resolvent estimates

In this preparatory subsection, we provide estimates for the magnetic resolvent kernel

\displaystyle G^{z}_{B}(x,y)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{z-\mathfrak{h}_{B}}(x,y),

\displaystyle x,y

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

We also introduce the function

\displaystyle g_{B}^{z}(x)

\displaystyle:=G_{B}^{z}(x,0),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

(4.27)

The proof of the following statement can be found in
[
17
, Lemma 8]
.

Lemma 4.3
.

For all
B\geqslant 0
,
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[B,\infty)
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
we have

(a)

g_{B}^{z}(-x)=g_{B}^{z}(x)
,

(b)

G_{B}^{z}(x,y)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot(x\wedge y)}\;g_{B}^{z}(x-y)
.

We start our analysis by providing a decay estimate for the
L^{1}
-norm of the resolvent kernel
g^{z}_{0}
in (
4.27
) and its gradient in the case
B=0
. For
g_{0}^{z}
such an estimate has been provided in
[
17
, Lemma 9]
. Since we additionally need an estimate for
\nabla g_{0}^{z}
, we repeat some of the arguments here.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
a>-2
. There is a constant
C_{a}>0
such that for
t,\omega\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\,|\cdot|^{a}g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{a}\;f(t,\omega)^{1+\frac{a}{2}},

(4.28)

where

\displaystyle f(t,\omega):=\frac{|\omega|+|t+\mu|}{(|\omega|+(t+\mu)_{-})^{2}}

(4.29)

and
x_{-}:=-\min\{x,0\}
. Furthermore, for any
a>-1
, there is a constant
C_{a}>0
with

\displaystyle\left\|\,|\cdot|^{a}\nabla g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}\right\|_{1}\leqslant C_{a}\;f(t,\omega)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{a}{2}}\;\Bigl[1+\frac{|\omega|+|t+\mu|}{|\omega|+(t+\mu)_{-}}\Bigr].

(4.30)

Proof.

The resolvent kernel
g_{0}^{z}
is given by

\displaystyle g_{0}^{z}(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4\pi|x|}\;\mathrm{e}^{-\sqrt{-(z+\mu)}\;|x|},

(4.31)

where
\sqrt{\cdot}
denotes the standard branch of the square root. As long as
a>-2
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\,|\cdot|^{a}g_{0}^{z}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}x\;|x|^{a}\;\Bigl|\frac{1}{4\pi|x|}\mathrm{e}^{-|x|\sqrt{-(z+\mu)}}\Bigr|=\frac{\Gamma(a+2)}{(\Re\sqrt{-(z+\mu)})^{a+2}}.

(4.32)

Moreover,

\displaystyle(\Re\sqrt{-z})^{2}=\frac{1}{2}(|z|-\Re z)\geqslant\begin{cases}\frac{1}{4}\frac{|\Im z|^{2}}{\Re z+|\Im z|}&\Re z\geqslant 0,\\
\frac{1}{2}|z|&\Re z<0,\end{cases}

and hence

\displaystyle(\Re\sqrt{-(t+\mu+\mathrm{i}\omega)})^{2}\geqslant\frac{1}{4}\frac{(|\omega|+(t+\mu)_{-})^{2}}{|\omega|+|t+\mu|}.

This proves (
4.28
). To prove (
4.30
), we use (
4.31
)
and estimate

\displaystyle|\nabla g_{0}^{z}(x)|\leqslant|z+\mu|^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\;|g_{0}^{z}(x)|+|x|^{-1}|g_{0}^{z}(x)|.

This shows the second estimate for
a>-1
.
∎

In the next step we prove estimates for the
L^{1}
-norms of
g_{B}^{z}
and
g_{B}^{z}-g_{0}^{z}
and the gradient of these functions if
B\neq 0
. Once more, some of the arguments in
[
17
, Lemma 10]
reappear in our proof below, ensuring self-consistency.

Lemma 4.5
.

For any
a\geqslant 0
, there are constants
\delta_{a},C_{a}>0
such that for all
t,\omega\in\mathbb{R}
and for all
B\geqslant 0
with
f(t,\omega)^{2}\,B^{2}\leqslant\delta_{a}
, we have

\displaystyle\bigl\||\cdot|^{a}g_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}\bigr\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{a}\,f(t,\omega)^{1+\frac{a}{2}},

\displaystyle\bigl\||\cdot|^{a}\nabla g_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}\bigr\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{a}\,f(t,\omega)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{a}{2}}\Bigl[1+\frac{|\omega|+|t+\mu|}{|\omega|+(t+\mu)_{-}}\Bigr],

(4.33)

and

\displaystyle\bigl\|\,|\cdot|^{a}(g_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}-g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t})\bigr\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{a}\,B^{2}\,f(t,\omega)^{3+\frac{a}{2}},

\displaystyle\bigl\|\,|\cdot|^{a}(\nabla g_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}-\nabla g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t})\bigr\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{a}\,B^{2}\,f(t,\omega)^{\frac{5}{2}+\frac{a}{2}}\Bigl[1+\frac{|\omega|+|t+\mu|}{|\omega|+(t+\mu)_{-}}\Bigr]

(4.34)

with the function
f(t,\omega)
in (
4.29
).

Proof.

During the proof we use the notation
z=\mathrm{i}\omega+t
.
We define the function

\displaystyle h^{z}(x):=\frac{1}{4}\,|e_{3}\wedge x|^{2}\,g_{0}^{z}(x)

(4.35)

and choose
\delta_{a}
such that
2\delta_{a}D_{a}C_{2}=1
. Here
C_{2}
denotes the constant in (
4.28
) and
D_{a}:=1
if
0\leqslant a\leqslant 1
and
D_{a}:=2^{a}
if
a>1
. Lemma
4.4
and the bound
\|h^{z}\|_{1}\leqslant\||\cdot|^{2}g_{0}^{z}\|_{1}
imply

\displaystyle B^{2}D_{a}\|h^{z}\|_{1}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}

(4.36)

for all
\omega
,
t
, and
B
that are allowed by our assumptions. We define the operator
\widetilde{G}_{B}^{z}
by the kernel

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{B}^{z}(x,y)

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}(x\wedge y)}g_{0}^{z}(x-y)

and note that

\displaystyle(z-\mathfrak{h}_{B})\widetilde{G}_{B}^{z}=1-T_{B}^{z},

(4.37)

where
T_{B}^{z}
is the operator given by the kernel

\displaystyle T_{B}^{z}(x,y)

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}(x\wedge y)}\bigl[\mathbf{B}\wedge(x-y)(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{x})g_{0}^{z}(x-y)+B^{2}\;h^{z}(x-y)\bigr].

The first term in square brackets equals
0
because
g_{0}^{z}
is a radial function, which implies that the vector
\nabla g_{0}^{z}(x-y)
is perpendicular to
\mathbf{B}\wedge(x-y)
. Multiplication of (
4.37
) with
(z-\mathfrak{h}_{B})^{-1}
from the left yields

\displaystyle G_{B}^{z}(x,y)-\widetilde{G}_{B}^{z}(x,y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}v\;G_{B}^{z}(x,v)T_{B}^{z}(v,y).

We set
y=0
, change variables
v\mapsto x-v
, and find

\displaystyle g_{B}^{z}(x)-g_{0}^{z}(x)=B^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}v\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot(v\wedge x)}\;g_{B}^{z}(v)\;h^{z}(x-v).

(4.38)

This implies

\displaystyle\|g_{B}^{z}-g_{0}^{z}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant B^{2}\||g_{B}^{z}|*|h^{z}|\|_{1}\leqslant B^{2}\;\|g_{B}^{z}-g_{0}^{z}\|_{1}\;\|h^{z}\|_{1}+B^{2}\;\|g_{0}^{z}\|_{1}\;\|h^{z}\|_{1}.

(4.39)

A straightforward calculation involving (
4.37
) and the Neumann series shows that
g_{B}^{z}-g_{0}^{z}
belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Therefore, (
4.36
) and (
4.39
) imply

\displaystyle\|g_{B}^{z}-g_{0}^{z}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|g_{0}^{z}\|_{1}

(4.40)

for all
t,\omega
, and
B
that are allowed by our assumptions.

We use this estimate as a basis to prove the bounds claimed in the lemma and start with the first bound in (
4.34
). By (
4.38
), we have

\displaystyle\|\,|\cdot|^{a}(g_{B}^{z}-g_{0}^{z})\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant D_{a}B^{2}\bigl[\|\,|\cdot|^{a}g_{0}^{z}\|_{1}\,\|h^{z}\|_{1}+\|\,|\cdot|^{a}(g_{B}^{z}-g_{0}^{z})\|_{1}\,\|h^{z}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 80.0pt+\|g_{B}^{z}-g_{0}^{z}\|_{1}\,\||\cdot|^{a}h^{z}\|_{1}+\|g_{0}^{z}\|_{1}\,\|\,|\cdot|^{a}h^{z}\|_{1}\bigr].

A similar argument to the one above (
4.40
) shows that
|\cdot|^{a}(g_{B}^{z}-g_{0}^{z})
belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. In combination with (
4.40
), we therefore obtain

\displaystyle\|\,|\cdot|^{a}(g_{B}^{z}-g_{0}^{z})\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2D_{a}B^{2}\;\bigl[\||\cdot|^{a}g_{0}^{z}\|_{1}\,\|h^{z}\|_{1}+2\,\|g_{0}^{z}\|_{1}\,\||\cdot|^{a}h^{z}\|_{1}\bigr].

(4.41)

With the help of Lemma
4.4
and (
4.36
), we read off the first bound in (
4.34
). Moreover, the triangle inequality, Lemma
4.4
, the first bound in (
4.34
) and the bound
f(t,\omega)^{2}\,B^{2}\leqslant\delta_{a}
imply the first bound in (
4.33
).

Next, we consider the bounds in (
4.33
) and (
4.34
) involving the gradient. As a preparation, the bound
|\nabla h^{z}(x)|\leqslant|x|\,|g_{0}^{z}(x)|+|x|^{2}\,|\nabla g_{0}^{z}(x)|
and Lemma
4.4
show

\displaystyle\|\,|\cdot|^{a}\nabla h^{z}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{a}\;f(t,\omega)^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{a}{2}}\Bigl[1+\frac{|\omega|+|t+\mu|}{|\omega|+(t+\mu)_{-}}\Bigr].

(4.42)

We use
|\nabla\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot(v\wedge x)}|\leqslant B|v|
and (
4.38
) to see that

\displaystyle|\nabla g_{B}^{z}(x)-\nabla g_{0}^{z}(x)|

\displaystyle\leqslant B^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}v\;\bigl[B\,|v|\,|g_{B}^{z}(v)|\,|h^{z}(x-v)|+|g_{B}^{z}(v)|\,|\nabla h^{z}(x-v)|\bigr]

as well as

\displaystyle\|\,|\cdot|^{a}(\nabla g_{B}^{z}-\nabla g_{0}^{z})\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant D_{a}B^{2}\bigl[B\,\|\,|\cdot|^{a+1}g_{B}^{z}\|_{1}\,\|h^{z}\|_{1}+B\,\|\,|\cdot|g_{B}^{z}\|_{1}\,\|\,|\cdot|^{a}h^{z}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 60.0pt+\|\,|\cdot|^{a}g_{B}^{z}\|_{1}\;\|\nabla h^{z}\|_{1}+\|g_{B}^{z}\|_{1}\;\|\,|\cdot|^{a}\nabla h^{z}\|_{1}\bigr].

(4.43)

When we combine (
4.43
), the first estimates in (
4.33
) and (
4.34
), the bound in (
4.42
) and Lemma
4.4
, we see that

\displaystyle\|\,|\cdot|^{a}(\nabla g_{B}^{z}-\nabla g_{0}^{z})\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{a}\,B^{2}\,f(t,\omega)^{\frac{5}{2}+\frac{a}{2}}\bigl[1+Bf(t,\omega)\bigr].

An application of the assumption
B^{2}f(t,\omega)^{2}\leqslant\delta_{a}
proves the second bound in (
4.34
). Finally, the triangle inequality, the second bound in (
4.34
), and Lemma
4.4
show

\displaystyle\|\,|\cdot|^{a}\nabla g_{B}^{z}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{a}\,f(t,\omega)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{a}{2}}\Bigl[1+\frac{|\omega|+|t+\mu|}{|\omega|+(t+\mu)_{-}}\Bigr]\bigl[1+B^{2}f(t,\omega)^{2}\bigr].

Another application of
B^{2}f(t,\omega)^{2}\leqslant\delta_{a}
on the right side proves the second bound in (
4.33
).
∎

4.4.2
A representation formula for
L_{T,B}
and an outlook on the quadratic terms

In this subsection we compute the terms in (
3.20
) involving the linear operator
L_{T,B}
defined in (
3.15
). Our starting point is the representation formula for
L_{T,B}
in
[
17
, Lemma 11]
,
which expresses the operator explicitly in terms of the relative and the center-of-mass coordinate.

Lemma 4.6
.

The operator
L_{T,B}\colon{L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}\rightarrow{L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
in (
3.15
) acts as

\displaystyle(L_{T,B}\alpha)(X,r)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;k_{T,B}(Z,r,s)\;(\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{X})\alpha)(X,s)

with

\displaystyle k_{T,B}(Z,r,s)

\displaystyle:=\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}k_{T,B}^{n}(Z,r-s)\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\mathbf{B}\cdot(r\wedge s)}

(4.44)

and

\displaystyle k_{T,B}^{n}(Z,r)

\displaystyle:=g_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigl(Z-\frac{r}{2}\bigr)\;g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigl(Z+\frac{r}{2}\bigr).

(4.45)

We analyze the operator
L_{T,B}
in three steps. In the first two steps we introduce two operators of increasing simplicity in their dependence on
B
:

\displaystyle L_{T,B}=(L_{T,B}-\widetilde{L}_{T,B})+(\widetilde{L}_{T,B}-M_{T,B})+M_{T,B},

(4.46)

where
\widetilde{L}_{T,B}
and
M_{T,B}
are defined below in (
4.47
) and (
4.71
), respectively. To obtain
\widetilde{L}_{T,B}
we replace the functions
g_{B}^{z}
in the definition of
L_{T,B}
by
g_{0}^{z}
. Moreover,
M_{T,B}
is obtained from
\widetilde{L}_{T,B}
when we replace
k_{T,B}
by
k_{T,0}
, i.e., when we additionally replace the magnetic phase
\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\mathbf{B}\cdot(r\wedge s)}
by
1
. In Section
4.4.3
we prove that the terms in the brackets in (
4.46
) are small in a suitable sense. The third step consists of a careful analysis of the operator
M_{T,B}
, which takes place in Section
4.4.4
. There, we expand the operator
\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{X})
in powers of
Z\cdot\Pi_{X}
up to second order and extract the quadratic terms of the Ginzburg–Landau functional in (
1.17
) as well as a term that cancels the last term on the left side of (
3.20
). In Section
4.4.5
we summarize our findings.

We remark that the operator
\widetilde{L}_{T,B}
is called
M_{T,B}
in
[
17
]
and that
M_{T,B}
is called
N_{T,B}
. The reason why we did not follow the notation in
[
17
]
is that
N_{T,B}
is reserved for the nonlinear term in the present paper. We note that our decomposition of
L_{T,B}
in (
4.46
) already appeared in
[
17
]
. Parts of our analysis follow the analysis of
L_{T,B}
in Section 4 and Section 5 in that reference. However, we additionally need
{H^{1}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
-norm bounds that are not provided in
[
17
]
. It should also be noted that
L_{T,B}
acts on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{6})
in
[
17
]
, while it acts on
{L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
in our case.

4.4.3
Approximation of
L_{T,B}

4.4.3.1
The operator
\widetilde{L}_{T,B}
.

The operator
\widetilde{L}_{T,B}
is defined by

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\alpha(X,r)

\displaystyle:=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;\widetilde{k}_{T,B}(Z,r,s)\;(\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{X})\alpha)(X,s)

(4.47)

with

\displaystyle\widetilde{k}_{T,B}(Z,r,s):=\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}k_{T,0}^{n}(Z,r-s)\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\mathbf{B}\cdot(r\wedge s)}

(4.48)

and
k_{T,0}^{n}
in (
4.45
). In the following proposition we provide an estimate that allows us to replace
L_{T,B}
by
\widetilde{L}_{T,B}
in our computations.

Proposition 4.7
.

For any
T_{0}>0
there is
B_{0}>0
such that for any
0<B\leqslant B_{0}
, any
T\geqslant T_{0}
and whenever
|\cdot|^{k}V\alpha_{*}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
for
k\in\{0,1\}
,
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})
, and
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
as in (
3.1
), we have

\displaystyle\|L_{T,B}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta\|_{H^{1}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}^{2}\leqslant C\;B^{5}\;\bigl(\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\,|\cdot|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}.

Remark 4.8
.

For the proof of Theorem
3.5
we only need a bound for
\langle\Delta,(L_{T,B}-\widetilde{L}_{T,B})\Delta\rangle
, which is easier to obtain. This bound follows directly from Proposition
4.7
, Lemma
4.1
and an application of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. The more general bound in Proposition
4.7
is needed in the proof of Proposition
3.2
.

Before we start with the proof of Proposition
4.7
let
a\geqslant 0
and introduce the functions

\displaystyle F_{T,B}^{a}

\displaystyle:=\smash{\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}}\;\bigl(|\cdot|^{a}\,|g_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}-g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)*|g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|+|g_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}-g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*\bigl(|\cdot|^{a}\,|g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt+\bigl(|\cdot|^{a}\,|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)*|g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}-g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*\bigl(|\cdot|^{a}\,|g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}-g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)

(4.49)

and

\displaystyle G_{T,B}

\displaystyle:=\smash{\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}}\;|\nabla g_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}-\nabla g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|+|g_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}-g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|\nabla g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|

\displaystyle\hskip 80.0pt+|\nabla g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}-g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|\nabla g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}-\nabla g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|

(4.50)

with the Matsubara frequencies
\omega_{n}
in (
3.13
) and the resolvent kernel
g_{B}^{z}
in (
4.27
). We claim that for any
a\geqslant 0
there is a constant
B_{0}>0
such that for
0\leqslant B\leqslant B_{0}
we have

\displaystyle\|F_{T,B}^{a}\|_{1}+\|G_{T,B}\|_{1}\leqslant C_{a}B^{2}.

(4.51)

To prove (
4.51
) we note that the function
f(t,\omega)
in (
4.29
) satisfies

\displaystyle f(0,\omega_{n})

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;(T^{-1}+T^{-2})\;|2n+1|^{-1}

(4.52)

and that

\displaystyle\frac{|\omega_{n}|+|\mu|}{|\omega_{n}|+\mu_{-}}\leqslant C\;(1+T^{-1}).

(4.53)

Since
T\geqslant T_{0}>0
, Lemmas
4.4
and
4.5
prove (
4.51
).

Proof of Proposition
4.7
.

We write

\displaystyle\|L_{T,B}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta\|_{H^{1}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-120.0pt=\|L_{T,B}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta\|_{2}^{2}+\|\Pi_{X}(L_{T,B}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}+\|\widetilde{\pi}_{r}(L_{T,B}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}

(4.54)

and claim that

\displaystyle\|L_{T,B}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\;\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\;\|F_{T,B}^{0}*|V\alpha_{*}|\,\|_{2}^{2}.

(4.55)

If this is true, Young’s inequality, (
2.5
), and (
4.51
) prove

\displaystyle\|L_{T,B}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant CB^{5}\;\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}.

To see that (
4.55
) holds, we expand the squared modulus in the Hilbert–Schmidt norm and obtain

\displaystyle\|L_{T,B}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}Z^{\prime}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}\;|V\alpha_{*}(s)|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\hskip 120.0pt\times|k_{T,B}(Z,r,s)-\widetilde{k}_{T,B}(Z,r,s)|

\displaystyle\hskip 120.0pt\times|k_{T,B}(Z^{\prime},r,s^{\prime})-\widetilde{k}_{T,B}(Z^{\prime},r,s^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt\times\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{X})\Psi(X)|\;|\cos(Z^{\prime}\cdot\Pi_{X})\Psi(X)|.

(4.56)

The operator
\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{X})
is bounded by
1
and we have

\displaystyle\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\cos(Z\cdot\Pi)\Psi(X)|\;|\cos(Z^{\prime}\cdot\Pi)\Psi(X)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}.

(4.57)

By (
4.56
), this implies

\displaystyle\|L_{T,B}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-80.0pt\leqslant 4\;\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\;\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\,\Bigl|\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;|k_{T,B}(Z,r,s)-\widetilde{k}_{T,B}(Z,r,s)|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s)|\Bigr|^{2},

(4.58)

where the integrand is bounded by

\displaystyle|k_{T,B}(Z,r,s)-\widetilde{k}_{T,B}(Z,r,s)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2}{\beta}\,\smash{\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}}\,\bigl[|g_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}-g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigl(Z-\frac{r}{2}\bigr)\;|g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigl(Z+\frac{r}{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.0pt+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigl(Z-\frac{r}{2}\bigr)\;|g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}-g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigl(Z+\frac{r}{2}\bigr)\bigr].

(4.59)

For
a\geqslant 0
, we have the estimate

\displaystyle|Z|^{a}

\displaystyle\leqslant\bigl|Z+\frac{r}{2}\bigr|^{a}+\bigl|Z-\frac{r}{2}\bigr|^{a}.

(4.60)

This, (
4.59
), and the fact that
g_{B}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}
is an even function imply

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;|Z|^{a}\;|k_{T,B}(Z,r,s)-\widetilde{k}_{T,B}(Z,r,s)|\leqslant F_{T,B}^{a}(r-s),

(4.61)

where
F_{T,B}^{a}
is the function in (
4.49
). We apply the case
a=0
to (
4.58
) and read off (
4.55
).

We claim that the second term on the right side of (
4.54
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\|\Pi_{X}(L_{T,B}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant CB^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}\;\|(F_{T,B}^{0}+F_{T,B}^{1})*|V\alpha_{*}|\,\|_{2}^{2}.

(4.62)

If this is true, Young’s inequality and (
4.51
) show the claimed bound for this term. To prove (
4.62
), we use (
4.56
) with
\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{X})
replaced by
\Pi_{X}\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{X})
, that is, we need to replace (
4.57
) by

\displaystyle\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\Pi\cos(Z\cdot\Pi)\Psi(X)|\;|\Pi\cos(Z^{\prime}\cdot\Pi)\Psi(X)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\Pi\cos(Z\cdot\Pi)\Psi\|_{2}\;\|\Pi\cos(Z^{\prime}\cdot\Pi)\Psi\|_{2}.

In Lemma
5.12
in Section
5
we prove intertwining relations for
\cos(Z\cdot\Pi)
with various magnetic momenta. The intertwining relation (
5.40
) therein and (
2.5
) show

\displaystyle\|\Pi\cos(Z\cdot\Pi)\Psi\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\Pi\Psi\|_{2}+2B|Z|\;\|\Psi\|_{2}\leqslant C\,B\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}\;(1+|Z|),

(4.63)

which yields

\displaystyle\|\Pi_{X}(L_{T,B}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,B^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-80.0pt\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\,\Bigl|\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;(1+|Z|)\;|k_{T,B}(Z,r,s)-\widetilde{k}_{T,B}(Z,r,s)|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s)|\Bigr|^{2}.

(4.64)

We apply the cases
a=0
and
a=1
of (
4.61
) to this and obtain (
4.62
).

Concerning the third term on the right side of (
4.54
) we claim that

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\pi}_{r}(L_{T,B}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\;\bigl\|\bigl(G_{T,B}+F_{T,B}^{1}\bigr)*|V\alpha_{*}|+F_{T,B}^{0}*|\cdot|\,|V\alpha_{*}|\,\bigr\|_{2}^{2}.

(4.65)

If this is true, Young’s inequality, (
4.51
), and (
2.5
) show the relevant bound for this term.
To prove (
4.65
), we estimate

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\pi}_{r}(L_{T,B}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-90.0pt\leqslant 4\;\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\,\Bigl|\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;|\widetilde{\pi}_{r}k_{T,B}(Z,r,s)-\widetilde{\pi}_{r}\widetilde{k}_{T,B}(Z,r,s)|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s)|\Bigr|^{2}.

(4.66)

Using
\frac{1}{4}|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|\leqslant B(|r-s|+|s|)
we see that the integrand on the right side is bounded by

\displaystyle|\widetilde{\pi}_{r}k_{T,B}(Z,r,s)-\widetilde{\pi}_{r}\widetilde{k}_{T,B}(Z,r,s)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big[|\nabla_{r}k_{T,B}^{n}(Z,r-s)-\nabla_{r}k_{T,0}^{n}(Z,r-s)|

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt+B|r-s|\;|k_{T,B}^{n}(Z,r-s)-k_{T,0}^{n}(Z,r-s)|

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt+B|s|\;|k_{T,B}^{n}(Z,r-s)-k_{T,0}^{n}(Z,r-s)|\Big].

(4.67)

We also have

\displaystyle|\nabla_{r}k_{T,B}^{n}(Z,r)-\nabla_{r}k_{T,0}^{n}(Z,r)|

\displaystyle\leqslant|\nabla g_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}-\nabla g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigl(Z+\frac{r}{2}\bigr)\;|g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigl(Z-\frac{r}{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt+|g_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}-g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigl(Z+\frac{r}{2}\bigr)\;|\nabla g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigl(Z-\frac{r}{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt+|\nabla g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigl(Z+\frac{r}{2}\bigr)\;|g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}-g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigl(Z-\frac{r}{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigl(Z+\frac{r}{2}\bigr)|\nabla g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}-\nabla g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigl(Z-\frac{r}{2}\bigr).

Since
g_{B}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}
is an even function this implies

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;|\nabla k_{T,B}^{n}(Z,r)-\nabla k_{T,0}^{n}(Z,r)|\leqslant G_{T,B}(r).

(4.68)

Moreover, the estimate

\displaystyle|r-s|

\displaystyle=\bigl|\frac{r-s}{2}+Z+\frac{r-s}{2}-Z\bigr|\leqslant\bigl|Z-\frac{r-s}{2}\bigr|+\bigl|Z+\frac{r-s}{2}\bigr|

(4.69)

shows that
|r-s|\,F_{T,B}^{0}(r-s)\leqslant F_{T,B}^{1}(r-s)
. We conclude the estimate

\displaystyle\smash{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}\mathrm{d}Z\;|\widetilde{\pi}_{r}k_{T,B}(Z,r,s)-\widetilde{\pi}_{r}\widetilde{k}_{T,B}(Z,r,s)|

\displaystyle\hskip-80.0pt\leqslant G_{T,B}(r-s)+B\;F_{T,B}^{1}(r-s)+B\;F_{T,B}^{0}(r-s)\;|s|.

(4.70)

From (
4.70
) we deduce (
4.65
), which proves the claim.
∎

4.4.3.2
The operator
M_{T,B}
.

The operator
M_{T,B}
is defined by

\displaystyle M_{T,B}\alpha(X,r):=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;k_{T}(Z,r-s)\;(\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{X})\alpha)(X,s),

(4.71)

where
k_{T}(Z,r):=k_{T,0}(Z,r,0)
with
k_{T,0}
in (
4.44
). The following proposition allows us to replace
\widetilde{L}_{T,B}
by
M_{T,B}
in our computations.

Proposition 4.9
.

For any
T_{0}>0
there is
B_{0}>0
such that for any
0<B\leqslant B_{0}
, any
T\geqslant T_{0}
, and whenever
|\cdot|^{k}V\alpha_{*}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
for
k\in\{0,1\}
,
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})
, and
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
as in (
3.1
), we have

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta-M_{T,B}\Delta\|_{H^{1}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;B^{3}\;\bigl(\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\,|\cdot|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}.

(4.72)

If instead
|\cdot|^{2}V\alpha_{*}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
then

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta,\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta-M_{T,B}\Delta\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;B^{3}\;\|\,|\cdot|^{2}V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}.

(4.73)

Remark 4.10
.

The
{H^{1}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
-norm bound in (
4.72
) is needed for the proof of Proposition
3.2
and the quadratic form bound in (
4.73
) is needed for the proof of Theorem
3.5
. We highlight that the bound in (
4.72
) is insufficient as far as the proof of Theorem
3.5
is concerned. More precisely, if we apply Cauchy–Schwarz to the left side of (
4.73
), and use (
4.72
) as well as the Lemma
4.1
to estimate
\|\Delta\|_{2}
we obtain a bound of the order
B^{2}
only. This is not good enough because
B^{2}
is the order of the Ginzburg–Landau energy.

To obtain the desired quality for the quadratic form bound (
4.73
), we exploit the fact that
V\alpha_{*}
is real-valued, which allows us to replace the magnetic phase factor
\exp(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\mathbf{B}(r\wedge s))
in
\widetilde{k}_{T,B}
in (
4.48
) by
\cos(\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{B}(r\wedge s))
. This improves the error estimate by an additional factor of
B
.

Before we start with the proof of Proposition
4.9
, let
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and define the functions

\displaystyle F_{T}^{a}:=\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{b=0}^{a}\binom{a}{b}\;\bigl(|\cdot|^{b}\,|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)*\bigl(|\cdot|^{a-b}\,|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)

(4.74)

and

\displaystyle G_{T}

\displaystyle:=\smash{\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}}\bigl(|\cdot|\,|\nabla g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|+|\nabla g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*\bigl(|\cdot|\,|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 110.0pt+\bigl(|\cdot|\,|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)*|\nabla g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*\bigl(|\cdot|\,|\nabla g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr).

(4.75)

For
T\geqslant T_{0}>0
and
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, by Lemma
4.4
, (
4.52
), and (
4.53
), we have

\displaystyle\|F_{T}^{a}\|_{1}+\|G_{T}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{a}.

(4.76)

Proof of Proposition
4.9
.

We start with the proof of (
4.72
), which is similar to the proof of Proposition
4.7
. We claim that

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta-M_{T,B}\Delta\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\;B^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\;\|F_{T}^{1}*|\cdot|\,|V\alpha_{*}|\,\|_{2}^{2}.

(4.77)

If this is true Young’s inequality, (
2.5
), and (
4.76
) prove the claimed bound for this term.
To see that (
4.77
) holds, we argue as in (
4.56
)-(
4.58
) and find

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta-M_{T,B}\Delta\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-60.0pt\leqslant 4\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\,\Bigl|\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;\bigl|k_{T,0}^{n}(Z,r-s)\bigl[\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\mathbf{B}\cdot(r\wedge s)}-1\bigr]\bigr|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s)|\Bigr|^{2}.

Since
|r\wedge s|\leqslant|r-s|\,|s|
, we have
|\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\mathbf{B}\cdot(r\wedge s)}-1|\leqslant B\,|r-s|\,|s|
as well as

\displaystyle|k_{T,0}^{n}(Z,r-s)|\bigl|\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\mathbf{B}\cdot(r\wedge s)}-1\bigr|

\displaystyle\leqslant B\;|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigl(Z-\frac{r-s}{2}\bigr)\;|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigl(Z+\frac{r-s}{2}\bigr)\;|r-s|\;|s|.

In combination with the estimate for
|r-s|
in (
4.69
) and the bound for
|Z|^{a}
in (
4.60
), this proves

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;|Z|^{a}\,|k_{T,0}^{n}(Z,r-s)|\,\bigl|\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\mathbf{B}\cdot(r\wedge s)}-1\bigr|

\displaystyle\leqslant B\;F_{T}^{a+1}(r-s)\;|s|

(4.78)

for
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. The case
a=0
implies (
4.77
). A computation similar to the one leading to (
4.64
) shows

\displaystyle\|\Pi_{X}(\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta-M_{T,B}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,B^{4}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}\;\|(F_{T}^{1}+F_{T}^{2})*|\cdot|\,|V\alpha_{*}|\,\|_{2}^{2}.

To obtain the result we also used (
4.63
) and (
4.78
). We apply Young’s inequality and use (
4.76
) to prove the claimed bound for this term.
Finally, a computation similar to the one that leads to (
4.66
) shows

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\pi}_{r}(\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta-M_{T,B}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-80.0pt\leqslant 4\,\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\,\Bigl|\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigl|\widetilde{\pi}_{r}k_{T,0}^{n}(Z,r-s)\bigl[\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\mathbf{B}(r\wedge s)}-1\bigr]\bigr|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s)|\Bigr|^{2}.

We argue as in the proof of (
4.70
) to see that

\displaystyle\smash{\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z}\;\bigl|\widetilde{\pi}_{r}k_{T,0}^{n}(Z,r-s)\bigl[\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\mathbf{B}(r\wedge s)}-1\bigr]\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip-100.0pt\leqslant C\,B\;\bigl(G_{T}(r-s)\;|s|+F_{T}^{1}(r-s)+F_{T}^{0}(r-s)\;|s|\bigr).

With the help of Young’s inequality and (
4.76
), these considerations prove (
4.72
).

It remains to prove (
4.73
). The term we need to estimate reads

\displaystyle\langle\Delta,\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta-M_{T,B}\Delta\rangle

\displaystyle=4\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s\;\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\mathbf{B}\cdot(r\wedge s)}-1\bigr)V\alpha_{*}(r)V\alpha_{*}(s)

\displaystyle\hskip-20.0pt\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}k_{T,0}^{n}(Z,r-s)\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\;\overline{\Psi(X)}\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{X})\Psi(X).

(4.79)

Except for the factor
\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\mathbf{B}(r\wedge s)}
, the right side is symmetric under the exchange of the coordinates
r
and
s
. The exponential factor acquires a minus sign in the exponent when this transformation is applied. When we add the right side of (
4.79
) and the same term with the roles of
r
and
s
interchanged, we get

\displaystyle\langle\Delta,\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta-M_{T,B}\Delta\rangle

\displaystyle=-8\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s\;\sin^{2}\bigl(\frac{1}{8}\,\mathbf{B}\cdot(r\wedge s)\bigr)\;V\alpha_{*}(r)V\alpha_{*}(s)

\displaystyle\hskip-20.0pt\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}k_{T,0}^{n}(Z,r-s)\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\;\overline{\Psi(X)}\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{X})\Psi(X).

(4.80)

To obtain (
4.80
) we also used
\cos(x)-1=-2\sin^{2}(\frac{x}{2})
. The operator norm of
\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{X})
is bounded by
1
and we have
\sin^{2}(\frac{1}{8}\mathbf{B}\cdot(r\wedge s))\leqslant\frac{1}{8}B^{2}|r|^{2}|s|^{2}
. Therefore, (
4.80
) proves

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta,\widetilde{L}_{T,B}\Delta-M_{T,B}\Delta\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant B^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\;\bigl\||\cdot|^{2}|V\alpha_{*}|\;\bigl(|\cdot|^{2}|V\alpha_{*}|*F_{T}^{0}\bigr)\bigr\|_{1}.

(4.81)

Finally, we use Young’s inequality, (
2.5
), and (
4.76
) and obtain (
4.73
). This proves Proposition
4.9
.
∎

4.4.4
Analysis of
M_{T,B}
and calculation of the quadratic terms

We decompose
M_{T,B}=M_{T}^{(1)}+M_{T,B}^{(2)}+M_{T,B}^{(3)}
, where

\displaystyle M_{T}^{(1)}\alpha(X,r)

\displaystyle:=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;k_{T}(Z,r-s)\;\alpha(X,s),

(4.82)

\displaystyle M_{T,B}^{(2)}\alpha(X,r)

\displaystyle:=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;k_{T}(Z,r-s)\;\bigl(-\frac{1}{2}\bigr)(Z\cdot\Pi_{X})^{2}\;\alpha(X,s),

(4.83)

\displaystyle M_{T,B}^{(3)}\alpha(X,r)

\displaystyle:=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;k_{T}(Z,r-s)\;\mathcal{R}(Z\cdot\Pi_{X})\;\alpha(X,s),

(4.84)

and
\mathcal{R}(x)=\cos(x)-1+\frac{1}{2}x^{2}
.

4.4.4.1
The operator
M_{T}^{(1)}
.

The expression
\langle\Delta,M_{T}^{(1)}\Delta\rangle
contains a term that cancels the last term on the left side of (
3.20
) as well as the quadratic term without magnetic gradient in the Ginzburg–Landau functional in (
1.17
). The following result allows us to extract these terms. We recall that
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}=-2V\alpha_{*}\Psi
.

Proposition 4.11
.

Assume that
V\alpha_{*}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and let
\Psi\in L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
and
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
as in (
3.1
).

(a)

We have
M_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(1)}\Delta(X,r)=-2\,\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)
.

(b)

For any
T_{0}>0
there is a constant
c>0
such that for
T_{0}\leqslant T\leqslant T_{\mathrm{c}}
we have

\displaystyle\langle\Delta,M_{T}^{(1)}\Delta-M_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(1)}\Delta\rangle\geqslant c\,\frac{T_{\mathrm{c}}-T}{T_{\mathrm{c}}}\;\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}.

(c)

Given
D\in\mathbb{R}
there is
B_{0}>0
such that for
0<B\leqslant B_{0}
, and
T=T_{\mathrm{c}}(1-DB)
we have

\displaystyle\langle\Delta,M_{T}^{(1)}\Delta-M_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(1)}\Delta\rangle=4\;DB\;\Lambda_{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}+R(\Delta)

with the coefficient
\Lambda_{2}
in (
3.23
), and

\displaystyle|R(\Delta)|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;B^{2}\;\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}.

(d)

Assume additionally that
|\cdot|V\alpha_{*}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. There is
B_{0}>0
such that for any
0<B\leqslant B_{0}
, any
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})
, and any
T\geqslant T_{0}>0
we have

\displaystyle\|M_{T}^{(1)}\Delta-M_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(1)}\Delta\|_{{H^{1}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,B\,|T-T_{\mathrm{c}}|^{2}\,\bigl(\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\,|\cdot|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}.

Remark 4.12
.

The above bound for the remainder term implies

|R(\Delta)|\leqslant C\;B^{3}\;\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}.

Part (b) in the Proposition is needed for the proof of Proposition
3.6
. Part (d) is needed in the proof of Proposition
3.2
.

Before we give the proof of the above proposition, we introduce the function

\displaystyle F_{T,T_{\mathrm{c}}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}|2n+1|\bigl[|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T}}|*|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T}}|+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}|\bigr],

(4.85)

where we indicated the
T
-dependence of the Matusubara frequencies in (
3.13
) because different temperatures appear in the formula. As long as
T\geqslant T_{0}>0
, Lemma
4.4
and (
4.52
) imply the bound

\|F_{T,T_{\mathrm{c}}}\|_{1}\leqslant C.

(4.86)

Proof of Proposition
4.11
.

We start with the proof of part (a). First of all, we recall that
k_{T}(Z,r)=k_{T,0}(Z,r,0)
with
k_{T,B}(Z,r,s)
in (
4.44
). In Fourier space the convolution operator
g_{0}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(x-y)
equals multiplication with
(\pm\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\mu-k^{2})^{-1}
. This allows us to write

\displaystyle k_{T}(Z,r)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k\cdot(Z-\frac{r}{2})}}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\mu-k^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\ell\cdot(Z+\frac{r}{2})}}{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\mu-\ell^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot q}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}r\cdot p}\;\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\mu-(p+\frac{q}{2})^{2}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mu+(p-\frac{q}{2})^{2}},

where we applied the change of variables
q=k+\ell
and
p=\frac{k-\ell}{2}
. We use the partial fraction expansion

\displaystyle\frac{1}{E+E^{\prime}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-E}-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+E^{\prime}}\Bigr)=\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-E}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+E^{\prime}}

and the representation formula of the hyperbolic tangent in (
3.12
) to see that

k_{T}(Z,r)=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot q}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}r\cdot p}\;L_{T}\bigl(p+\frac{q}{2},p-\frac{q}{2}\bigr),

(4.87)

where

\displaystyle L_{T}(p,q):=\frac{\tanh(\frac{\beta}{2}(p^{2}-\mu))+\tanh(\frac{\beta}{2}(q^{2}-\mu))}{p^{2}-\mu+q^{2}-\mu}.

(4.88)

In particular,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;k_{T}(Z,r)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}r\cdot p}L_{T}(p,p)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}r\cdot p}K_{T}(p)^{-1}

(4.89)

with
K_{T}(p)
in (
1.14
). Therefore, we have

\displaystyle M_{T}^{(1)}\Delta(X,r)=K_{T}^{-1}V\alpha_{*}(r)\,\Psi(X),

which together with
K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}\alpha_{*}=V\alpha_{*}
proves part (a).
To prove part (b), we use (
4.89
) to write

\displaystyle\langle\Delta,M_{T}^{(1)}\Delta-M_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(1)}\Delta\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;\bigl[K_{T}(p)^{-1}-K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(p)^{-1}\bigr]\,|(-2)V\alpha_{*}(p)|^{2}\,\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}.

(4.90)

With the help of the first order Taylor expansion

\displaystyle K_{T}(p)^{-1}-K_{T_{c}}(p)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{T}^{T_{c}}\mathrm{d}T^{\prime}\;\frac{1}{(T^{\prime})^{2}}\frac{1}{\cosh^{2}(\frac{p^{2}-\mu}{2T^{\prime}})}

(4.91)

we see that

\displaystyle K_{T}(p)^{-1}-K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(p)^{-1}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\frac{T_{\mathrm{c}}-T}{T_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}\,\frac{1}{\cosh^{2}(\frac{p^{2}-\mu}{2T_{0}})}

holds for
T_{0}\leqslant T\leqslant T_{\mathrm{c}}
. This and (
4.90
) prove part (b).

To prove part (c), we expand (
4.91
) to second order in
T-T_{c}
and find

\displaystyle\Bigl|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;[K_{T}(p)^{-1}-K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(p)^{-1}]\;|(-2)\widehat{V\alpha_{*}}(p)|^{2}-4\,\Lambda_{2}\;\frac{T_{\mathrm{c}}-T}{T_{\mathrm{c}}}\Bigr|\leqslant C\,|T-T_{\mathrm{c}}|^{2}\;\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}

with
\Lambda_{2}
in (
3.23
). By (
4.90
), this proves part (c).

It remains to prove part (d). We use the resolvent identity to see that

\displaystyle g_{0}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T}}-g_{0}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}=\mp\mathrm{i}(\omega_{n}^{T}-\omega_{n}^{T_{\mathrm{c}}})\;g_{0}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T}}*g_{0}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}.

(4.92)

Using (
4.92
), it is straightforward to see that

\displaystyle\|M_{T}^{(1)}\Delta-M_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(1)}\Delta\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,|T-T_{\mathrm{c}}|^{2}\,\|F_{T,T_{\mathrm{c}}}\|_{1}\,\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\,\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}

holds with
F_{T,T_{\mathrm{c}}}
in (
4.85
). In combination with (
4.86
) this proves the claimed bound for this term. The estimates for the terms
\|\widetilde{\pi}_{r}(M_{T}^{(1)}\Delta-M_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(1)}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}
and
\|\Pi_{X}(M_{T}^{(1)}\Delta-M_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(1)}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}
are proved similarly. We omit the details.
∎

4.4.4.2
The operator
M_{T,B}^{(2)}
.

The term
\langle\Delta,M_{T,B}^{(2)}\Delta\rangle
with
M_{T,B}^{(2)}
in (
4.83
) contains the kinetic term in the Ginzburg–Landau functional in (
1.17
). The following proposition allows us to compare the two.

Proposition 4.13
.

Assume that the function
V\alpha_{*}
is radial and belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. For any
B>0
,
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})
, and
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
as in (
3.1
), we have

\displaystyle\langle\Delta,M_{T_{\mathrm{c}},B}^{(2)}\Delta\rangle=-4\;\Lambda_{0}\;\|\Pi\Psi\|_{2}^{2}

(4.93)

with
\Lambda_{0}
in (
3.22
). Moreover, for any
T\geqslant T_{0}>0
we have

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta,M_{T,B}^{(2)}\Delta-M_{T_{\mathrm{c}},B}^{(2)}\Delta\rangle|\leqslant C\;B^{2}\;|T-T_{\mathrm{c}}|\;\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}.

(4.94)

Before we give the proof of Proposition
4.13
, let us introduce the function

\displaystyle F_{T,T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{a}

\displaystyle:=\smash{\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\\
a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}=a\end{subarray}}}|2n+1|\bigl[\bigl(|\cdot|^{a_{1}}\,|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T}}|\bigr)*\bigl(|\cdot|^{a_{2}}\,|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}|\bigr)*\bigl(|\cdot|^{a_{3}}\,|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T}}|\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 110.0pt+\bigl(|\cdot|^{a_{1}}\,|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}|\bigr)*\bigl(|\cdot|^{a_{2}}\,|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T}}|\bigr)*\bigl(|\cdot|^{a_{3}}\,|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}|\bigr)\bigr],

(4.95)

where
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and where we indicated the
T
-dependence of the Matusubara frequencies in (
3.13
) because different temperatures appear in the formula.
As long as
T\geqslant T_{0}>0
, Lemma
4.4
and (
4.52
) imply the bound
\|F_{T,T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{a}\|_{1}\leqslant C_{a}
.

Proof of Proposition
4.13
.

We have

\displaystyle\langle\Delta,M_{T_{\mathrm{c}},B}^{(2)}\Delta\rangle

\displaystyle=-2\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s\;V\alpha_{*}(r)V\alpha_{*}(s)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;k_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(Z,r-s)\;\langle\Psi,(Z\cdot\Pi_{X})^{2}\Psi\rangle

(4.96)

and

\displaystyle\langle\Psi,(Z\cdot\Pi)^{2}\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{3}Z_{i}Z_{j}\;\langle\Pi^{(i)}\Psi,\Pi^{(j)}\Psi\rangle.

The integration over
Z
in (
4.96
) defines a
3\times 3
matrix with matrix elements

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\,k_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(Z,r)\;Z_{i}Z_{j}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}Z\cdot q}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}p\cdot r}\;L_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}\bigl(p+\frac{q}{2},p-\frac{q}{2}\bigr)Z_{i}Z_{j},

which we have written in terms of the Fourier representation of
k_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(Z,r)
in (
4.87
). We use
Z_{i}Z_{j}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}Z\cdot q}=-\partial_{q_{i}}\partial_{q_{j}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}Z\cdot q}
, integrate by parts twice, and find

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}Z\cdot q}\;L_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}\bigl(p+\frac{q}{2},p-\frac{q}{2}\bigr)\;Z_{i}Z_{j}

\displaystyle=-\Bigl[\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{j}}L_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}\big(p+\frac{q}{2},p-\frac{q}{2}\bigr)\Bigr]_{q=0}.

A tedious but straightforward computation shows that the right side of the above equation can be written in terms of the functions
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
in (
3.21
) as

\displaystyle-\Bigl[\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{j}}L_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}\bigl(p+\frac{q}{2},p-\frac{q}{2}\bigr)\Bigr]_{q=0}=\frac{\beta_{c}^{2}}{2}\bigl[g_{1}(\beta_{c}(p^{2}-\mu))\delta_{ij}+2\beta_{c}\;g_{2}(\beta_{c}(p^{2}-\mu))\;p_{i}p_{j}\bigr],

and hence

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;k_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(Z,r)\,Z_{i}Z_{j}=\frac{\beta_{c}^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}p\cdot r}\bigl[g_{1}(\beta_{c}(p^{2}-\mu))\delta_{ij}+2\beta_{c}\;g_{2}(\beta_{c}(p^{2}-\mu))\;p_{i}p_{j}\bigr].

Let us denote the term in the bracket on the right side by
A_{ij}(p)
. When we insert the above identity into (
4.96
) we find

\langle\Delta,M_{T_{\mathrm{c}},B}^{(2)}\Delta\rangle=-\frac{\beta_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}}{4}\sum_{i,j=1}^{3}\langle\Pi^{(i)}\Psi,\Pi^{(j)}\Psi\rangle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;|(-2)\widehat{V\alpha_{*}}(p)|^{2}A_{ij}(p).

(4.97)

We use that
V\alpha_{*}
is a radial function to see that the integral of the term proportional to
p_{i}p_{j}
equals zero unless
i=j
. Since the angular average of
p_{i}^{2}
equals
\frac{1}{3}p^{2}
this proves (
4.93
).

It remains to prove (
4.94
). To this end, we estimate

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta,M_{T,B}^{(2)}\Delta-M_{T_{\mathrm{c}},B}^{(2)}\Delta\rangle|

\displaystyle\hskip-100.0pt\leqslant 2\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}Z\;|V\alpha_{*}(r)|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s)|\;|k_{T}(Z,r-s)-k_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(Z,r-s)|

\displaystyle\hskip 160.0pt\times|\langle\Psi,(Z\cdot\Pi)^{2}\Psi\rangle|.

(4.98)

For general operators
A,B,C
, we have
|A+B+C|^{2}\leqslant 3(|A|^{2}+|B|^{2}+|C|^{2})
.
This implies

\displaystyle(Z\cdot\Pi)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 3\;\bigl(Z_{1}^{2}\;(\Pi^{(1)})^{2}+Z_{2}^{2}\;(\Pi^{(2)})^{2}+Z_{3}^{2}\;(\Pi^{(3)})^{2}\bigr)\leqslant 3\;Z^{2}\;\Pi^{2},

(4.99)

and, in particular,

\displaystyle|\langle\Psi,(Z\cdot\Pi)^{2}\Psi\rangle|\leqslant 3\,|Z|^{2}\,\|\Pi\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant 3\,B^{2}\,|Z|^{2}\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}.

(4.100)

Moreover, (
4.92
)
and the estimate for
|Z|^{2}
in (
4.60
) show

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;|Z|^{2}\,|k_{T}(Z,r)-k_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(Z,r)|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,|T-T_{\mathrm{c}}|\;F_{T,T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{2}(r)

(4.101)

with
F_{T,T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{2}
in (
4.95
).
With the help of (
4.98
), (
4.100
), and (
4.101
), we deduce

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta,M_{T,B}^{(2)}\Delta-M_{T_{\mathrm{c}},B}^{(2)}\Delta\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,B^{2}\;|T-T_{\mathrm{c}}|\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}\;\bigl\||V\alpha_{*}|\;\bigl(|V\alpha_{*}|*F_{T,T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{2}\bigr)\bigr\|_{1}.

An application of Young’s inequality completes the proof.
∎

4.4.4.3
The operator
M_{T,B}^{(3)}
.

The term
\langle\Delta,M_{T,B}^{(3)}\Delta\rangle
with
M_{T,B}^{(3)}
in (
4.84
) is the remainder of our expansion of
\langle\Delta,M_{T,B}\Delta\rangle
in powers of
B
. In contrast to the previous estimates, we need the
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
-norm of
\Psi
to control its size.

Proposition 4.14
.

For any
T_{0}>0
there is
B_{0}>0
such that for any
0<B\leqslant B_{0}
, any
T\geqslant T_{0}
, and whenever
V\alpha_{*}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
, and
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
as in (
3.1
), we have

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta,M_{T,B}^{(3)}\Delta\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;B^{3}\;\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})}^{2}.

Before we give the proof of Proposition
4.14
, let us introduce the function

\displaystyle F_{T}(r):=\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\;\bigl(|\cdot|^{4}|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*\bigl(|\cdot|^{4}|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr).

(4.102)

As long as
T\geqslant T_{0}>0
, Lemma
4.4
and (
4.52
) imply
\|F_{T}\|_{1}\leqslant C
.

Proof of Proposition
4.14
.

We have

\displaystyle\langle\Delta,M_{T_{B}}^{(3)}\Delta\rangle

\displaystyle=4\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}Z\;V\alpha_{*}(r)V\alpha_{*}(s)\,k_{T}(Z,r-s)\;\langle\Psi,\mathcal{R}(Z\cdot\Pi_{X})\Psi\rangle,

(4.103)

where the function
\mathcal{R}(x)=\cos(x)-1+\frac{x^{2}}{2}
obeys the bound
0\leqslant\mathcal{R}(x)\leqslant\frac{1}{24}x^{4}
. We claim that

\displaystyle|Z\cdot\Pi|^{4}

\displaystyle\leqslant 9\;|Z|^{4}\;(\Pi^{4}+8B^{2}),

(4.104)

which implies

\displaystyle\langle\Psi,\mathcal{R}(Z\cdot\Pi)\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{9}{24}\;|Z|^{4}\;\bigl(\|\Pi^{2}\Psi\|_{2}^{2}+8B^{2}\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)\leqslant C\;B^{3}\;|Z|^{4}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})}^{2}.

(4.105)

To see that (
4.104
) is true, we note that
[\Pi^{(1)},\Pi^{(2)}]=-2\mathrm{i}B
implies

\displaystyle\Pi\;\Pi^{2}=\Pi^{2}\;\Pi+4\mathrm{i}B\;(-\Pi^{(2)},\Pi^{(1)},0)^{t},

(4.106)

and hence

\displaystyle\Pi\;\Pi^{2}\;\Pi=\Pi^{4}+8B^{2}.

(4.107)

We also have
[Z\cdot\Pi,\Pi]=-2\mathrm{i}\,\mathbf{B}\wedge Z
, which implies
(Z\cdot\Pi)\Pi^{2}(Z\cdot\Pi)=\Pi(Z\cdot\Pi)^{2}\Pi
. We combine this with the operator inequality (
4.99
) for
(Z\cdot\Pi)^{2}
and (
4.107
) and get
(Z\cdot\Pi)\Pi^{2}(Z\cdot\Pi)\leqslant 3|Z|^{2}(\Pi^{4}+8B^{2})
. Finally, we write
|Z\cdot\Pi|^{4}=(Z\cdot\Pi)(Z\cdot\Pi)^{2}(Z\cdot\Pi)
, apply (
4.99
) again, and obtain (
4.104
).

Using the estimate (
4.60
) on
|Z|^{4}
and (
4.105
), we argue as in the proof of (
4.61
) to see that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;|Z|^{4}\;|k_{T}(Z,r)|

\displaystyle\leqslant F_{T}(r)

(4.108)

with
F_{T}
in (
4.102
). The bound on the
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-norm of
F_{T}
below (
4.102
), (
4.103
), (
4.105
), and (
4.108
) prove the claim.
∎

4.4.5
Summary: The quadratic terms

Let us summarize the results concerning the quadratic terms in
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
that are relevant for the proof of Theorem
3.5
and provide an intermediate statement that is needed for the proof of Proposition
3.6
.

Proposition 4.15
.

Given
T_{0}>0
there is a constant
B_{0}>0
such that for any
T_{0}\leqslant T\leqslant T_{\mathrm{c}}
, any
0<B\leqslant B_{0}
, and whenever
|\cdot|^{k}V\alpha_{*}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
for
k\in\{0,1,2\}
,
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})
, and
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
as in (
3.1
), we have

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\langle\Delta,L_{T,B}\Delta\rangle+\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\;\langle\alpha_{*},V\alpha_{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\leqslant c\,\frac{T-T_{\mathrm{c}}}{T_{\mathrm{c}}}\,\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}+CB^{2}\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}.

(4.109)

Proof.

By Lemma
4.6
, the decomposition (
4.46
) of
L_{T,B}
, as well as Propositions
4.7
and
4.9
, we have

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\langle\Delta,L_{T,B}\Delta\rangle+\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\;\langle\alpha_{*},V\alpha_{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip-100.0pt=-\frac{1}{4}\langle\Delta,M_{T}^{(1)}\Delta-M_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(1)}\Delta\rangle-\frac{1}{4}\langle\Delta,M_{T,B}\Delta-M_{T}^{(1)}\Delta\rangle+R_{1}(\Delta),

(4.110)

where

\displaystyle|R_{1}(\Delta)|\leqslant C\;B^{3}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}

and, by Proposition
4.11
,

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\langle\Delta,M_{T}^{(1)}\Delta-M_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(1)}\Delta\rangle\leqslant c\;\frac{T-T_{\mathrm{c}}}{T_{\mathrm{c}}}\;\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}.

We claim that

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta,M_{T,B}\Delta-M_{T}^{(1)}\Delta\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;B^{2}\;\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}.

(4.111)

The proof of (
4.111
) goes along the same lines as that of Proposition
4.9
and uses the operator inequality (
4.99
) on
(Z\cdot\Pi)^{2}
to estimate

\displaystyle|\langle\Psi,[\cos(Z\cdot\Pi)-1]\Psi\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;B^{2}\;|Z|^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}.

(4.112)

We omit the details. This proves (
4.109
).
∎

Let the assumptions of Theorem
3.5
hold. We combine (
4.110
) with the results of
Propositions
4.11
,
4.13
, and
4.14
to see that for
T=T_{\mathrm{c}}(1-DB)
with
D\in\mathbb{R}
we have

-\frac{1}{4}\langle\Delta,L_{T,B}\Delta\rangle+\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\,\langle\alpha_{*},V\alpha_{*}\rangle=\Lambda_{0}\;\|\Pi\Psi\|_{2}^{2}-DB\;\Lambda_{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}+R_{2}(\Delta),

(4.113)

where

|R_{2}(\Delta)|\leqslant C\,B^{3}\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})}^{2}.

This concludes our analysis of the operator
L_{T,B}
.

4.4.6
A representation formula for the operator
N_{T,B}

Let us introduce the notation
\mathbf{Z}
for the vector
(Z_{1},Z_{2},Z_{3})
with
Z_{1},Z_{2},Z_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We also denote
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Z}=\mathrm{d}Z_{1}\mathrm{d}Z_{2}\mathrm{d}Z_{3}
.
Remarkably, the strategy of the analysis we used for
L_{T,B}
carries over to the nonlinear operator
N_{T,B}
in (
3.16
). As in the case of
L_{T,B}
, we start with a representation formula for the operator
N_{T,B}
and note the analogy to Lemma
4.6
.

Lemma 4.16
.

The operator
N_{T,B}\colon{H^{1}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}\rightarrow{L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
in (
3.16
) acts as

\displaystyle N_{T,B}(\alpha)(X,r)

\displaystyle=\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Z}\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{s}\;\ell_{T,B}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})\;\mathcal{A}(X,\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{s})

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(X,\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z_{1}\cdot\Pi_{X}}\alpha(X,s_{1})\;\overline{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z_{2}\cdot\Pi_{X}}\alpha(X,s_{2})}\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z_{3}\cdot\Pi_{X}}\alpha(X,s_{3})

(4.114)

and

\displaystyle\ell_{T,B}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle:=\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\ell_{T,B}^{n}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot\Phi(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})},

(4.115)

where

\displaystyle\ell_{T,B}^{n}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle:=g_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigl(Z_{1}-\frac{r-s_{1}}{2}\bigr)\;g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigl(Z_{1}-Z_{2}-\frac{s_{1}+s_{2}}{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt\times g_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigl(Z_{2}-Z_{3}-\frac{s_{2}+s_{3}}{2}\bigr)\;g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigl(Z_{3}+\frac{r-s_{3}}{2}\bigr)

(4.116)

with
g_{B}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}
in (
4.27
) and

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle:=\frac{r}{2}\wedge\bigl(Z_{1}-\frac{r-s_{1}}{2}\bigr)+\frac{r}{2}\wedge\bigl(Z_{3}+\frac{r-s_{3}}{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip-35.0pt+\bigl(Z_{2}-Z_{3}-\frac{s_{2}+s_{3}}{2}\bigr)\wedge\bigl(Z_{1}-Z_{2}-\frac{s_{1}+s_{2}}{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip-35.0pt+\bigl(Z_{3}+\frac{r-s_{3}}{2}\bigr)\wedge\bigl(Z_{1}-Z_{2}-\frac{s_{1}+s_{2}}{2}\bigr)+\bigl(s_{2}+s_{3}-\frac{r}{2}\bigr)\wedge\bigl(Z_{1}-Z_{2}-\frac{s_{1}+s_{2}}{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip-35.0pt+\bigl(Z_{3}+\frac{r-s_{3}}{2}\bigr)\wedge\bigl(Z_{3}-Z_{2}+\frac{s_{2}+s_{3}}{2}\bigr)+\bigl(s_{3}-\frac{r}{2}\bigr)\wedge\bigl(Z_{3}-Z_{2}+\frac{s_{2}+s_{3}}{2}\bigr).

(4.117)

Remark 4.17
.

We highlight that the formula (
4.117
) for the phase function
\Phi
only involves the coordinates that appear in (
4.116
) and the relative coordinates
r
and
\mathbf{s}
. This structure allows us to remove the magnetic phase factor in (
4.115
) with techniques that are similar to the ones used in the analysis of
L_{T,B}
.

Proof of Lemma
4.16
.

The integral kernel of
N_{T,B}
reads

\displaystyle N_{T,B}(\alpha)(X,r)

\displaystyle=\smash{\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}}\;G_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(\zeta_{X}^{r},u_{1})\,\alpha(u_{1},v_{1})\,G_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(u_{2},v_{1})\,\overline{\alpha(u_{2},v_{2})}

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt\times G_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(v_{2},u_{3})\,\alpha(u_{3},v_{3})\,G_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(\zeta_{X}^{-r},v_{3}),

where we used the short-hand notation
\zeta_{X}^{r}:=X+\frac{r}{2}
. We also used that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{B}}}(x,y)=-G_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(y,x),

(4.118)

which follows from
\overline{A^{*}(x,y)}=A(y,x)
and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{z-\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{B}}}=\overline{\Bigl(\frac{1}{z-\mathfrak{h}_{B}}\Bigr)^{*}}.

We hereby correct a typo in the analogue of (
4.118
) in the proof of
[
17
, Lemma 11]
.

Let us define the coordinates
\mathbf{Z}
and
\mathbf{s}
by

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}

\displaystyle=X+\mathbf{Z}+\frac{\mathbf{s}}{2},

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle=X+\mathbf{Z}-\frac{\mathbf{s}}{2},

and note that we interpret them as relative and center-of-mass coordinates. For
N_{T,B}
this implies

\displaystyle N_{T,B}(\alpha)(X,r)

\displaystyle=\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Z}\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{s}\;\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\mathbf{B}\cdot(X\wedge Z_{1})}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mathbf{B}\cdot(X\wedge Z_{2})}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\mathbf{B}\cdot(X\wedge Z_{3})}\;\mathcal{A}(X,\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle\hskip-60.0pt\times\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}G_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(\zeta_{X}^{r},\zeta_{Z_{1}+X}^{s_{1}})\,G_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(\zeta_{Z_{2}+X}^{s_{2}},\zeta_{Z_{1}+X}^{-s_{1}})\,G_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(\zeta_{Z_{2}+X}^{-s_{2}},\zeta_{Z_{3}+X}^{s_{3}})\,G_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(\zeta_{X}^{-r},\zeta_{Z_{3}+X}^{-s_{3}})

with
\mathcal{A}(X,\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{s})
in (
4.114
). Here, we used
\mathbf{B}\cdot(X\wedge Z)=Z\cdot(\mathbf{B}\wedge X)
and that
Z\cdot(\mathbf{B}\wedge X)
commutes with
Z\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X})
, which implies

\displaystyle\alpha(X+Z,s)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X})}\,\alpha(X,s)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\mathbf{B}\cdot(X\wedge Z)}\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot\Pi_{X}}\alpha(X,s).

A tedious but straightforward computation that uses Lemma
4.3
(b) shows

\displaystyle G_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(\zeta_{X}^{r},\zeta_{Z_{1}+X}^{s_{1}})\;G_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(\zeta_{Z_{2}+X}^{s_{2}},\zeta_{Z_{1}+X}^{-s_{1}})\;G_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(\zeta_{Z_{2}+X}^{-s_{2}},\zeta_{Z_{3}+X}^{s_{3}})\;G_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(\zeta_{X}^{-r},\zeta_{Z_{3}+X}^{-s_{3}})

\displaystyle\hskip-300.0pt=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mathbf{B}\cdot(X\wedge Z_{1})}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\mathbf{B}\cdot(X\wedge Z_{2})}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mathbf{B}\cdot(X\wedge Z_{3})}\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot\Phi(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})}\;\ell_{T,B}^{n}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s}).

(4.119)

This proves the claim.
∎

As in the case of
L_{T,B}
, we analyze the operator
N_{T,B}
by introducing several steps of simplification. Namely, we write

\displaystyle N_{T,B}=(N_{T,B}-\widetilde{N}_{T,B})+(\widetilde{N}_{T,B}-N_{T,B}^{(1)})+(N_{T,B}^{(1)}-N_{T}^{(2)})+N_{T}^{(2)}.

(4.120)

with
\widetilde{N}_{T,B}
in (
4.121
),
N_{T,B}^{(1)}
in (
4.129
), and
N_{T}^{(2)}
in (
4.135
). To obtain
\widetilde{N}_{T,B}
we replace the functions
g_{B}^{z}
by
g_{0}^{z}
in
N_{T,B}
. When we replace
\ell_{T,B}
by
\ell_{T,0}
, we obtain
N_{T,B}^{(1)}
, and
N_{T}^{(2)}
is obtained from
N_{T,B}^{(1)}
by replacing the magnetic translations
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z_{i}\cdot\Pi_{X}}
by
1
. Using arguments that are comparable to the ones applied in the analysis of the operator
L_{T,B}
, we show in Section
4.4.7
below that the contributions from the terms in the parentheses in (
4.120
) can be treated as remainders. In Section
4.4.8
we prove a proposition that allows us to extract the quartic term in the Ginzburg–Landau functional from the term
\langle\Delta,N_{T}^{(2)}(\Delta)\rangle
. Finally, we summarize our findings in Section
4.4.9
.

4.4.7
Approximation of
N_{T,B}

4.4.7.1
The operator
\widetilde{N}_{T,B}
.

The operator
\widetilde{N}_{T,B}
is defined by

\displaystyle\widetilde{N}_{T,B}(\alpha)(X,r)

\displaystyle:=\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Z}\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{s}\;\widetilde{\ell}_{T,B}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})\;\mathcal{A}(X,\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{s})

(4.121)

with
