\displaystyle\widetilde{\ell}_{T,B}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle:=\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\ell_{T,0}^{n}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot\Phi(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})},

\mathcal{A}
in (
4.114
),
\ell_{T,0}^{n}
in (
4.116
) and
\Phi
in (
4.117
). The following proposition quantifies the error that we make when we replace
N_{T,B}(\Delta)
by
\widetilde{N}_{T,B}(\Delta)
in our computations.

Proposition 4.18
.

Assume that
V\alpha_{*}\in L^{\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. For every
T_{0}>0
there is
B_{0}>0
such that for any
0<B\leqslant B_{0}
, any
T\geqslant T_{0}
, any
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})
, and
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
as in (
3.1
), we have

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta,N_{T,B}(\Delta)-\widetilde{N}_{T,B}(\Delta)\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;B^{4}\;\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}}^{4}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{4}.

Before we give the proof of Proposition
4.18
, let us introduce the function

\displaystyle F_{T,B}

\displaystyle:=\smash{\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}}\;|g_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}-g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}-g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{B}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}-g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{B}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}-g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|.

(4.122)

By Lemmas
4.4
and
4.5
as well as (
4.52
) we have

\displaystyle\|F_{T,B}\|_{1}\leqslant C\,B^{2}

(4.123)

for
T\geqslant T_{0}>0
.

Proof of Proposition
4.18
.

The function
|\Psi|
is periodic and (
2.7
) therefore implies

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot\Pi}\Psi\|_{6}^{2}=\|\Psi\|_{6}^{2}\leqslant C\,B\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}.

(4.124)

Consequently, we have

\displaystyle\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\Psi(X)|\;\prod_{i=1}^{3}|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z_{i}\cdot\Pi}\Psi(X)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\Psi\|_{2}\;\prod_{i=1}^{3}\|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z_{i}\cdot\Pi}\Psi\|_{6}\leqslant C\,B^{2}\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{4}

(4.125)

as well as

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta,N_{T,B}(\Delta)-\widetilde{N}_{T,B}(\Delta)\rangle|

\displaystyle\hskip-100.0pt\leqslant C\,B^{2}\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B}))}^{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\,\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{s}\;|V\alpha_{*}(r)|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s_{1})|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s_{2})|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s_{3})|

\displaystyle\hskip 60.0pt\times\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Z}\;|\ell_{T,B}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})-\widetilde{\ell}_{T,B}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})|.

(4.126)

We use the change of variables

\displaystyle Z_{1}^{\prime}-Z_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=Z_{1}-Z_{2}-\frac{s_{1}+s_{2}}{2},

\displaystyle Z_{2}^{\prime}-Z_{3}^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=Z_{2}-Z_{3}-\frac{s_{2}+s_{3}}{2},

\displaystyle Z_{3}^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=Z_{3}+\frac{r-s_{3}}{2},

(4.127)

whence

\displaystyle Z_{1}-\frac{r-s_{1}}{2}=Z_{1}^{\prime}-(r-s_{1}-s_{2}-s_{3}).

(4.128)

As in the proof of (
4.61
), we conclude

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Z}\;|\ell_{T,B}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})-\widetilde{\ell}_{T,B}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})|\leqslant F_{T,B}(r-s_{1}-s_{2}-s_{3})

with
F_{T,B}
in (
4.122
). We insert the above bound in (
4.126
) and use

\displaystyle\bigl\|V\alpha_{*}\;\bigl(V\alpha_{*}*V\alpha_{*}*V\alpha_{*}*F_{T,B}\bigr)\bigr\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}}^{4}\;\|F_{T,B}\|_{1}

as well as (
4.123
), which proves the claim.
∎

4.4.7.2
The operator
N_{T,B}^{(1)}
.

The operator
N_{T,B}^{(1)}
is defined by

\displaystyle N_{T,B}^{(1)}(\alpha)(X,r)

\displaystyle:=\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Z}\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{s}\;\ell_{T,0}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})\;\mathcal{A}(X,\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{s})

(4.129)

with
\mathcal{A}
in (
4.114
) and
\ell_{T,0}
in (
4.115
). The following proposition allows us to replace
\langle\Delta,\widetilde{N}_{T,B}(\Delta)\rangle
by
\langle\Delta,N_{T,B}^{(1)}(\Delta)\rangle
in our computations.

Proposition 4.19
.

Assume that
|\cdot|^{k}V\alpha_{*}\in L^{\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
for
k\in\{0,1\}
. For every
T\geqslant T_{0}>0
, every
B>0
, every
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})
and
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
as in (
3.1
), we have

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta,\widetilde{N}_{T,B}(\Delta)-N_{T,B}^{(1)}(\Delta)\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;B^{3}\;\bigl(\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}}^{4}+\|\,|\cdot|V\alpha_{*}\|_{\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}}^{4}\bigr)\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{4}.

Before we start with the proof of Proposition
4.19
, we introduce the functions

\displaystyle F_{T}^{(1)}

\displaystyle:=\smash{\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}}\,|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*\bigl(|\cdot|\,|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)*\bigl(|\cdot|\,|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*\bigl(|\cdot|\,|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)*|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*\bigl(|\cdot|\,|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*\bigl(|\cdot|\,|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)*\bigl(|\cdot|\,|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)

(4.130)

and

\displaystyle F_{T}^{(2)}

\displaystyle:=\smash{\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}}\,\bigl(|\cdot|\,|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*\bigl(|\cdot|\,|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)*|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*\bigl(|\cdot|\,|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*\bigl(|\cdot|\,|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr).

(4.131)

As long as
T\geqslant T_{0}>0
, Lemma
4.4
and (
4.52
) imply the bound

\displaystyle\|F_{T}^{(1)}\|_{1}+\|F_{T}^{(2)}\|_{1}\leqslant C.

(4.132)

Proof of Proposition
4.19
.

We use (
4.125
) and estimate

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta,\widetilde{N}_{T,B}(\Delta)-N_{T,B}^{(1)}(\Delta)\rangle|

\displaystyle\hskip-100.0pt\leqslant C\,B^{2}\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{s}\;|V\alpha_{*}(r)|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s_{1})|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s_{2})|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s_{3})|

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt\times\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Z}\;|\ell_{T,0}^{n}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})|\;\bigl|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mathbf{B}\,\Phi(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})}-1\bigr|.

(4.133)

In terms of the coordinates in (
4.127
) and with (
4.128
), the phase function
\Phi
in (
4.117
) can be written as

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=(Z_{2}^{\prime}-Z_{3}^{\prime})\wedge(Z_{1}^{\prime}-Z_{2}^{\prime})+Z_{3}^{\prime}\wedge(Z_{1}^{\prime}-Z_{2}^{\prime})+Z_{3}^{\prime}\wedge(Z_{3}^{\prime}-Z_{2}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt+\frac{r}{2}\wedge\bigl(Z_{1}^{\prime}-(r-s_{1}-s_{2}-s_{3})\bigr)+\bigl(s_{2}+s_{3}-\frac{r}{2}\bigr)\wedge(Z_{1}^{\prime}-Z_{2}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt+\bigl(s_{3}-\frac{r}{2}\bigr)\wedge(Z_{3}^{\prime}-Z_{2}^{\prime})+\frac{r}{2}\wedge Z_{3}^{\prime}.

(4.134)

We use the estimate
|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot\Phi(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})}-1|\leqslant B\,|\Phi(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})|
, (
4.133
), and argue as in the proof of (
4.78
) to see that

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Z}\;|\ell_{T,0}^{n}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})|\;\bigl|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot\Phi(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})}-1\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt\leqslant CB\;\bigl[F_{T}^{(1)}(r-s_{1}-s_{2}-s_{3})+F_{T}^{(2)}(r-s_{1}-s_{2}-s_{3})\;\bigl(1+|r|+|s_{1}|+|s_{2}|+|s_{3}|\bigr)\bigr]

with
F_{T}^{(1)}
in (
4.130
) and
F_{T}^{(2)}
in (
4.131
). Young’s inequality then implies

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta,\widetilde{N}_{T,B}(\Delta)-N_{T,B}^{(1)}(\Delta)\rangle|

\displaystyle\hskip-100.0pt\leqslant C\;B^{3}\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{4}\;\bigl(\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}}^{4}+\|\,|\cdot|V\alpha_{*}\|_{\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}}^{4}\bigr)\bigl(\|F_{T}^{(1)}\|_{1}+\|F_{T}^{(2)}\|_{1}\bigr).

Finally, an application of (
4.132
)
proves the claim.
∎

4.4.7.3
The operator
N_{T}^{(2)}
.

The operator
N_{T}^{(2)}
is defined by

\displaystyle N_{T}^{(2)}(\alpha)(X,r)

\displaystyle:=\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Z}\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{s}\;\ell_{T,0}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})\,\mathcal{A}(X,0,\mathbf{s})

(4.135)

with
\mathcal{A}
in (
4.114
) and
\ell_{T,0}
in (
4.115
).

The following proposition allows us to replace
\langle\Delta,N_{T,B}^{(1)}(\Delta)\rangle
by
\langle\Delta,N_{T}^{(2)}(\Delta)\rangle
in our computations. We highlight that the
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
-norm of
\Psi
is needed to bound the difference between the two terms.

Proposition 4.20
.

Assume that
|\cdot|^{k}V\alpha_{*}\in L^{\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
for
k\in\{0,2\}
. For any
T\geqslant T_{0}>0
, any
B>0
, any
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
, and
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
as in (
3.1
), we have

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta,N_{T,B}^{(1)}(\Delta)-N_{T}^{(2)}(\Delta)\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;B^{3}\;\bigl(\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}}^{4}+\|\,|\cdot|^{2}V\alpha_{*}\|_{\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}}^{4}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 130.0pt\times\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{3}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})}.

Before we prove the above proposition, let us introduce the functions

\displaystyle F_{T}^{(1)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|

and

\displaystyle F_{T}^{(2)}

\displaystyle:=\smash{\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}}\;|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*\bigl(|\cdot|^{2}\,|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)*|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*\,|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*\bigl(|\cdot|^{2}\,|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*\bigl(|\cdot|^{2}\,|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr).

For
T\geqslant T_{0}>0
, an application of Lemma
4.4
and the estimate (
4.52
) on
f(t,\omega)
show

\displaystyle\|F_{T}^{(1)}\|_{1}+\|F_{T}^{(2)}\|_{1}\leqslant C.

(4.136)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\langle\Delta,N_{T,B}^{(1)}(\Delta)-N_{T}^{(2)}(\Delta)\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip-115.0pt=16\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{s}\;V\alpha_{*}(r)V\alpha_{*}(s_{1})V\alpha_{*}(s_{2})V\alpha_{*}(s_{3})\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Z}\;\ell_{T,0}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle\hskip-115.0pt\times\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\;\overline{\Psi(X)}\;\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z_{1}\cdot\Pi_{X}}\Psi(X)\,\overline{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z_{2}\cdot\Pi_{X}}\Psi(X)}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z_{3}\cdot\Pi_{X}}\Psi(X)-\Psi(X)\overline{\Psi(X)}\Psi(X)\bigr).

(4.137)

Apart from the exponential factors, this expression is symmetric under the simultaneous replacement of
(Z_{1},Z_{2},Z_{3})
by
(-Z_{1},-Z_{2},-Z_{3})
.
When we expand the magnetic translations in cosine and sine functions of
Z_{i}\cdot\Pi_{X}
,
i=1,2,3
, the above symmetry implies that all terms with an odd number of sine functions vanish. Accordingly, we may replace the bracket in the last line of (
4.137
) by

\displaystyle\bigl(\cos(Z_{1}\cdot\Pi_{X})\Psi(X)\;\overline{\cos(Z_{2}\cdot\Pi_{X})\Psi(X)}\;\cos(Z_{3}\cdot\Pi_{X})\Psi(X)-\Psi(X)\overline{\Psi(X)}\Psi(X)\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt+\cos(Z_{1}\cdot\Pi_{X})\Psi(X)\;\overline{\mathrm{i}\sin(Z_{2}\cdot\Pi_{X})\Psi(X)}\;\mathrm{i}\sin(Z_{3}\cdot\Pi_{X})\Psi(X)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt+\mathrm{i}\sin(Z_{1}\cdot\Pi_{X})\Psi(X)\;\overline{\cos(Z_{2}\cdot\Pi_{X})\Psi(X)}\;\mathrm{i}\sin(Z_{3}\cdot\Pi_{X})\Psi(X)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt+\mathrm{i}\sin(Z_{1}\cdot\Pi_{X})\Psi(X)\;\overline{\mathrm{i}\sin(Z_{2}\cdot\Pi_{X})\Psi(X)}\;\cos(Z_{3}\cdot\Pi_{X})\Psi(X).

(4.138)

Let us consider the first term in (
4.138
). We use
|\cos(x)-1|^{2}=4|\sin^{4}(\frac{x}{2})|\leqslant\frac{1}{4}|x|^{4}
and the operator inequality in (
4.104
) to see that
|\cos(Z\cdot\Pi)-1|^{2}\leqslant C\cdot|Z|^{4}\;(\Pi^{4}+B^{2})
holds. In particular,

\displaystyle\|[\cos(Z\cdot\Pi)-1]\Psi\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;B^{3}\;|Z|^{4}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})}^{2}.

(4.139)

In combination with the estimate (
4.124
) on
\|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot\Pi}\Psi\|_{6}
, this implies

\displaystyle\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\Psi(X)|\bigl|\cos(Z_{1}\cdot\Pi)\Psi(X)\;\overline{\cos(Z_{2}\cdot\Pi)\Psi(X)}\;\cos(Z_{3}\cdot\Pi)\Psi(X)-\Psi(X)\overline{\Psi(X)}\Psi(X)\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt\leqslant\|\Psi\|_{6}\;\|(\cos(Z_{1}\cdot\Pi)-1)\Psi\|_{2}\;\|\cos(Z_{2}\cdot\Pi)\Psi\|_{6}\;\|\cos(Z_{3}\cdot\Pi)\Psi\|_{6}

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt+\|\Psi\|_{6}^{2}\;\|(\cos(Z_{2}\cdot\Pi)-1)\Psi\|_{2}\;\|\cos(Z_{3}\cdot\Pi)\Psi\|_{6}+\|\Psi\|_{6}^{3}\;\|(\cos(Z_{3}\cdot\Pi)-1)\Psi\|_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt\leqslant C\,B^{3}\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{3}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})}\;\bigl(|Z_{1}|^{2}+|Z_{2}|^{2}+|Z_{3}|^{2}\bigr).

(4.140)

To treat the other terms in (
4.138
) we use the operator inequality in (
4.99
) to see that

\displaystyle\|\sin(Z\cdot\Pi)\Psi\|_{2}^{2}=\langle\Psi,\sin^{2}(Z\cdot\Pi)\,\Psi\rangle\leqslant C\,B^{2}\,|Z|^{2}\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2},

which yields

\displaystyle\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\Psi(X)|\;|\cos(Z_{i}\cdot\Pi)\Psi(X)|\;|\sin(Z_{j}\cdot\Pi)\Psi(X)|\;|\sin(Z_{k}\cdot\Pi)\Psi(X)|

\displaystyle\hskip 180.0pt\leqslant C\,B^{3}\,\bigl(|Z_{j}|^{2}+|Z_{k}|^{2}\bigr)\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{4}.

(4.141)

We gather (
4.137
), (
4.138
), (
4.140
), and (
4.141
) and find

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta,N_{T,B}^{(1)}(\Delta)-N_{T}^{(2)}(\Delta)\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,B^{3}\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{3}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})}

\displaystyle\hskip-30.0pt\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{s}\;|V\alpha_{*}(r)|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s_{1})|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s_{2})|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s_{3})|

\displaystyle\hskip 60.0pt\times\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Z}\;|\ell_{T,0}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})|\;\bigl(|Z_{1}|^{2}+|Z_{2}|^{2}+|Z_{3}|^{2}\bigr).

When we write the coordinates
Z_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
, in terms of the coordinates in (
4.127
) and (
4.128
) plus linear combinations of
r
and
s_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
, we see that

\displaystyle|Z_{1}|

\displaystyle\leqslant|Z_{1}^{\prime}-Z_{2}^{\prime}|+|Z_{2}^{\prime}-Z_{3}^{\prime}|+|Z_{3}^{\prime}|+|r|+|s_{1}|+|s_{2}|+|s_{3}|,

\displaystyle|Z_{2}|

\displaystyle\leqslant|Z_{2}^{\prime}-Z_{3}^{\prime}|+|Z_{3}^{\prime}|+|r|+|s_{2}|+|s_{3}|,

\displaystyle|Z_{3}|

\displaystyle\leqslant|Z_{3}^{\prime}|+|r|+|s_{3}|.

We use this and argue as in the proof of (
4.78
), which yields

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Z}\;|\ell_{T,0}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})|\;\bigl(|Z_{1}|^{2}+|Z_{2}|^{2}+|Z_{3}|^{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt\leqslant C\bigl(F_{T}^{(1)}(r-s_{1}-s_{2}-s_{3})\;(|r|^{2}+|s_{1}|^{2}+|s_{2}|^{2}+|s_{3}|^{2})+F_{T}^{(2)}(r-s_{1}-s_{2}-s_{3})\bigr).

In particular,

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta,N_{T,B}^{(1)}(\Delta)-N_{T}^{(2)}(\Delta)\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;B^{3}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{3}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})}

\displaystyle\hskip 40.0pt\times\bigl(\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}}^{4}+\|\,|\cdot|^{2}V\alpha_{*}\|_{\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}}^{4}\bigr)\bigl(\|F_{T}^{(1)}\|_{1}+\|F_{T}^{(2)}\|_{1}\bigr).

In combination with (
4.136
), this proves the claim.
∎

4.4.8
Calculation of the quartic term in the Ginzburg–Landau functional

The following proposition allows us to extract the quartic term in the Ginzburg–Landau functional in (
1.17
) from
\langle\Delta,N_{T}^{(2)}(\Delta)\rangle
.

Proposition 4.21
.

Assume
V\alpha_{*}\in L^{\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. For any
B>0
, any
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})
, and
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
as in (
3.1
), we have

\displaystyle\langle\Delta,N_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(2)}(\Delta)\rangle=8\;\Lambda_{3}\;\|\Psi\|_{4}^{4}

with
\Lambda_{3}
in (
3.24
). Moreover, for any
T\geqslant T_{0}>0
, we have

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta,N_{T}^{(2)}(\Delta)-N_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(2)}(\Delta)\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;B^{2}\;|T-T_{\mathrm{c}}|\;\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}}^{4}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{4}.

Before we prove the above proposition, let us introduce the function

\displaystyle F_{T,T_{\mathrm{c}}}

\displaystyle:=\smash{\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}|2n+1|\bigl[}|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T}}|*|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T}}|*|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T}}|\phantom{\sum^{i}}

\displaystyle\hskip 120.0pt+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}|*|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T}}|

\displaystyle\hskip 120.0pt+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}|*|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T}}|*|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T}}|

\displaystyle\hskip 120.0pt+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}|*|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T}}|*|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}^{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}|\bigr],

(4.142)

where we have included the
T
-dependence of the Matsubara frequencies in our notation once more because different temperatures appear in the formula. As long as
T\geqslant T_{0}>0
, Lemma
4.4
and (
4.52
) imply

\|F_{T,T_{\mathrm{c}}}\|_{1}\leqslant C.

(4.143)

Proof of Proposition
4.21
.

Set

\displaystyle\ell_{T}(\mathbf{Z},r)

\displaystyle:=\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(r-Z_{1})\,g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(Z_{1}-Z_{2})\,g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(Z_{2}-Z_{3})\,g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(Z_{3}).

Then, by the change of variables (
4.127
) and (
4.128
), we have

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Z}\;\ell_{T,0}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})=\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Z}\;\ell_{T}(\mathbf{Z},r-s_{1}-s_{2}-s_{3}).

We use that
(\pm\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\mu-p^{2})^{-1}
is the Fourier transform of
g_{0}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(x)
, which yields

\displaystyle\ell_{T}(\mathbf{Z},r)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\iiiint_{\mathbb{R}^{12}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{12}}\;\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{1}\cdot(r-Z_{1})}}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\mu-p_{1}^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{2}\cdot(Z_{1}-Z_{2})}}{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\mu-p_{2}^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{3}\cdot(Z_{2}-Z_{3})}}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\mu-p_{3}^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{4}\cdot Z_{3}}}{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\mu-p_{4}^{2}}.

Integration over
\mathbf{Z}
gives

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Z}\;\ell_{T}(\mathbf{Z},r)=\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot r}\frac{1}{(\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\mu-p^{2})^{2}(\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mu+p^{2})^{2}}.

In view of the partial fraction expansion

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-E)^{2}(\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+E)^{2}}=\frac{1}{4E^{2}}\Bigl[\frac{1}{(\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-E)^{2}}+\frac{1}{(\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+E)^{2}}\Bigr]-\frac{1}{4E^{3}}\Bigl[\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-E}-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+E}\Bigr]

and the identity

\displaystyle\frac{\beta}{2}\frac{1}{\cosh^{2}(\frac{\beta}{2}z)}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}\tanh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}z\bigr)=-\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{(\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-z)^{2}},

(4.144)

which follows from (
3.12
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{(\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-E)^{2}(\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+E)^{2}}=\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}\;\frac{g_{1}(\beta E)}{E}

with the function
g_{1}
in (
3.21
). We conclude that

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Z}\;\ell_{T}(\mathbf{Z},r)

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot r}\;\frac{g_{1}(\beta(p^{2}-\mu))}{p^{2}-\mu}.

For the term we are interested in, this implies

\displaystyle\langle\Delta,N_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(2)}(\Delta)\rangle

\displaystyle=16\,\|\Psi\|_{4}^{4}\;\frac{\beta_{c}^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{s}\;V\alpha_{*}(r)V\alpha_{*}(s_{1})V\alpha_{*}(s_{2})V\alpha_{*}(s_{3})

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\cdot(r-s_{1}-s_{2}-s_{3})}\;\frac{g_{1}(\beta_{c}(p^{2}-\mu))}{p^{2}-\mu}

\displaystyle=8\,\|\Psi\|_{4}^{4}\,\frac{\beta_{c}^{2}}{16}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;|(-2)\widehat{V\alpha_{*}}(p)|^{4}\;\frac{g_{1}(\beta_{c}(p^{2}-\mu))}{p^{2}-\mu}=8\;\Lambda_{3}\;\|\Psi\|_{4}^{4}

(4.145)

with
\Lambda_{3}
in (
3.24
). This proves the first claim.

To prove the second claim, we note that

\displaystyle\langle\Delta,N_{T}^{(2)}(\Delta)-N_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(2)}(\Delta)

\displaystyle\rangle=16\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{s}\;V\alpha_{*}(r)V\alpha_{*}(s_{1})V\alpha_{*}(s_{2})V\alpha_{*}(s_{3})

\displaystyle\times\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Z}\;\left(\ell_{T,0}-\ell_{T_{\mathrm{c}},0}\right)(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\Psi(X)|^{4}.

(4.146)

Afterwards, we argue as in the proof of (
4.94
), that is, we use the resolvent equation (
4.92
) as well as the change of variables in (
4.127
) and (
4.128
) and obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Z}\;|\ell_{T,0}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})-\ell_{T_{\mathrm{c}},0}(\mathbf{Z},r,\mathbf{s})|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,|T-T_{\mathrm{c}}|\;F_{T,T_{\mathrm{c}}}(r-s_{1}-s_{2}-s_{3})

(4.147)

with the function
F_{T,T_{\mathrm{c}}}
in (
4.142
).
Together with (
4.146
), this implies

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta,N_{T}^{(2)}(\Delta)-N_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(2)}(\Delta)\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,|T-T_{\mathrm{c}}|\,\|\Psi\|_{6}^{3}\|\Psi\|_{2}\,\bigl\|V\alpha_{*}\;\bigl(V\alpha_{*}*V\alpha_{*}*V\alpha_{*}*F_{T,T_{\mathrm{c}}}\bigr)\bigr\|_{1}.

Finally, an application of (
2.7
), Young’s inequality, and (
4.143
) concludes the proof.
∎

4.4.9
Summary: The quartic terms and proof of Theorem
3.5

Let the assumptions of Theorem
3.5
hold. We collect the results of Lemma
4.16
, as well as Propositions
4.18
,
4.19
,
4.20
, and
4.21
, which yield

\frac{1}{8}\langle\Delta,N_{T,B}(\Delta)\rangle=\;\Lambda_{3}\;\|\Psi\|_{4}^{4}+R(B)

(4.148)

with

|R(B)|\leqslant C\;B^{3}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{3}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})}.

Together with (
4.113
), this completes the proof of Theorem
3.5
.

4.5
Proof of Proposition
3.2

We use
\alpha_{\Delta}=[\Gamma_{\Delta}]_{12}
, the resolvent equation (
4.3
) and (
4.4
) to see that

\displaystyle\alpha_{\Delta}

\displaystyle=[\mathcal{O}]_{12}+[\mathcal{Q}_{T,B}(\Delta)]_{12}=[\mathcal{O}]_{12}+\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta),

with
\mathcal{O}
in (
4.5
),
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Bigl[\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{\Delta}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{0}}\Bigr]_{12}.

The definition of
L_{T,B}
in (
3.15
) implies
[\mathcal{O}]_{12}=-\frac{1}{2}L_{T,B}\Delta
, and we define

\displaystyle\eta_{0}(\Delta)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(L_{T,B}\Delta-M_{T,B}\Delta\bigr)+\frac{1}{2}\bigl(M_{T}^{(1)}\Delta-M_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(1)}\Delta\bigr)+\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta),

\displaystyle\eta_{\perp}(\Delta)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(M_{T,B}\Delta-M_{T}^{(1)}\Delta\bigr),

(4.149)

with
M_{T,B}
in (
4.71
) and
M_{T}^{(1)}
in (
4.82
). Proposition
4.11
implies that
-\frac{1}{2}M_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}^{(1)}\Delta=\Psi\alpha_{*}
, so these definitions allow us to write
\alpha_{\Delta}
as in (
3.6
). It remains to prove the properties of
\eta_{0}
and
\eta_{\perp}
that are listed in Proposition
3.2
.

We start with the proof of (
3.7
), and note that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{B}}\,\Delta\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{B}}}\,\overline{\Delta}\,\Bigl[\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{\Delta}}\Bigr]_{11}\,\Delta\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{B}}}.

Using Hölder’s inequality, we immediately see that
\|\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)\|_{2}\leqslant C\beta^{3}\|\Delta\|_{6}^{3}
. Furthermore, we estimate

\|\pi\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)\|_{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Bigl\|\pi\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{B}}\Bigr\|_{\infty}\Bigl\|\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{B}}}\Bigr\|_{\infty}^{2}\Bigl\|\Bigl[\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{\Delta}}\Bigr]_{11}\Bigr\|_{\infty}\|\Delta\|_{6}^{3}.

With the help of
\|A\|_{\infty}^{2}=\|A^{*}A\|_{\infty}
for a general operator
A
, the first norm on the right side is bounded by

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\pi\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{B}}\Bigr\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant\Bigl\|\frac{1}{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{B}}\Bigr\|_{\infty}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\Bigl\|\pi^{2}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{B}}\Bigr\|_{\infty}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\leqslant C\,|\omega_{n}|^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}.

Hence,

\|\pi\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)\|_{2}\leqslant C\,\beta^{\nicefrac{{5}}{{2}}}\,\|\Delta\|_{6}^{3}.

(4.150)

With a similar argument, we see that
\|\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)\pi\|_{2}
is bounded by the right side of (
4.150
), too. An application of Lemma
4.1
and of (
2.7
) on the right side of (
4.150
) finally shows

\|\mathcal{R}_{T,B}(\Delta)\|_{H^{1}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\mathrm{s}})}^{2}\leqslant C\;B^{3}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{6}.

The remaining terms in
\eta_{0}(\Delta)
can be estimated with the help of Propositions
4.7
,
4.9
, and
4.11
, which establishes (
3.7
).

It remains to prove (
3.8
) and (
3.9
). We start with the proof of (
3.8
) and write

\displaystyle\eta_{\perp}(\Delta)(X,r)=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;k_{T}(Z,r-s)\;[\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{X})-1]\;\Delta(X,s).

(4.151)

Using (
4.139
) we see that

\displaystyle\|\eta_{\perp}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;B^{3}\;\|F_{T}^{(2)}\|_{1}^{2}\;\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})}^{2},

(4.152)

with the function
F_{T}^{(2)}
in (
4.74
). The
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}
)-norm of this function was estimated in (
4.132
). We use this bound and conclude the claimed bound for the
L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\mathrm{s}})
-norm of
\eta_{\perp}
. Bounds for
\|\widetilde{\pi}_{r}\eta_{\perp}\|_{2}
and
\||r|\eta_{\perp}\|_{2}
can be proved similarly and we leave the details to the reader.

To prove the claimed bound for
\|\Pi_{X}\eta_{\perp}\|_{2}
, we need to replace
[\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{X})-1]\Psi(X)
by
\Pi_{X}[\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{X})-1]\Psi(X)
in the proof of (
4.152
). Using the intertwining relation (
5.40
) in Lemma
5.12
below, the operator inquality (
4.99
) for
(Z\cdot\Pi)^{2}
, and the equality (
4.107
) for
\Pi\,\Pi^{2}\,\Pi
, we see that

\displaystyle\|\Pi[\cos(Z\cdot\Pi)-1]\Psi\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;B^{3}\;|Z|^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})}^{2}

(4.153)

holds. The claimed bound for
\|\Pi_{X}\eta_{\perp}\|_{2}
follows from (
4.151
) and (
4.153
), which, in combination with the previous considerations, proves (
3.8
).

To prove (
3.9
), we note that for any two radial functions
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
the function

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s\;f(r)\,k_{T}(Z,r-s)\,g(s)

(4.154)

is radial in
Z
. We claim that this implies that the operator

\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}r\;f(r)k_{T}(Z,r-s)g(s)[\cos(Z\cdot\Pi)-1]

(4.155)

equals
h(\Pi^{2})
for some function
h\colon[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
. To prove this, let us denote by
\widetilde{\Pi}
the same operator
\Pi
but understood to act on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
instead of
L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
. From
[
17
, Lemma 28]
we know that the above statement is true when
\Pi
is replaced by
\widetilde{\Pi}
. To reduce our claim to this case, we use the unitary Bloch–Floquet transformation

(\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{BF}}\Psi)(k,X):=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{B}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}k\cdot(X-\lambda)}(T_{B}(\lambda)\Psi)(X)

(4.156)

with
T_{B}(\lambda)
in (
1.16
) and inverse

(\mathcal{U}^{*}_{\mathrm{BF}}\Phi)(X)=\int_{[0,\sqrt{2\pi B}]^{3}}\mathrm{d}k\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k\cdot X}\Phi(k,X).

(4.157)

The magnetic momentum operator
\widetilde{\Pi}
obeys the identity

\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{BF}}\,\widetilde{\Pi}\,\mathcal{U}^{*}_{\mathrm{BF}}=\int^{\oplus}_{[0,\sqrt{2\pi B}]^{3}}\mathrm{d}k\;\widetilde{\Pi}(k)

(4.158)

with
\widetilde{\Pi}(k)=\Pi+k
acting on
L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
. The claim follows when we conjugate both sides of the equation

\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}r\;f(r)k_{T}(Z,r-s)g(s)[\cos(Z\cdot\widetilde{\Pi})-1]=h(\widetilde{\Pi}^{2})

with the Bloch–Floquet transformation and use that
\widetilde{\Pi}(0)=\Pi
. Eq. (
3.9
) is a direct consequence of the fact that the operator in (
4.155
) equals
h(\Pi^{2})
. This proves Proposition
3.2
.

4.6
Proof of Proposition
3.6

Let the assumptions of Proposition
3.6
hold. We show that there are constants
D_{0}>0
and
B_{0}>0
such that for
0<B\leqslant B_{0}
and temperatures
T
obeying

\displaystyle 0<T_{0}\leqslant T<T_{\mathrm{c}}(1-D_{0}B)

there is a function
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
, such that the Gibbs state
\Gamma_{\Delta}
in (
3.4
) built upon the gap function
\Delta(X,r)=-2V\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)
obeys (
3.27
).

To prove this, we choose
\psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{1})
with
\|\psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})}=1
and
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
as in (
1.18
). This, in particular, implies
\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})}=1
. We collect the results of Propositions
3.2
,
3.4
,
4.15
, as well as (
2.7
) and (
4.148
), and conclude that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{\Delta})-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{0})

\displaystyle<B\,\bigl(-cD_{0}\,\|\psi\|_{2}^{2}+C\bigr)

holds as long as
B
is small enough. We remark that this argument can be carried out without the assumption of
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{1})
-regularity of
\psi
by instead using the sign of
V
. Compare this to the discussion below (
3.28
). Choosing
D_{0}=\frac{C}{c\|\psi\|_{2}^{2}}
ends the proof of Proposition
3.6
.

5
The Structure of Low-Energy States

In Section
3
we use a Gibbs state to show that the BCS free energy is bounded from above by the Ginzburg–Landau energy plus corrections of lower order. The Gibbs state has a Cooper pair wavefunction which is given by a product of the form
\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)
to leading order, where
\Psi
is a minimizer of the Ginzburg–Landau functional in (
1.17
) and
\alpha_{*}
is the unique solution of the gap equation (
1.15
).
Moreover, close to the critical temperature the Cooper pair wave function is small in an appropriate sense, which allows us to expand the BCS functional in powers of
\Psi
and to obtain the terms in the Ginzburg–Landau functional.

Our proof of a matching lower bound for the BCS free energy in Section
6
is based on the fact that certain low-energy states of the BCS functional have a Cooper pair wave function with a similar structure. The precise statement is provided in Theorem
5.1
below, which is the main technical novelty of this paper. This section is devoted to its proof.

We recall the definition of the generalized one-particle density matrix
\Gamma
in (
1.5
), its offdiagonal entry
\alpha
, as well as the normal state
\Gamma_{0}
in (
1.12
).

Theorem 5.1

(Structure of low-energy states)
.

Let Assumptions
1.3
and
1.5
hold. For given
D_{0},D_{1}\geqslant 0
, there is a constant
B_{0}>0
such that for all
0<B\leqslant B_{0}
the following holds: If
T>0
obeys
T-T_{\mathrm{c}}\geqslant-D_{0}B
and if
\Gamma
is a gauge-periodic state with low energy, that is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{0})\leqslant D_{1}B^{2},

(5.1)

then there are
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})
and
\xi\in{H^{1}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
such that

\displaystyle\alpha(X,r)=\Psi(X)\alpha_{*}(r)+\xi(X,r),

(5.2)

where

\displaystyle\sup_{0<B\leqslant B_{0}}\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C,

\displaystyle\|\xi\|_{{H^{1}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant CB^{2}\bigl(\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}+D_{1}\bigr).

(5.3)

Remarks
.

(a)

Equation (
5.3
)
proves that, despite
\Psi
being dependent on
B
, it is a macroscopic quantity in the sense that its
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})
-norm scales as that of the function in (
1.18
).

(b)

We highlight that, in contrast to the
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})
-norm of
\Psi
, the
{H^{1}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
-norm of
\xi
is not scaled with additional factors of
B
, see (
2.9
). The unscaled
L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
-norm of
\Psi
is of the order
B^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
, whence it is much larger than that of
\xi
.

(c)

Theorem
5.1
should be compared to
[
19
, Eq. (5.1)]
and
[
17
, Theorem 22]
.

Theorem
5.1
contains the natural a priori bounds for the Cooper pair wave function
\alpha
of a low-energy state
\Gamma
in the sense of (
5.1
). However, in Section
6
we are going to need more regularity of
\Psi
than is provided by Theorem
5.1
. More precisely, we are going to use the function
\Psi
from this decomposition to construct a Gibbs state
\Gamma_{\Delta_{\Psi}}
and apply our trial state analysis provided by Propositions
3.2
and
3.4
as well as Theorem
3.5
to extract the Ginzburg–Landau energy. In order to control the errors during this analysis, we need the
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
-norm of
\Psi
. The following corollary provides us with a decomposition of
\alpha
in terms of a center-of-mass Cooper pair wave function
\Psi_{\leqslant}
with
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
-regularity.

Corollary 5.2
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
5.1
hold and let
\varepsilon\in[B,B_{0}]
. Let
\Psi
be as in
(
5.2
) and define

\displaystyle\Psi_{\leqslant}

\displaystyle:=\mathbbs 1_{[0,\varepsilon]}(\Pi^{2})\Psi,

\displaystyle\Psi_{>}

\displaystyle:=\mathbbs 1_{(\varepsilon,\infty)}(\Pi^{2})\Psi.

(5.4)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\|\Psi_{\leqslant}\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2},

\displaystyle\|\Psi_{\leqslant}\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{k}(Q_{B})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,(\varepsilon B^{-1})^{k-1}\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2},\qquad k\geqslant 2,

(5.5)

as well as

\displaystyle\|\Psi_{>}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-1}B^{2}\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2},

\displaystyle\|\Pi\Psi_{>}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant CB^{2}\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}.

(5.6)

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\sigma_{0}(X,r):=\Psi_{>}(X)\alpha_{*}(r)

(5.7)

satisfies

\displaystyle\|\sigma_{0}\|_{{H^{1}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-1}B^{2}\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}

(5.8)

and, with
\xi
in (
5.2
), the function

\displaystyle\sigma:=\xi+\sigma_{0}

(5.9)

obeys

\displaystyle\|\sigma\|_{{H^{1}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}}^{2}\leqslant CB^{2}\bigl(\varepsilon^{-1}\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})}^{2}+D_{1}\bigr).

(5.10)

In terms of these functions, the Cooper pair wave function
\alpha
of the low-energy state
\Gamma
in (
5.1
) admits the decomposition

\displaystyle\alpha(X,r)=\Psi_{\leqslant}(X)\alpha_{*}(r)+\sigma(X,r).

(5.11)

Proof.

The bounds for
\Psi_{\leqslant}
and
\Psi_{>}
in (
5.5
) and (
5.6
) are a direct consequence of their definition in (
5.4
).
The bound (
5.6
) immediately implies (
5.8
). Moreover,
\sigma
obeys (
5.10
) by (
5.3
) and (
5.8
). Finally, (
5.11
) follows from (
5.2
).
∎

5.1
A lower bound for the BCS functional

We start the proof of Theorem
5.1
with the following lower bound on the BCS functional.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\Gamma_{0}
be the normal state in (
1.12
). We have the lower bound

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{0})\geqslant\Tr\bigl[(K_{T,B}-V)\alpha\alpha^{*}\bigr]+\frac{4T}{5}\Tr\bigl[(\alpha^{*}\alpha)^{2}\bigr],

(5.12)

where
K_{T,B}=K_{T}(\pi)
and
V\alpha(x,y)=V(x-y)\alpha(x,y)
.

Proof.

The statement follows from Eqs. (5.3)–(5.12) in
[
19
]
with the evident replacements. The argument uses the relative entropy inequality
[
19
, Lemma 1]
, which is a refinement of the bound
[
31
, Theorem 1]
.
∎

In Proposition
A.1
in Appendix
A
we show that the magnetic field can lower the lowest eigenvalue zero of
K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V

at most by a constant times
B
. This information is used in the following lemma to bound
K_{T,B}-V
from below by a nonnegative operator, up to a correction of the size
CB
. The inequality (
5.13
) below is stated for
K_{T,B}-V
as a one-particle operator but it holds equally for the operator
K_{T,B}-V(x-y)
in (
5.12
) because
V
intertwines as
T(y)^{*}V(x)T(y)=V(x-y)
with the magnetic translations
T(y)
in (
1.3
).

Lemma 5.4
.

Let Assumptions
1.3
and
1.5
be true.
For any
D_{0}\geqslant 0
, there are constants
B_{0}>0
and
T_{0}>0
such that for
0<B\leqslant B_{0}
and
T>0
with
T-T_{\mathrm{c}}\geqslant-D_{0}B
, the estimate

\displaystyle K_{T,B}-V

\displaystyle\geqslant c\;(1-P)(1+\pi^{2})(1-P)+c\,\min\{T_{0},(T-T_{\mathrm{c}})_{+}\}-CB

(5.13)

holds. Here,
P=|\alpha_{*}\rangle\langle\alpha_{*}|
is the orthogonal projection onto the ground state
\alpha_{*}
of
K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V
.

Proof.

We prove two lower bounds on
K_{T,B}-V
, which we add up to etablish (
5.13
).

Step 1.
We claim that there are
T_{0},c,C>0
such that

\displaystyle K_{T,B}-V\geqslant c\;\min\{T_{0},(T-T_{\mathrm{c}})_{+}\}-CB.

(5.14)

To prove (
5.14
), we note that the derivative of the symbol
K_{T}
in (
1.14
) with respect to
T
equals

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}T}K_{T}(p)=2\;\frac{(\frac{p^{2}-\mu}{2T})^{2}}{\sinh^{2}(\frac{p^{2}-\mu}{2T})}

(5.15)

and is bounded from above by 2. If
T\leqslant T_{\mathrm{c}}
, we infer
K_{T,B}-K_{T_{\mathrm{c}},B}\geqslant-2D_{0}B
as an operator inequality, which, in combination with Proposition
A.1
in the appendix, proves (
5.14
) in this case. To treat the case
T\geqslant T_{\mathrm{c}}
, we denote by
e_{0}^{T,B}
and
e_{1}^{T,B}
the lowest and the second lowest eigenvalue of the operator
K_{T,B}-V
, respectively. Also let
P_{T,B}
be the spectral projection corresponding to
e_{0}^{T,B}
and define
Q_{T,B}=1-P_{T,B}
. We have

\displaystyle K_{T,B}-V\geqslant e_{0}^{T,B}P_{T,B}+e_{1}^{T,B}Q_{T,B}.

Since
K_{T}(p)-K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(p)\geqslant 0
for all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which follows from (
5.15
), we know the lower bound
e_{1}^{T,B}\geqslant e_{1}^{T_{\mathrm{c}},B}\geqslant\kappa
for some
\kappa>0
. Here, the second inequality follows from Proposition
A.1
. From Proposition
A.1
we also know that the lowest eigenvalue of
K_{T,B}-V
is simple. According to (
5.15
), the function
T\mapsto K_{T}(p)
is increasing and has a non-vanishing derivative for each
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Analytic perturbation theory therefore implies the lower bound
e_{0}^{T,B}\geqslant e_{0}^{T_{\mathrm{c}},B}+c(T-T_{\mathrm{c}})
for some
c>0
as long as
|T-T_{\mathrm{c}}|
is small enough. Since Proposition
A.1
shows
e_{0}^{T_{\mathrm{c}},B}\geqslant-CB
these consideration prove (
5.14
) in the case
T\geqslant T_{\mathrm{c}}
.

Step 2.
We claim there are
c,C>0
such that

\displaystyle K_{T,B}-V\geqslant c\;(1-P)(1+\pi^{2})(1-P)-CB.

(5.16)

From the arguments in Step 1 we know that we can replace
T
by
T_{\mathrm{c}}
for a lower bound if we allow for a remainder of the size
-CB
. To prove (
5.16
), we choose
0<\eta<1
and write

\displaystyle K_{T_{\mathrm{c}},B}-V=e_{0}^{B}P_{B}+(1-P_{B})[(1-\eta)K_{T_{\mathrm{c}},B}-V](1-P_{B})+\eta(1-P_{B})K_{T_{\mathrm{c}},B}(1-P_{B}),

(5.17)

where
e_{0}^{B}
denotes the ground state energy of
K_{T_{\mathrm{c}},B}-V
and
P_{B}=|\alpha_{*}^{B}\rangle\langle\alpha_{*}^{B}|
is the spectral projection onto the corresponding unique ground state vector
\alpha_{*}^{B}
. From Proposition
A.1
we know that the first term on the right side of (
5.17
) is bounded from below by
-CB
. The lowest eigenvalue of
K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V
is simple and isolated from the rest of the spectrum. Proposition
A.1
therefore implies that the second term in (
5.17
) is nonnegative as long as
\eta
is, independently of
B
, chosen small enough, and can be dropped for a lower bound.
To treat the third term, we note that the symbol
K_{T}(p)
in (
1.14
) satisfies the inequality
K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(p)\geqslant c^{\prime}(1+p^{2})
for some constant
c^{\prime}
, and hence
K_{T_{\mathrm{c}},B}\geqslant c^{\prime}(1+\pi^{2})
. In combination, the above considerations prove

\displaystyle K_{T_{\mathrm{c}},B}-V\geqslant c^{\prime}\;(1-P_{B})(1+\pi^{2})(1-P_{B})-CB.

It remains to replace
P_{B}
by
P=|\alpha_{*}\rangle\langle\alpha_{*}|
. To this end, we write

\displaystyle(1-P_{B})(1+\pi^{2})(1-P_{B})-(1-P)(1+\pi^{2})(1-P)

\displaystyle\hskip-160.0pt=(P-P_{B})+(P-P_{B})\pi^{2}(1-P_{B})+(1-P)\pi^{2}(P-P_{B}).

(5.18)

From Proposition
A.1
we know that
\|P_{B}-P\|_{\infty}\leqslant CB
and
\|\pi^{2}(P_{B}-P)\|_{\infty}\leqslant CB
. Hence, the norm of the operator on the right side of (
5.18
) is bounded by a constant times
B
. This shows (
5.16
) and concludes our proof.
∎

We deduce two corollaries from (
5.12
) and Lemma
5.4
. The first statement is an a priori bound that we use in the proof of Theorem
2
(b).

Corollary 5.5
.

Let Assumptions
1.3
and
1.5
be true. Then, there are constants
B_{0}>0
and
C>0
such that for all
0<B\leqslant B_{0}
and all temperatures
T\geqslant T_{\mathrm{c}}(1+CB)
, we have
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{0})>0
unless
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
D_{0}>0
and assume that
T\geqslant T_{\mathrm{c}}(1+D_{0}B)
. From (
5.12
) and Lemma
5.4
we know that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}(\Gamma_{0})\geqslant(c\,\min\{T_{0},T_{\mathrm{c}}D_{0}B\}-CB)\,\|\alpha\|_{2}^{2}.

(5.19)

For the choice
D_{0}=\frac{2C}{cT_{\mathrm{c}}}
and
B_{0}=\frac{T_{0}}{D_{0}T_{\mathrm{c}}}
the right side of (
5.19
) is strictly positive unless
\alpha=0
. We conclude that
\Gamma_{0}
is the unique minimizer of
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathbf{B},T}
, which proves the claim.
∎

The second corollary provides a bound for the Cooper pair wave functions of low-energy BCS states in the sense of (
5.1
). It is based upon (
5.12
) and to state it we need to introduce the operator

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}}.

(5.20)

We highlight that it acts on both, the relative coordinate
r=x-y
and the center-of-mass coordinate
X=\frac{x+y}{2}
of a function
\alpha(x,y)
.

Corollary 5.6
.

Let Assumptions
1.3
and
1.5
be true. For any
D_{0},D_{1}\geqslant 0
, there is a constant
B_{0}>0
such that if
\Gamma
satisfies (
5.1
), if
0<B\leqslant B_{0}
, and if
T
is such that
T-T_{\mathrm{c}}\geqslant-D_{0}B
, then
\alpha=\Gamma_{12}
obeys

\displaystyle\langle\alpha,[U(1-P)(1+\pi_{r}^{2})(1-P)U^{*}+U^{*}(1-P)(1+\pi_{r}^{2})(1-P)U]\alpha\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 200.0pt+\Tr\bigl[(\alpha^{*}\alpha)^{2}\bigr]\leqslant CB\|\alpha\|_{2}^{2}+D_{1}B^{2},

(5.21)

where
P=|\alpha_{*}\rangle\langle\alpha_{*}|
and
\pi_{r}=-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{r}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{B}\wedge r
both act on the relative coordinate.

In the statement of the corollary and in the following, we refrain from equipping the projection
P=|\alpha_{*}\rangle\langle\alpha_{*}|
with an index
r
although it acts on the relative coordinate. This should not lead to confusion and keeps the formulas readable.

Proof.

We recall that the operator
V
acts by multiplication with
V(x-y)
and that
K_{T}(p)
is defined in (
1.14
). Using
\alpha(x,y)=\alpha(y,x)
we write

\displaystyle\Tr\bigl[(K_{T,B}-V)\alpha\alpha^{*}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}y\;\overline{\alpha(x,y)}\bigl[(K_{T}(\pi_{x})-V)+(K_{T}(\pi_{y})-V)\bigr]\alpha(x,y).

(5.22)

We note that
\pi_{x}=\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{X}+\widetilde{\pi}_{r}=U\pi_{r}U^{*}
and
\pi_{y}=\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{X}-\widetilde{\pi}_{r}=-U^{*}\pi_{r}U
, with
\widetilde{\pi}_{r}
and
\Pi_{X}
in (
2.10
). Using the above identities we see that

\displaystyle K_{T}(\pi_{x})-V(r)

\displaystyle=U(K_{T}(\pi_{r})-V(r))U^{*},

\displaystyle K_{T}(\pi_{y})-V(r)

\displaystyle=U^{*}(K_{T}(\pi_{r})-V(r))U.

(5.23)

The result follows from a short computation or from Lemma
5.11
below.
We combine (
5.1
), (
5.12
), (
5.22
) and (
5.23
) to show the inequality

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\langle\alpha,[U(K_{T}(\pi_{r})-V(r))U^{*}+U^{*}(K_{T}(\pi_{r})-V(r))U]\alpha\rangle+c\Tr\bigl[(\alpha^{*}\alpha)^{2}\bigr]\leqslant D_{1}B^{2}.

Finally, we apply Lemma
5.4
to the first term on the left side and obtain (
5.21
).
∎

5.2
The first decomposition result

The proof of Theorem
5.1
is based on Corollary
5.6
and is given in two steps. In the first step we drop the second term on the left side of (
5.21
) for a lower bound, and investigate the implications of the resulting inequality for
\alpha
. The result of the corresponding analysis is summarized in Proposition
5.7
below. The second term on the left side of (
5.21
) is used later in Lemma
5.14
.

Proposition 5.7
.

Given
D_{0},D_{1}\geqslant 0
, there is
B_{0}>0
with the following properties. If, for some
0<B\leqslant B_{0}
, the wave function
\alpha\in{L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
satisfies

\displaystyle\langle\alpha,[U^{*}(1-P)(1+\pi_{r}^{2})(1-P)U+U(1-P)(1+\pi_{r}^{2})(1-P)U^{*}]\alpha\rangle

\displaystyle\leqslant D_{0}B\|\alpha\|_{2}^{2}+D_{1}B^{2},

(5.24)

then there are
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{B})
and
\xi_{0}\in{H^{1}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
such that

\displaystyle\alpha(X,r)=\alpha_{*}(r)\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\cdot\Pi_{X}\bigr)\Psi(X)+\xi_{0}(X,r)

(5.25)

with

\displaystyle\langle\Psi,\Pi^{2}\Psi\rangle+\|\xi_{0}\|_{{H^{1}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}}^{2}\leqslant C\bigl(B\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}+D_{1}B^{2}\bigr).

(5.26)

Before we give the proof of the a priori estimates in Proposition
5.7
, we define the decomposition of
\alpha
, explain the idea behind it, and discuss relations to the existing literature. For this purpose, let the operator
A\colon{L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}\rightarrow L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
be given by

\displaystyle(A\alpha)(X):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\alpha_{*}(r)\;\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\cdot\Pi_{X}\bigr)\alpha(X,r).

(5.27)

A short computation shows that its adjoint
A^{*}\colon L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})\rightarrow{L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
is given by

\displaystyle(A^{*}\Psi)(X,r)=\alpha_{*}(r)\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\cdot\Pi_{X}\bigr)\Psi(X).

(5.28)

We highlight that this is the form of the first term in (
5.25
). For a given Cooper pair wave function
\alpha
, we use these operators to define the two functions
\Psi
and
\xi_{0}
by

\displaystyle\Psi

\displaystyle:=(AA^{*})^{-1}A\alpha,

\displaystyle\xi_{0}

\displaystyle:=\alpha-A^{*}\Psi.

(5.29)

Lemma
5.8
below guarantees that
AA^{*}
is invertible, and we readily check that (
5.25
) holds with these definitions. Moreover, this decomposition of
\alpha
is orthogonal in the sense that
\langle A^{*}\Psi,\xi_{0}\rangle=0
holds. The claimed orthogonality follows from

\displaystyle A\xi_{0}=0,

(5.30)

which is a direct consequence of (
5.29
). In the following we motivate our choice for
\Psi
and
\xi_{0}
and comment on its appearance in the literature.

The decomposition of
\alpha
is motivated by the minimization problem for the low-energy operator
2-UPU^{*}-U^{*}PU
, that is, the operator in (
5.24
) with
\pi_{r}^{2}
replaced by zero. The operators
UPU^{*}
and
U^{*}PU
act as
A^{*}A
on the space
{L^{2}(Q_{B}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
of reflection symmetric functions in the relative coordinate. If
\Pi
is replaced by
P_{X}
in the definition of
U
then
A^{*}A
can be written as

A^{*}A\cong\int^{\oplus}_{[0,\sqrt{2\pi B}]^{3}}\mathrm{d}P_{X}\;|a_{P_{X}}\rangle\langle a_{P_{X}}|,

(5.31)

with
|a_{P_{X}}\rangle\langle a_{P_{X}}|
the orthogonal projection onto the function
a_{P_{X}}(r)=\cos(r/2\cdot P_{X})\alpha_{*}(r)
. Here the variable
P_{X}
is the dual of the center-of-mass coordinate
X
in the sense of Fourier transformation and
r
denotes the relative coordinate. That is, the function
a_{P_{X}}(r)
minimizes
1-A^{*}A
in each fiber, whence it is the eigenfunction with respect to the lowest eigenvalue of
1-A^{*}A=1-(UPU^{*}+U^{*}PU)/2
. This discussion should be compared to
[
19
, Eq. (5.47)]
and the discussion before Lemma 20 in
[
16
]
.

If we replace
P_{X}
by the magnetic momentum operator
\Pi
again the above picture changes because the components of
\Pi
cannot be diagonalized simultaneously (they do not commute), and hence (
5.31
) has no obvious equivalent in this case. The decomposition of
\alpha
in terms of the operators
A
and
A^{*}
above has been introduced in
[
17
]
in order to study the operator
1-V^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}L_{T,B}V^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
with
L_{T,B}
in (
3.15
), see also the discussion below Theorem
3.5
. The situation in this work is comparable to our case with
\pi_{r}^{2}
replaced by zero in (
5.24
). Our analysis below shows that the ansatz (
5.29
) is useful even if the full range of energies is considered, that is, if
\pi_{r}^{2}
is present in (
5.24
).

In the following lemma we collect useful properties of the operator
AA^{*}
. It should be compared to
[
17
, Lemma 27]
.

Lemma 5.8
.

The operators

\displaystyle AA^{*}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\alpha_{*}(r)^{2}\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\cdot\Pi\bigr),

\displaystyle 1-AA^{*}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\alpha_{*}(r)^{2}\sin^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\cdot\Pi\bigr)

on
L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
are both bounded nonnegative functions of
\Pi^{2}
and satisfy the following properties:

(a)

0\leqslant AA^{*}\leqslant 1
and
0\leqslant 1-AA^{*}\leqslant 1
.

(b)

There is a constant
c>0
such that
AA^{*}\geqslant c
and
1-AA^{*}\geqslant c\;\Pi^{2}\;(1+\Pi^{2})^{-1}
.

In particular,
AA^{*}
and
1-AA^{*}
are boundedly invertible on
L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
.

Proof.

Part (a) is a direct consequence of the fact that
\|\alpha_{*}\|_{2}=1
. In the following we reduce the proof of part (b) to known results in
[
17
]
. To this end, we introduce the operator

\displaystyle R:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\alpha_{*}(r)^{2}\;\cos(r\cdot\Pi)

(5.32)

and note that

\displaystyle AA^{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(1+R),

\displaystyle 1-AA^{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(1-R).

Let us also denote by
\widetilde{R}
the operator in (
5.32
) but with
\Pi
replaced by
\widetilde{\Pi}
, which is the same operator but understood to act on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
instead of
L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
. In Lemma 28 in
[
17
]
it has been shown that
\widetilde{R}
is a function of
\widetilde{\Pi}^{2}
. Moreover, the statement of Lemma 27 in
[
17
]
is equivalent to

\displaystyle 1-\widetilde{R}^{2}\geqslant c\,\frac{\widetilde{\Pi}^{2}}{1+\widetilde{\Pi}^{2}}

(5.33)

for some
0<c<1
, and Eq. (55) in the same reference implies

\displaystyle|\widetilde{R}|\leqslant 1-c.

(5.34)

In combination, (
5.33
), (
5.34
), and
\widetilde{R}\leqslant 1
show

\displaystyle 1+\widetilde{R}

\displaystyle\geqslant c,

\displaystyle 1-\widetilde{R}\geqslant\frac{1-\widetilde{R}^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{c}{2}\frac{\widetilde{\Pi}^{2}}{1+\widetilde{\Pi}^{2}}.

(5.35)

It remains to argue that
R
is a function of
\Pi^{2}
and that a version of (
5.35
) with
\widetilde{R}
and
\widetilde{\Pi}
replaced by
R
and
\Pi
holds.

The fact that
R
is a function of
\Pi^{2}
follows from the argument that we used to show that the same statement is true for the operator in (
4.155
). To show that (
5.35
) with
\widetilde{R}
and
\widetilde{\Pi}
replaced by
R
and
\Pi
holds, we conjugate both sides of the inequalities with the Bloch-Floquet transformation in (
4.156
) and (
4.157
), and use (
4.158
). The inequalities in (
5.35
) therefore hold equally in any fiber, that is, with
\widetilde{\Pi}
on the left and on the right sides replaced by
\Pi(k)=\Pi+k
acting on
L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
. Since
\widetilde{\Pi}(0)=\Pi
, this proves the claim.
∎

The remainder of this subsection is devoted to the proof of Proposition
5.7
. We start with a lower bound on the operator in (
5.24
) when it acts on wave functions of the form
A^{*}\Psi
, see Lemma
5.9
below.

5.2.1
Step one – lower bound on the range of
A^{*}

The main result of this subsection is the following lemma.

Lemma 5.9
.

For any
\Psi\in L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
, with
A
and
A^{*}
given by (
5.27
) and (
5.28
), with
U
given by (
5.20
), and
P=|\alpha_{*}\rangle\langle\alpha_{*}|
with
\alpha_{*}
from (
1.15
) acting on the relative coordinate, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\langle A^{*}\Psi,[U^{*}(1-P)(1+\pi_{r}^{2})(1-P)U+U(1-P)(1+\pi_{r}^{2})(1-P)U^{*}]A^{*}\Psi\rangle\hskip-340.0pt

\displaystyle=\langle\Psi,AA^{*}(1-AA^{*})(1+\Pi^{2})\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt+\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{(1-AA^{*})\Psi(X)}\;|\nabla\alpha_{*}(r)|^{2}\;\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;(1-AA^{*})\Psi(X)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt+\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{AA^{*}\Psi(X)}\;|\nabla\alpha_{*}(r)|^{2}\;\sin^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;AA^{*}\Psi(X)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt+\frac{1}{4}\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{(1-AA^{*})\Psi(X)}\;|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|^{2}\alpha_{*}(r)^{2}\;\sin^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;(1-AA^{*})\Psi(X)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt+\frac{1}{4}\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{AA^{*}\Psi(X)}\;|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|^{2}\alpha_{*}(r)^{2}\;\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;AA^{*}\Psi(X).

(5.36)

In particular, we have the lower bound

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\langle A^{*}\Psi,[U^{*}(1-P)(1+\pi_{r}^{2})(1-P)U+U(1-P)(1+\pi_{r}^{2})(1-P)U^{*}]A^{*}\Psi\rangle\geqslant c\,\langle\Psi,\Pi^{2}\Psi\rangle.

(5.37)

Remark 5.10
.

Let us replace
\pi_{r}^{2}
on the left side of (
5.36
) by zero for the moment. In this case, the substitute of (
5.36
) reads

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\langle A^{*}\Psi,[U^{*}(1-P)U+U(1-P)U^{*}]A^{*}\Psi\rangle=\langle\Psi,AA^{*}(1-AA^{*})\Psi\rangle.

(5.38)

It follows from Lemma
5.8
that the operator
AA^{*}(1-AA^{*})
is bounded from below by
\Pi^{2}
only for small values of
\Pi^{2}
, which is not enough for the proof of Proposition
5.7
. This justifies the term “low-energy operator” for
1-A^{*}A
, which we used earlier in the discussion below (
5.28
). The additional factor
1+\Pi^{2}
in the first term on the right side of (
5.36
) compensates for the problematic behavior of (
5.38
) for high energies. The expression on the right side of (
5.38
) also appears in
[
17
]
.

Before we give the proof of Lemma
5.9
, we prove two technical lemmas, which provide intertwining relations for various magnetic momentum operators with
U
and linear combinations of
U
. A part of the relations in the first lemma can be found in
[
17
, Lemma 24]
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
p_{r}:=-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{r}
,
\pi_{r}=p_{r}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{B}\wedge r
and
\widetilde{\pi}_{r}
and
\Pi_{X}
be given by (
2.10
). With
U
in (
5.20
), we have the following intertwining relations:

\displaystyle\begin{split}U\Pi_{X}U^{*}&=\Pi_{X}-\mathbf{B}\wedge r,\phantom{\frac{1}{2}}\\
U^{*}\Pi_{X}U&=\Pi_{X}+\mathbf{B}\wedge r,\phantom{\frac{1}{2}}\end{split}

\displaystyle\begin{split}U\pi_{r}U^{*}&=\widetilde{\pi}_{r}+\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{X},\\
U^{*}\pi_{r}U&=\widetilde{\pi}_{r}-\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{X},\phantom{\frac{1}{2}}\end{split}

\displaystyle\begin{split}U\widetilde{\pi}_{r}U^{*}&=p_{r}+\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{X},\\
U^{*}\widetilde{\pi}_{r}U&=p_{r}-\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{X}.\end{split}

Proof.

Let us denote
P_{X}:=-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X}
. We use the fact that
r\cdot P_{X}
commutes with
r\cdot(\mathbf{B}\wedge X)
to see that

\displaystyle U^{*}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot(X\wedge r)}\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{r}{2}P_{X}}

(5.39)

holds. To prove the first intertwining relation with
\Pi_{X}
, we compute

\displaystyle\Pi_{X}U^{*}

\displaystyle=(P_{X}+\mathbf{B}\wedge X)U^{*}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot(X\wedge r)}\Bigl[P_{X}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{B}\wedge r+\mathbf{B}\wedge X\Bigr]\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{r}{2}P_{X}}

\displaystyle=U^{*}\Bigl[P_{X}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{B}\wedge r+\mathbf{B}\wedge\bigl(X-\frac{r}{2}\bigr)\Bigr]=U^{*}[\Pi_{X}-\mathbf{B}\wedge r].

Here we used that
f(X)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{r}{2}P_{X}}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{r}{2}P_{X}}\,f(X-\frac{r}{2})
. The second intertwining relation with
\Pi_{X}
is obtained by replacing
r
by
-r
.

Next we consider the first intertwining relation with
\pi_{r}
and compute

\displaystyle\pi_{r}U^{*}

\displaystyle=\bigl(p_{r}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{B}\wedge r\bigr)U^{*}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot(X\wedge r)}\Bigl[p_{r}+(-\mathrm{i})\mathrm{i}\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{B}\wedge X+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{B}\wedge r\Bigr]\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{r}{2}P_{X}}

\displaystyle=U^{*}\Bigl[p_{r}+\frac{P_{X}}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{B}\wedge\bigl(X-\frac{r}{2}\bigr)+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{B}\wedge r\Bigr]=U^{*}\Bigl[\widetilde{\pi}_{r}+\frac{\Pi_{X}}{2}\Bigr].

The remaining relations can be proved similarly and we skip the details.
∎

Lemma 5.12
.

(a)

We have the following intertwining relations for
\Pi_{X}
:

\displaystyle\Pi_{X}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\Pi_{X}-\mathrm{i}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\mathbf{B}\wedge r,

(5.40)

\displaystyle\Pi_{X}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\Pi_{X}+\mathrm{i}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\mathbf{B}\wedge r.

(5.41)

(b)

The operators
p_{r}
,
\widetilde{\pi}_{r}
and
\pi_{r}
intertwine according to

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}_{r}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)p_{r}+\mathrm{i}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\frac{\Pi_{X}}{2},

(5.42)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}_{r}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\pi_{r}+\mathrm{i}\frac{\Pi_{X}}{2}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr),

(5.43)

and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}_{r}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)p_{r}-\mathrm{i}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\frac{\Pi_{X}}{2},

(5.44)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}_{r}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\pi_{r}-\mathrm{i}\frac{\Pi_{X}}{2}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)

(5.45)

It will be useful in the proof of Lemma
5.9
to have displayed both, (
5.42
) and (
5.43
) as well as (
5.44
) and (
5.45
), even though they follow trivially from each other and (
5.40
) or (
5.41
).

Proof.

The proof is a direct consequence of the representations

\displaystyle\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(U^{*}+U),

\displaystyle\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}}(U^{*}-U),

(5.46)

and the intertwining relations in Lemma
5.12
. We omit the details.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.9
.

The proof is a tedious computation that is based on the intertwining relations in Lemma
5.12
.
We start by defining

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{1}

\displaystyle:=U^{*}\pi_{r}^{2}U+U\pi_{r}^{2}U^{*}=2\,\widetilde{\pi}_{r}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\,\Pi_{X}^{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{2}

\displaystyle:=U^{*}P\pi_{r}^{2}PU+UP\pi_{r}^{2}PU^{*},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{3}

\displaystyle:=U^{*}P\pi_{r}^{2}U+UP\pi_{r}^{2}U^{*},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{4}

\displaystyle:=U^{*}\pi_{r}^{2}PU+U\pi_{r}^{2}PU^{*}.

(5.47)

Then, (
5.36
) can be written as

\displaystyle\langle A^{*}\Psi,[U^{*}(1-P)(1+\pi_{r}^{2})(1-P)U+U(1-P)(1+\pi_{r}^{2})(1-P)U^{*}]A^{*}\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip-330.0pt=2\langle A^{*}\Psi,(1-A^{*}A)A^{*}\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip-300.0pt+\langle A^{*}\Psi,\mathcal{T}_{1}A^{*}\Psi\rangle+\langle A^{*}\Psi,\mathcal{T}_{2}A^{*}\Psi\rangle-\langle A^{*}\Psi,\mathcal{T}_{3}A^{*}\Psi\rangle-\langle A^{*}\Psi,\mathcal{T}_{4}A^{*}\Psi\rangle.

(5.48)

The first term on the right side equals twice the term in (
5.38
), which is in its final form.

We start by computing the
\Pi_{X}^{2}
term in
\mathcal{T}_{1}
, which reads

\displaystyle\langle A^{*}\Psi,\Pi_{X}^{2}A^{*}\Psi\rangle=\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{\Psi(X)}\alpha^{*}(r)\;\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\Pi_{X}^{2}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X).

Our goal is to move
\Pi_{X}^{2}
to the right. To that end, we apply (
5.40
) twice and obtain

\displaystyle\Pi_{X}^{2}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)=\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\Pi_{X}^{2}-\mathrm{i}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\Pi_{X}\mathbf{B}\wedge r-\mathrm{i}\Pi_{X}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\mathbf{B}\wedge r.

We multiply this from the left with
\cos(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X})
, use (
5.40
) to commute
\Pi_{X}
to the left in the last term, and find

\displaystyle\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\Pi_{X}^{2}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\Pi_{X}^{2}+\sin^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-60.0pt-\mathrm{i}\bigl[\Pi_{X}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)+\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\Pi_{X}\bigr]\mathbf{B}\wedge r.

(5.49)

The operator
|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|^{2}
in the second term on the right side commutes with
\sin^{2}(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X})
. The operator in square brackets is self-adjoint and commutes with
\mathbf{B}\wedge r
. When we add (
5.49
) and its own adjoint, we obtain

\displaystyle\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\Pi_{X}^{2}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip-50.0pt=\frac{1}{2}\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\Pi_{X}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\,\Pi_{X}^{2}\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)+\sin^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|^{2}.

(5.50)

We evaluate (
5.50
) in the inner product with
\alpha_{*}\Phi
and
\alpha_{*}\Psi
on the left and right side, respectively, use the fact that
AA^{*}
commutes with
\Pi^{2}
, see Lemma
5.8
, and obtain

\displaystyle\langle A^{*}\Phi,\Pi_{X}^{2}A^{*}\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\Phi,AA^{*}\Pi^{2}\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt+\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{\Phi(X)}\;|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|^{2}\alpha_{*}(r)^{2}\;\sin^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\Psi(X).

(5.51)

When we choose
\Phi=\Psi
we obtain the result for the term proportional to
\Pi_{X}^{2}
in
\mathcal{T}_{1}
.

Next, we investigate the term proportional to
\widetilde{\pi}_{r}^{2}
in
\mathcal{T}_{1}
. We use (
5.43
) to move the operators
\widetilde{\pi}_{r}
from the middle to the outer positions and find

\displaystyle\langle A^{*}\Psi,\widetilde{\pi}_{r}^{2}A^{*}\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{\Psi(X)}\alpha_{*}(r)\left[\pi_{r}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)-\mathrm{i}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\frac{\Pi_{X}}{2}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 80.0pt\times\left[\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\pi_{r}+\mathrm{i}\frac{\Pi_{X}}{2}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\right]\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X).

(5.52)

We multiply out the brackets and obtain four terms. The terms proportional to
\cos^{2}
and
\sin^{2}
read

\displaystyle\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{\Psi(X)}\;|\pi_{r}\alpha_{*}(r)|^{2}\;\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\Psi(X)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt+\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{\Psi(X)}\;\alpha_{*}(r)\;\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\frac{\Pi_{X}^{2}}{4}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\alpha_{*}(r)\;\Psi(X).

(5.53)

For the moment the second line remains untouched. It is going to be canceled by a term in (
5.57
) below. The term in the first line equals

\displaystyle\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{\Psi(X)}\;|\nabla\alpha_{*}(r)|^{2}\;\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\Psi(X)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt+\frac{1}{4}\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{\Psi(X)}\;|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|^{2}\alpha_{*}(r)^{2}\;\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\Psi(X).

(5.54)

To obtain this result, we used
(\nabla\alpha_{*})(r)\cdot\mathbf{B}\wedge r=0
, which holds because
\alpha_{*}
is radial. This term is in its final form.

Now we have a closer look at the terms proportional to
\sin
times
\cos
in (
5.52
). The operator inside the relevant quadratic form is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\pi_{r}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\frac{\Pi_{X}}{2}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)-\mathrm{i}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\frac{\Pi_{X}}{2}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\pi_{r}.

(5.55)

We intend to interchange
\sin(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X})
and
\cos(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X})
in the first term. To do this, we use (
5.41
) to move
\Pi_{X}
out of the center so that the first term equals

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\pi_{r}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\frac{\Pi_{X}}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\pi_{r}\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\mathbf{B}\wedge r.

In the first term we may now commute the sine and the cosine and use (
5.40
) and (
5.45
) to bring
\pi_{r}
and
\Pi_{X}
in the center again. We also move
\pi_{r}
into the center in the second term in (
5.55
). As a result, (
5.55
) equals

\displaystyle\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\frac{\Pi_{X}^{2}}{4}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)-\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\frac{\Pi_{X}^{2}}{4}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.0pt+\mathrm{i}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\frac{\Pi_{X}}{4}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\mathbf{B}\wedge r

\displaystyle\hskip 80.0pt-\frac{1}{2}\pi_{r}\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\mathbf{B}\wedge r-\frac{1}{2}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\widetilde{\pi}_{r}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\mathbf{B}\wedge r.

(5.56)

We use (
5.45
) to move
\widetilde{\pi}_{r}
to the left in the last term in (
5.56
). One of the terms we obtain in this way cancels the third term in (
5.56
). We also use
\cos(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X})^{2}+\sin(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X})^{2}=1
to rewrite the fourth term in (
5.56
). In combination, these considerations imply that the terms in (
5.56
) equal

\displaystyle\begin{split}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\frac{\Pi_{X}^{2}}{4}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)-\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\frac{\Pi_{X}^{2}}{4}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)-\frac{1}{2}\pi_{r}\mathbf{B}\wedge r.\end{split}

(5.57)

The expectation of the second term with respect to
\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)
cancels the second term in (
5.53
). We multiply the last term from the left and from the right with
\alpha_{*}(r)
, integrate over
r
and find

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\alpha_{*}(r)\pi_{r}\mathbf{B}\wedge r\alpha_{*}(r)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\overline{p_{r}\alpha_{*}(r)}\mathbf{B}\wedge r\alpha_{*}(r)+\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|^{2}\alpha_{*}(r)^{2}.

(5.58)

The first term on the right side vanishes because
\alpha_{*}
is radial, see the remark below (
5.53
). Let us summarize where we are. We combine (
5.52
)-(
5.58
) to see that

\displaystyle\langle A^{*}\Psi,\widetilde{\pi}_{r}^{2}A^{*}\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{\Psi(X)}\;|\nabla\alpha_{*}(r)|^{2}\;\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\Psi(X)

\displaystyle\hskip-30.0pt+\frac{1}{4}\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{\Psi(X)}\;|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|^{2}\alpha_{*}(r)^{2}\;\bigl(\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)-1\bigr)\;\Psi(X)

\displaystyle\hskip-30.0pt+\frac{1}{4}\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)}\;\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\Pi_{X}^{2}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X).

(5.59)

The term in the last line equals
\langle A^{*}\Psi,\Pi_{X}^{2}A^{*}\Psi\rangle
and we use (
5.51
) to rewrite it. This yields

\displaystyle\langle A^{*}\Psi,\widetilde{\pi}_{r}^{2}A^{*}\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\langle\Psi,AA^{*}\Pi^{2}\Psi\rangle+\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{\Psi(X)}\;|\nabla\alpha_{*}(r)|^{2}\;\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\Psi(X).

In combination with (
5.47
) and (
5.51
), this yields

\displaystyle\langle A^{*}\Psi,\mathcal{T}_{1}A^{*}\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\Psi,AA^{*}\Pi^{2}\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt+2\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{\Psi(X)}\;|\nabla\alpha_{*}(r)|^{2}\;\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\Psi(X)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt+\frac{1}{2}\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{\Psi(X)}\;|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|^{2}\alpha_{*}(r)^{2}\;\sin^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\Psi(X)

(5.60)

and completes our computation of the term involving
\mathcal{T}_{1}
.

A short computation shows that

\langle A^{*}\Psi,\mathcal{T}_{2}A^{*}\Psi\rangle=2\,\langle AA^{*}\Psi,AA^{*}\Psi\rangle\Bigl[\|\nabla\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|^{2}\alpha_{*}(r)^{2}\Bigr].

(5.61)

It remains to compute the terms in (
5.48
) involving the operators
\mathcal{T}_{3}
and
\mathcal{T}_{4}
, where
\mathcal{T}_{4}^{*}=\mathcal{T}_{3}
.

In the following we compute the term with
\mathcal{T}_{3}
. A short computation, which uses the fact that
\alpha_{*}
is radial, shows

\displaystyle\langle A^{*}\Psi,\mathcal{T}_{3}A^{*}\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\alpha_{*}AA^{*}\Psi,\pi_{r}^{2}(U^{*}+U)A^{*}\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip-30.0pt=2\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{AA^{*}\Psi(X)}\;\overline{p_{r}\alpha_{*}(r)}\;p_{r}\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\alpha_{*}(r)\;\Psi(X)

\displaystyle\hskip-10.0pt+\frac{1}{2}\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{AA^{*}\Psi(X)}\;|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|^{2}\alpha_{*}(r)^{2}\;\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\Psi(X).

(5.62)

The second term on the right side is in its final form and will be canceled by a term below. We continue with the first term, use (
5.42
) twice to commute
p_{r}
with the squared cosine, as well as (
5.40
) and (
5.41
) to commute
\Pi_{X}
to the center in the emerging terms, and find

\displaystyle p_{r}\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)p_{r}+\mathrm{i}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\Pi_{X}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{B}\wedge r.

(5.63)

We note that the last term, when inserted back into (
5.62
), vanishes because
\alpha_{*}
is radial. The first term is final and its quadratic form with
p_{r}\alpha_{*}AA^{*}\Psi
and
\alpha_{*}\Psi
reads

\displaystyle 2\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{AA^{*}\Psi(X)}\;|\nabla\alpha_{*}(r)|^{2}\;\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\Psi(X).

(5.64)

Let us continue with the second term on the right side of (
5.63
). We multiply it with
p_{r}
from the left and use (
5.42
) and (
5.44
) to commute
p_{r}
to the right. In the two emerging terms we bring
\Pi_{X}
to the center and obtain

\displaystyle p_{r}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\Pi_{X}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\Pi_{X}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)p_{r}+\mathrm{i}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\frac{\Pi_{X}^{2}}{2}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 145.0pt-\mathrm{i}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\frac{\Pi_{X}^{2}}{2}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr).

We plug the second term of (
5.63
), written in this form, back into (
5.62
) and obtain

\displaystyle 2\mathrm{i}\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{AA^{*}\Psi(X)}\;\overline{p_{r}\alpha_{*}(r)}\;\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\Pi_{X}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\alpha_{*}(r)\;\Psi(X)\hskip-350.0pt

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{i}\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{AA^{*}\Psi(X)}\;\alpha_{*}(r)\;\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\Pi_{X}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;p_{r}\alpha_{*}(r)\;\Psi(X)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt+\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{AA^{*}\Psi(X)}\;\alpha_{*}(r)\;\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\Pi_{X}^{2}\cos\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\alpha_{*}(r)\;\Psi(X)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt-\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{AA^{*}\Psi(X)}\;\alpha_{*}(r)\;\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\Pi_{X}^{2}\sin\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\alpha_{*}(r)\;\Psi(X).

(5.65)

Notice that the first term on the right side equals
(-1)
times the term on the left side. Thus, the left side equals
\frac{1}{2}
times the third line plus the fourth line. To compute the third line of (
5.65
) we use (
5.51
) with the choice
\Phi=AA^{*}\Psi
. A short computation shows that (
5.50
) holds equally with
\cos
and
\sin
interchanged. Accordingly,
(-1)
times the fourth line of (
5.65
) equals

\langle AA^{*}\Psi,(1-AA^{*})\Pi^{2}\Psi\rangle+\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{AA^{*}\Psi(X)}\;|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|^{2}\alpha_{*}(r)^{2}\;\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\Psi(X).

In combination, these considerations imply that the left side of (
5.65
) is given by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\langle AA^{*}\Psi,AA^{*}\Pi^{2}\Psi\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle AA^{*}\Psi,(1-AA^{*})\Pi^{2}\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt+\frac{1}{2}\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{AA^{*}\Psi(X)}\;|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|^{2}\alpha_{*}(r)^{2}\;\sin^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\Psi(X)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt-\frac{1}{2}\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{AA^{*}\Psi(X)}\;|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|^{2}\alpha_{*}(r)^{2}\;\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\Psi(X).

(5.66)

We note that the third term in (
5.66
) cancels the second term in (
5.62
). Adding all this to (
5.64
), we find

\displaystyle\langle A^{*}\Psi,\mathcal{T}_{3}A^{*}\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\langle\Psi,AA^{*}AA^{*}\Pi^{2}\Psi\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle\Psi,AA^{*}(1-AA^{*})\Pi^{2}\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip-10.0pt+2\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{AA^{*}\Psi(X)}\;|\nabla\alpha_{*}(r)|^{2}\;\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\Psi(X)

\displaystyle\hskip-10.0pt+\frac{1}{2}\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{AA^{*}\Psi(X)}\;|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|^{2}\alpha_{*}(r)^{2}\;\sin^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\Psi(X).

(5.67)

The corresponding result for
\langle A^{*}\Psi,\mathcal{T}_{4}A^{*}\Psi\rangle
is obtained by taking the complex conjugate of the right side of (
5.67
), which amounts to interchanging the roles of
AA^{*}\Psi
and
\Psi
in the last two lines.

We are now prepared to collect our results and to provide the final formula for (
5.48
). We need to collect the terms in (
5.60
), (
5.61
), (
5.67
) and the complex conjugate of (
5.67
). The terms involving
|\nabla\alpha_{*}|^{2}
read

\displaystyle 2\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{\Psi(X)}\;|\nabla\alpha_{*}(r)|^{2}\;\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\Psi(X)+2\langle AA^{*}\Psi,AA^{*}\Psi\rangle\|\nabla\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt-2\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{AA^{*}\Psi(X)}\;|\nabla\alpha_{*}(r)|^{2}\;\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\Psi(X)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt-2\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{\Psi(X)}\;|\nabla\alpha_{*}(r)|^{2}\;\cos^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;AA^{*}\Psi(X).

(5.68)

When we insert the factor
1=\cos^{2}(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X})+\sin^{2}(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X})
in the second term, we obtain the final result for the terms proportional to
|\nabla\alpha_{*}|^{2}
.

The terms proportional to
\alpha_{*}^{2}
with magnetic fields read

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{\Psi(X)}\;|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|^{2}\alpha_{*}(r)^{2}\;\sin^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\Psi(X)

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt+\frac{1}{2}\langle AA^{*}\Psi,AA^{*}\Psi\rangle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|^{2}\alpha_{*}(r)^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt-\frac{1}{2}\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{AA^{*}\Psi(X)}\;|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|^{2}\alpha_{*}(r)^{2}\;\sin^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;\Psi(X)

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt-\frac{1}{2}\fint_{Q_{B}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;\overline{\Psi(X)}\;|\mathbf{B}\wedge r|^{2}\alpha_{*}(r)^{2}\;\sin^{2}\bigl(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}\bigr)\;AA^{*}\Psi(X).

When we insert
1=\cos^{2}(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X})+\sin^{2}(\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X})
in the second term we can bring these terms in the claimed form.

Finally, we collect the terms proportional to
\alpha_{*}^{2}
but without magnetic field. Taking into account the first term in (
5.48
), we find

\displaystyle 2\langle\Psi,AA^{*}(1-AA^{*})\Psi\rangle+\langle\Psi,AA^{*}\Pi^{2}\Psi\rangle+\langle\Psi,AA^{*}(1-AA^{*})\Pi^{2}\Psi\rangle-\langle\Psi,AA^{*}AA^{*}\Pi^{2}\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt=2\langle\Psi,AA^{*}(1-AA^{*})(1+\Pi^{2})\Psi\rangle.

To obtain the result, we used that the terms coming from
\mathcal{T}_{3}
and
\mathcal{T}_{4}
are actually the same because
AA^{*}
and
1-AA^{*}
commute with
\Pi^{2}
